## Section 1: PostgreSQL Internals (25 questions)

### 1.1 MVCC and Concurrency

**Q1: "Explain MVCC. How does PostgreSQL handle concurrent reads and writes without locking?"**

**Answer (full):**

MVCC (Multi-Version Concurrency Control) is the mechanism that allows PostgreSQL to handle concurrent reads and writes without the reader blocking the writer or vice versa. This is fundamental to understanding why PostgreSQL performs well under concurrent workloads.

**The core problem MVCC solves:**

In a naive database, if Transaction A is reading a row while Transaction B wants to update it, one must wait for the other. This creates lock contention: reads block writes, writes block reads. At scale (like Docita with 25 clinics all booking simultaneously), this kills performance.

**How MVCC works:**

Every row in PostgreSQL has hidden system columns:
- `xmin`: the transaction ID that created this row version
- `xmax`: the transaction ID that deleted/updated this row (0 if not deleted)
- `ctid`: the physical location of the row version on disk

When you `UPDATE` a row, PostgreSQL does NOT modify the existing row. Instead:

```
Before UPDATE:
  Row version 1: { id: 1, name: "Alice", xmin: 100, xmax: 0 }

After UPDATE (name = "Bob"):
  Row version 1: { id: 1, name: "Alice", xmin: 100, xmax: 200 }  ← marked dead
  Row version 2: { id: 1, name: "Bob",   xmin: 200, xmax: 0 }    ← new version
```

When a transaction reads, it checks: "Which row versions are visible to me?" A row version is visible if:
1. `xmin` is committed AND before my snapshot
2. `xmax` is either not committed OR after my snapshot

This means:
- **Readers never block writers**: A reader sees the old version while the writer creates a new one
- **Writers never block readers**: A reader sees a consistent snapshot regardless of concurrent writes
- **Each transaction sees a consistent snapshot**: No dirty reads, no phantom reads (at repeatable read level)

**The trade-off — dead tuples and VACUUM:**

```
Transaction timeline:
  T1 starts (snapshot taken)
  T2 updates row (creates new version, marks old as dead)
  T3 updates same row (creates another version, marks T2's as dead)
  T1 reads (sees original version — both T2 and T3's versions are invisible)
  
  Now: 3 versions exist, only 1 is live, 2 are dead
  VACUUM must clean up the dead versions
```

If VACUUM falls behind (long-running transactions, overloaded server), dead tuples accumulate, table bloat grows, and performance degrades. This is why autovacuum tuning is critical for high-write tables like `appointments`.

**Why this matters for Docita:**

In Docita, multiple clinicians book appointments simultaneously. Without MVCC, every booking would lock the appointments table, creating a serialization bottleneck. With MVCC, each booking transaction sees a consistent snapshot and creates its own row versions. The `SELECT FOR UPDATE` in the booking flow is a deliberate pessimistic lock on a specific row (the patient), not a table-wide lock.

**What NOT to claim:** "PostgreSQL uses optimistic locking everywhere" (it uses MVCC + row-level locks when you explicitly use `FOR UPDATE`). "MVCC eliminates all locking" (it eliminates read-write locking, but write-write conflicts still use row-level locks).

---

**Q2: "What are transaction IDs? How does PostgreSQL track which rows are visible to which transaction?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
**Answer (full):**

Transaction IDs (XIDs) are PostgreSQL's mechanism for tracking which transactions created and modified which rows. Understanding XIDs is fundamental to understanding MVCC.

**How XIDs work:**

```
Each transaction gets a unique XID (monotonically increasing):
Transaction 1000: INSERT INTO appointments (id=1)  → xmin=1000
Transaction 1001: UPDATE appointments SET status='confirmed' WHERE id=1
  → old tuple: xmax=1001 (deleted by txn 1001)
  → new tuple: xmin=1001 (created by txn 1001)
Transaction 1002: DELETE FROM appointments WHERE id=1
  → tuple: xmax=1002 (deleted by txn 1002)
```

**Tuple visibility rules:**

```sql
-- For each tuple, PostgreSQL checks:
-- 1. Is xmin COMMITTED? (the creating transaction committed)
-- 2. Is xmin BEFORE my snapshot? (created before I started)
-- 3. Is xmax NOT COMMITTED? (not yet deleted)
-- 4. Is xmax AFTER my snapshot? (deleted after I started)

-- Visibility matrix:
-- xmin committed + before snapshot + xmax not committed → VISIBLE
-- xmin committed + before snapshot + xmax committed + after snapshot → INVISIBLE
-- xmin not committed + not my txn → INVISIBLE (uncommitted)
-- xmin my txn → VISIBLE (my own insert)
```

**Special XIDs:**

```
0 = Invalid XID (no transaction)
1 = Bootstrap XID (used during initdb)
2 = FrozenTransactionId (permanently visible, immune to wraparound)

These special XIDs are never used for regular transactions
```

**Impact on Docita:**

Every booking creates tuples with XIDs. When Dr. Smith loads their calendar, PostgreSQL checks each appointment tuple's xmin/xmax against the snapshot to determine visibility. This is why:
- Concurrent bookings don't conflict (each sees its own snapshot)
- Rollbacks work (xmax is cleared, tuple becomes visible again)
- Long-running reports don't block writes (readers don't hold locks)

**30-second answer:** Every transaction gets a unique XID. Every tuple stores xmin (who created it) and xmax (who deleted it). When reading, PostgreSQL compares these against the snapshot to determine visibility. This is MVCC — reads never block writes because each transaction sees its own consistent snapshot.

---

**Q3: "What is a tuple version? How does UPDATE create a new version rather than modifying in place?"**

**Answer (full):**

When you UPDATE a row, PostgreSQL does not modify the row in place. Instead, it follows a copy-on-write model that is fundamental to how MVCC works.

**The UPDATE lifecycle — step by step:**

```sql
-- Before UPDATE:
-- Tuple version 1 (visible to all transactions):
--   xmin=100, xmax=0, data="status=pending"

UPDATE appointments SET status = 'confirmed' WHERE id = 42;

-- After UPDATE:
-- Tuple version 1 (dead — xmax set):
--   xmin=100, xmax=105, data="status=pending"
-- Tuple version 2 (new — visible to current and future transactions):
--   xmin=105, xmax=0, data="status=confirmed"
```

**What happens internally:**

1. **Read the old tuple** — PostgreSQL locates the current visible version using the snapshot
2. **Mark xmax** — The old tuple's xmax is set to the current transaction ID (XID), marking it as "deleted by this transaction"
3. **Insert a new tuple** — A new copy of the row with updated data is inserted into the same page (or overflowed to a new page if the table is full)
4. **Index updates** — If any indexed columns changed, the old index entry is marked dead and a new one is created
5. **On COMMIT** — The xmax becomes permanent; other transactions can now see the new version
6. **On ROLLBACK** — The xmax is cleared; the old tuple becomes visible again, and the new tuple is discarded

**Why this matters for performance:**

```sql
-- This UPDATE creates 2 tuple versions and requires VACUUM to clean up:
UPDATE appointments SET note = 'updated' WHERE id = 42;
-- Result: old tuple becomes dead, new tuple created
-- If the table is heavily updated, dead tuples accumulate → table bloat

-- This is why autovacuum must run frequently on hot tables:
-- Dead tuples waste disk space and slow down sequential scans
```

**The heap page layout:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                    Heap Page (8KB)                    │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Page Header (24 bytes)                              │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Item Pointer Array (4 bytes per tuple)              │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Free Space                                          │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Tuple Version 2 (new — status=confirmed)            │
│ xmin=105, xmax=0                                    │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Tuple Version 1 (dead — status=pending)             │
│ xmin=100, xmax=105                                  │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**HOT UPDATEs (Heap Only Tuple):**

When an UPDATE doesn't change any indexed columns, PostgreSQL can perform a HOT update — inserting the new tuple version on the same page without updating any indexes. This is much faster because it avoids the index update overhead.

```sql
-- HOT update possible (no indexed columns changed):
UPDATE appointments SET note = 'new note' WHERE id = 42;
-- Only the heap is updated; index entries remain valid

-- Non-HOT update (indexed column changed):
UPDATE appointments SET status = 'confirmed' WHERE id = 42;
-- If "status" is indexed, the old index entry must be deleted
-- and a new one inserted → slower
```

**Impact on Docita's booking system:**

The appointments table is write-heavy (every booking creates a row). When appointments are updated (status changes, notes added), dead tuples accumulate. This is why:
1. Autovacuum is configured aggressively for the appointments table
2. We avoid updating columns unnecessarily (use separate columns for mutable vs immutable data)
3. We monitor table bloat with `pg_stat_user_tables` and `pgstattuple`

**What NOT to claim:** "UPDATEs modify rows in place" (they don't — they create new versions). "HOT updates always happen" (only when no indexed columns change). "VACUUM FULL is needed regularly" (it's almost never needed with proper autovacuum tuning).

**30-second answer:** PostgreSQL UPDATEs create a new tuple version instead of modifying in place. The old version gets marked dead (xmax set) and is cleaned up by VACUUM. This is the core of MVCC — reads never block writes. The trade-off is dead tuple accumulation that requires VACUUM to manage. HOT updates optimize this when no indexed columns change.

---

**Q4: "What is transaction ID wraparound? How does VACUUM prevent it?"**

**Answer (full):**

Transaction ID wraparound is one of the most dangerous failure modes in PostgreSQL. Understanding it is critical for any production system.

**The problem — 32-bit wraparound:**

```
Transaction IDs are 32-bit unsigned integers:
0 → 1 → 2 → ... → 4,294,967,295 → 0 (WRAPAROUND)

After ~4 billion transactions, XID 0 becomes "newer" than XID 4,294,967,294
This means PostgreSQL can no longer determine which tuples are visible
```

**How visibility comparison works:**

```sql
-- PostgreSQL compares XIDs using modular arithmetic:
-- If (current_xid - tuple_xid) is between 0 and 2^31:
--   tuple is OLD (not visible)
-- If (current_xid - tuple_xid) is between 2^31 and 2^32:
--   tuple is NEW (visible)

-- This works because PostgreSQL treats XIDs as a circular buffer:
-- [0 ... 2^31-1] is the "past"
-- [2^31 ... 2^32-1] is the "future"
```

**How VACUUM prevents wraparound:**

```sql
-- VACUUM FREEZE replaces old XIDs with FrozenTransactionId (2):
-- This "freezes" the tuple so it's always visible
-- Frozen tuples are immune to wraparound

-- The threshold:
-- autovacuum_freeze_max_age = 200,000,000 (default)
-- When a table's oldest XID is older than this, VACUUM MUST freeze
-- If autovacuum can't keep up, PostgreSQL forces a shutdown

-- You can see the current state:
SELECT
  datname,
  age(datfrozenxid) AS xid_age,
  2^31 - age(datfrozenxid) AS remaining
FROM pg_database;
```

**The wraparound shutdown:**

```
When the database reaches the wraparound threshold:
1. PostgreSQL logs: "WARNING: database must be vacuumed within X transactions"
2. At 1 million transactions remaining: database goes READ-ONLY
3. At 100,000 transactions remaining: emergency autovacuum runs
4. If emergency vacuum can't keep up: database SHUTS DOWN

This is catastrophic — your production database goes offline
```

**Prevention strategy:**

```sql
-- 1. Monitor XID age:
SELECT
  schemaname, relname,
  age(relfrozenxid) AS xid_age,
  pg_size_pretty(pg_total_relation_size(relid)) AS size
FROM pg_stat_user_tables
ORDER BY xid_age DESC
LIMIT 10;

-- 2. Configure autovacuum aggressively for high-transaction tables:
ALTER TABLE appointments SET (
  autovacuum_freeze_max_age = 100000000,  -- freeze earlier
  autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor = 0.05,   -- vacuum more frequently
  autovacuum_analyze_scale_factor = 0.02
);

-- 3. Set a safety margin:
-- autovacuum_freeze_max_age = 100,000,000 (not 200,000,000)
-- This gives autovacuum twice as much time to freeze
```

**Impact on Docita:**

With 25 clinics generating ~500 bookings/day, Docita creates ~180,000 transactions/year. At the default freeze_max_age of 200M, wraparound would take ~1,100 years. But if autovacuum is disabled or misconfigured (common in dev environments), wraparound can happen much faster.

**What NOT to claim:** "Wraparound is not a problem for small databases" (it affects ALL databases). "You can manually vacuum to prevent it" (autovacuum is essential; manual VACUUM is not enough). "Wraparound only happens in old PostgreSQL versions" (it's a fundamental limitation of 32-bit XIDs).

**30-second answer:** Transaction IDs are 32-bit, so they wrap after ~4 billion transactions. PostgreSQL freezes old tuples during VACUUM to prevent this. If autovacuum falls behind, the database goes read-only and eventually shuts down. Monitor XID age with `pg_stat_user_tables` and configure aggressive autovacuum for hot tables.

---

**Q5: "What is read committed vs repeatable read vs serializable in PostgreSQL? How does each affect Docita's booking queries?"**

**Answer (full):**

PostgreSQL offers three transaction isolation levels. Understanding the differences is critical for designing correct concurrent systems.

**Isolation levels explained:**

| Level | What you see | Phantom reads | Write skew | Performance |
|-------|-------------|---------------|------------|-------------|
| **Read Committed** | Each statement sees committed data at statement start | Possible | Possible | Best |
| **Repeatable Read** | Each statement sees committed data at transaction start | Prevented | Possible | Good |
| **Serializable** | Like RR but detects dangerous patterns | Prevented | Prevented | Worst (aborts) |

**Read Committed (PostgreSQL default):**

```sql
-- Transaction A                    -- Transaction B
BEGIN;                              BEGIN;
SELECT * FROM appointments          
WHERE practitioner_id = 'dr_1'      
  AND slot_start = '2026-08-25 10:00';
-- Returns: 0 rows (no booking yet) 

                                    INSERT INTO appointments 
                                    (practitioner_id, slot_start, ...)
                                    VALUES ('dr_1', '2026-08-25 10:00', ...);
                                    COMMIT;

-- Same query again (NEW STATEMENT)
SELECT * FROM appointments          
WHERE practitioner_id = 'dr_1'      
  AND slot_start = '2026-08-25 10:00';
-- Returns: 1 row! (phantom read)
-- Transaction A now sees Transaction B's insert
COMMIT;
```

In Read Committed, each statement gets a fresh snapshot. So the second SELECT sees the committed insert from Transaction B. This is fine for Docita's booking because the overlap check and insert happen in a SINGLE statement (within the transaction), not across multiple statements.

**Repeatable Read:**

```sql
-- Transaction A                    -- Transaction B
BEGIN;                              BEGIN;
SELECT * FROM appointments          
WHERE practitioner_id = 'dr_1'      
  AND slot_start = '2026-08-25 10:00';
-- Returns: 0 rows

                                    INSERT INTO appointments 
                                    (practitioner_id, slot_start, ...)
                                    VALUES ('dr_1', '2026-08-25 10:00', ...);
                                    COMMIT;

-- Same query again (SAME TRANSACTION)
SELECT * FROM appointments          
WHERE practitioner_id = 'dr_1'      
  AND slot_start = '2026-08-25 10:00';
-- Returns: 0 rows! (same snapshot as first query)
-- Transaction A does NOT see Transaction B's insert

-- But if A tries to INSERT the same slot:
INSERT INTO appointments (...) VALUES ('dr_1', '2026-08-25 10:00', ...);
-- ERROR: could not serialize access due to concurrent update
```

Repeatable Read uses a snapshot taken at the first statement. All subsequent statements see the same snapshot. If a conflict is detected (another transaction modified the same row), PostgreSQL aborts the transaction.

**Serializable:**

Serializable is like Repeatable Read but also detects **write skew** — a dangerous pattern where two transactions read overlapping data and make conflicting writes, even though they don't directly conflict on the same row.

```sql
-- Write skew example (Serializable prevents this):
-- Transaction A reads all available doctors for a time slot
-- Transaction B reads the same
-- Both book different doctors for the same patient
-- Result: patient has two appointments at the same time (impossible)
```

Serializable uses SSI (Serializable Snapshot Isolation) to track read-write dependencies. If it detects a dangerous pattern, it aborts one transaction.

**For Docita's booking:**

```typescript
// Docita uses Read Committed (default) because:
// 1. The overlap check + insert happens in ONE statement (SELECT FOR UPDATE + INSERT)
// 2. There's no read-then-write pattern that would cause write skew
// 3. Read Committed has the best performance

await prisma.$transaction(async (tx) => {
  // This is ONE atomic operation — no phantom read possible
  const overlapping = await tx.$queryRaw`
    SELECT id FROM appointments
    WHERE practitioner_id = ${dto.practitionerId}
      AND tstzrange(slot_start, slot_end, '[]') &&
          tstzrange(${dto.slotStart}::timestamptz, ${dto.slotEnd}::timestamptz, '[]')
      FOR UPDATE  -- Lock prevents concurrent inserts
  `;
  
  if (overlapping.length > 0) throw new ConflictException('Slot taken');
  
  await tx.appointment.create({ data: { ... } });
});
```

**When you WOULD use Serializable:**

If Docita had a more complex booking rule — like "a patient cannot have more than 3 appointments per week" — you'd need to read all the patient's appointments (read), check the count, and then insert (write). This read-then-write pattern is vulnerable to write skew under Read Committed. Serializable would detect and prevent it.

**What NOT to claim:** "Docita uses Serializable isolation" (it uses Read Committed, which is sufficient for the current booking logic). "Read Committed is unsafe" (it's safe when the conflict check is in a single statement).

---

**Q6: "What is a snapshot? How does PostgreSQL determine which tuple version a transaction sees?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
**Answer (full):**

A snapshot is PostgreSQL's mechanism for determining which rows a transaction can see. It's the foundation of MVCC — without snapshots, concurrent reads and writes would conflict.

**What a snapshot contains:**

```
Snapshot = {
  xmin: 1050        // All XIDs < 1050 are committed (visible)
  xmax: 1055        // All XIDs >= 1055 are in-progress (invisible)
  xip: [1051, 1053] // Specifically which XIDs are in-progress
}
```

**How tuple visibility is determined:**

```sql
-- For each tuple, PostgreSQL checks:
-- 1. Is tuple.xmin committed? (visible to this snapshot?)
-- 2. Is tuple.xmax committed? (deleted by this snapshot?)

-- Visibility rules:
-- xmin committed AND xmax NOT committed → visible (row exists)
-- xmin committed AND xmax committed → invisible (row was deleted)
-- xmin NOT committed → invisible (row created by in-progress txn)
-- xmin NOT committed AND xmin in my txn → visible (my own insert)
```

**When snapshots are taken:**

| Isolation Level | Snapshot timing | Implication |
|----------------|-----------------|-------------|
| Read Committed | Start of EACH statement | Each statement sees latest committed data |
| Repeatable Read | Start of transaction | All statements see same snapshot |
| Serializable | Start of transaction + SSI tracking | Like RR + detects dangerous patterns |

**Impact on Docita:**

When a practitioner loads their calendar, PostgreSQL takes a snapshot. Even if another transaction inserts a booking during the query, the snapshot ensures the practitioner sees a consistent view. This prevents the "phantom booking" problem — a booking appearing mid-query.

**30-second answer:** A snapshot is a frozen view of the database at a specific point in time. It records which XIDs are committed (visible) and which are in-progress (invisible). Each query uses the snapshot to determine tuple visibility, allowing concurrent reads without locks.

---

**Q7: "How does PostgreSQL handle a long-running transaction that blocks VACUUM?"**

**Answer (full):**

This is one of the most common PostgreSQL performance problems in production. A long-running transaction holds a snapshot that prevents VACUUM from cleaning up dead tuples.

**The problem:**

```
Timeline:
  T0: VACUUM runs, sees dead tuples, wants to reclaim space
  T1: Long-running transaction started at T0
  T2: Other transactions update rows (creating dead tuples)
  T3: VACUUM cannot reclaim dead tuples because the long transaction
      might still need to see them (MVCC snapshot)
  
  Result: Dead tuples accumulate, table grows, queries slow down
  
  After 24 hours:
  - Table should be 10GB (actual data)
  - Table is 50GB (40GB of dead tuples waiting for VACUUM)
  - Queries scan 50GB instead of 10GB
  - Performance degrades 5x
```

**Why VACUUM can't clean up:**

```
MVCC rule: VACUUM cannot remove a dead tuple if ANY active transaction
might still need to see it.

Long transaction at T0 holds a snapshot:
  - Sees all data committed before T0
  - Dead tuples created after T0 are invisible to this transaction
  - But dead tuples created BEFORE T0 are visible
  - VACUUM cannot remove tuples that might be visible to anyone

VACUUM waits for the long transaction to finish
If the transaction runs for hours, VACUUM waits for hours
```

**Solutions:**

**1. Kill long queries (statement_timeout)**

```sql
-- Kill queries that run longer than 5 minutes
ALTER SYSTEM SET statement_timeout = '5min';
SELECT pg_reload_conf();

-- Or per-role
ALTER ROLE app_user SET statement_timeout = '5min';
```

**2. Kill idle-in-transaction sessions**

```sql
-- Kill sessions idle in transaction for more than 10 minutes
ALTER SYSTEM SET idle_in_transaction_session_timeout = '10min';
SELECT pg_reload_conf();

-- This is the most common fix — developers start a transaction
-- and forget to commit/rollback
```

**3. Monitor long transactions**

```sql
-- Find long-running transactions
SELECT 
  pid,
  now() - xact_start AS duration,
  query,
  state,
  wait_event_type,
  wait_event
FROM pg_stat_activity
WHERE state != 'idle'
  AND xact_start < now() - interval '5 minutes'
ORDER BY duration DESC;

-- If you see a transaction running for 30 minutes:
-- 1. Check what it's doing (query column)
-- 2. If it's a bug: SELECT pg_terminate_backend(pid);
-- 3. If it's intentional: optimize the query
```

**4. Use NOWAIT for contention**

```typescript
// ❌ Bad: waits indefinitely for a lock
const patient = await tx.$queryRaw`
  SELECT * FROM patients WHERE id = ${patientId} FOR UPDATE
`;
// If another transaction holds the lock, this waits forever

// ✅ Good: fails immediately if lock is held
const patient = await tx.$queryRaw`
  SELECT * FROM patients WHERE id = ${patientId} FOR UPDATE NOWAIT
`;
// If lock is held: throws error immediately
// Caller can retry or return 409 Conflict
```

**5. Tune autovacuum for high-write tables**

```sql
-- For the appointments table (high write volume):
ALTER TABLE appointments SET (
  autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor = 0.01,  -- Run VACUUM after 1% of rows change
  autovacuum_analyze_scale_factor = 0.005, -- Run ANALYZE after 0.5% change
  autovacuum_vacuum_cost_limit = 1000      -- More aggressive VACUUM
);

-- Default is 0.20 (20%) — too high for high-write tables
-- At 20%, VACUUM only runs after 200,000 rows change (too late)
```

**For Docita:**

| Setting | Value | Why |
|---------|-------|-----|
| **statement_timeout** | 5 minutes | Kill runaway queries |
| **idle_in_transaction_session_timeout** | 10 minutes | Kill forgotten transactions |
| **autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor** (appointments) | 0.01 | High-write table needs frequent VACUUM |
| **Monitor** | pg_stat_activity query | Check for long transactions daily |

**What NOT to claim:** "VACUUM always keeps up" (it doesn't if long transactions block it). "Long transactions are always bugs" (some are intentional, like reports — but they should be optimized).

---

**Q8: "What is predicate locking in serializable isolation? How does it prevent phantom reads?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
**Answer (full):**

Serializable isolation in PostgreSQL uses SSI (Serializable Snapshot Isolation) — an advanced concurrency control mechanism that prevents all three phenomena: dirty reads, non-repeatable reads, and phantom reads.

**How SSI works:**

```
SSI tracks two types of dependencies between transactions:

1. Read-Write (rw) dependencies:
   Transaction A reads a row → Transaction B modifies the same row
   This creates a "rw-dependency" from A to B

2. Write-Write (ww) dependencies:
   Transaction A modifies a row → Transaction B modifies the same row
   This is handled by regular row locks

SSI detects "dangerous structures" — cycles in the dependency graph:
   A reads → B writes → A reads (different row) → B writes (different row)
   This cycle means the serialization order is ambiguous
```

**The dangerous pattern:**

```sql
-- Write skew (SSI prevents this):
-- Rule: "At most 2 doctors on night shift"

-- Transaction A:
SELECT count(*) FROM staff WHERE shift = 'night';
-- Returns 1 (currently on night shift)
INSERT INTO staff (name, shift) VALUES ('Dr. A', 'night');

-- Transaction B (concurrent):
SELECT count(*) FROM staff WHERE shift = 'night';
-- Returns 1 (same snapshot as A)
INSERT INTO staff (name, shift) VALUES ('Dr. B', 'night');

-- Result WITHOUT SSI: 3 doctors on night shift (violates rule)
-- Result WITH SSI: Transaction B is ABORTED
```

**SSI implementation details:**

```sql
-- PostgreSQL uses predicate locks (not actual locks — they're tracked in memory)
-- When a transaction reads data, SSI records:
-- - Which rows were read
-- - Which predicate the query matched

-- When another transaction tries to write:
-- SSI checks: "Would this write change the result of a previous read?"
-- If yes → dangerous pattern detected → ABORT one transaction

-- The abort happens at COMMIT time (not during the query)
-- This means SSI has low overhead during query execution
```

**Performance implications:**

| Metric | Serializable | Repeatable Read |
|--------|-------------|-----------------|
| Overhead | 10-30% slower | Baseline |
| Abort rate | 1-5% | 0-1% |
| Write skew | Prevented | Possible |
| Phantom reads | Prevented | Possible |
| Use case | Financial, booking rules | General purpose |

**When to use Serializable:**

```sql
-- Use Serializable when:
-- 1. Business rules span multiple rows (write skew)
-- 2. "At most N" or "at least M" constraints
-- 3. Consistent snapshots across multiple queries matter

-- Don't use Serializable when:
-- 1. Single-row operations (Read Committed is fine)
-- 2. High-throughput systems (abort rate hurts performance)
-- 3. You can use SELECT FOR UPDATE to avoid the problem
```

**30-second answer:** SSI tracks read-write dependencies between transactions. If it detects a dangerous pattern (where two transactions read overlapping data and make conflicting writes), it aborts one transaction. This prevents write skew and phantom reads. Overhead is 10-30% slower than Repeatable Read, with 1-5% abort rate. Use it when business rules span multiple rows.

---

### 1.2 WAL and Recovery

**Q9: "What is the WAL? How does it enable crash recovery?"**

**Answer (full):**

The Write-Ahead Log (WAL) is PostgreSQL's mechanism for durability and crash recovery. Every change is written to the WAL before being applied to the actual data files.

**Why WAL exists:**

```
Without WAL:
  1. Modify data page in memory (shared_buffers)
  2. Flush to disk
  Problem: If crash happens between step 1 and 2:
    - Page is modified in memory but not on disk
    - Data is corrupted
    - No way to recover

With WAL:
  1. Write change to WAL (sequential write — fast)
  2. Flush WAL to disk (fsync)
  3. Modify data page in memory
  4. Data page flushed to disk later (checkpoint)
  
  If crash happens:
    - WAL has the complete history of changes
    - Replay WAL to restore data pages
    - Committed transactions are restored
    - Uncommitted transactions are rolled back
```

**How WAL enables crash recovery:**

```
Normal operation:
  T1: BEGIN; UPDATE patients SET name = 'Alice'; COMMIT;
  T2: BEGIN; INSERT INTO appointments (...); COMMIT;
  T3: BEGIN; DELETE FROM prescriptions WHERE id = 1; (not committed yet)
  
  Crash!
  
Recovery:
  1. Find last checkpoint in control file
  2. Replay WAL from checkpoint forward:
     - T1: Replay UPDATE → patients.name = 'Alice' ✅
     - T2: Replay INSERT → appointment created ✅
     - T3: DELETE was not committed → roll back ❌
  3. Database is consistent as of the last committed transaction
```

**WAL segments and checkpoints:**

```
WAL files:
  pg_wal/000000010000000000000001  (16MB)
  pg_wal/000000010000000000000002  (16MB)
  pg_wal/000000010000000000000003  (16MB)
  ...

Checkpoint:
  - Flushes dirty pages from memory to disk
  - Updates control file with checkpoint location
  - WAL segments before checkpoint can be archived/removed
  - Triggered by: WAL volume, timeout, or manual command

Timeline:
  ──[Checkpoint]──────[WAL growth]──────[Checkpoint]──────
       │                    │                    │
  Data flushed to disk    New changes logged   Data flushed again
```

**WAL and replication:**

```
Primary:
  1. Write change to WAL
  2. Send WAL record to replica (streaming replication)
  3. Replica applies WAL record
  4. Replica is now in sync

If primary crashes:
  1. Replica has all WAL records up to the crash
  2. Replica promotes to primary
  3. No data loss (if async replication caught up)
```

**The trade-off:**

| Aspect | Without WAL | With WAL |
|--------|------------|----------|
| **Write latency** | Lower (direct write) | Higher (WAL + data write) |
| **Crash recovery** | ❌ Data corruption | ✅ Full recovery |
| **Point-in-time recovery** | ❌ Not possible | ✅ Restore to any point |
| **Replication** | ❌ Not possible | ✅ Streaming replication |
| **Durability** | ❌ Data loss on crash | ✅ Committed data preserved |

**What NOT to claim:** "WAL adds no overhead" (it adds 5-15% write latency). "WAL is the same as a transaction log" (WAL is PostgreSQL's implementation of a transaction log).

---

**Q10: "How does WAL archiving work? How does it enable point-in-time recovery?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
**Answer (full):**

WAL archiving is the foundation of point-in-time recovery (PITR) — the ability to restore your database to any specific moment in the past. This is essential for compliance (HIPAA, GDPR) and disaster recovery.

**How WAL archiving works:**

```
┌─────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     ┌─────────────┐
│  PostgreSQL  │────▶│  WAL Writer  │────▶│  Archive     │
│  generates   │     │  writes to   │     │  Storage     │
│  WAL segments│     │  pg_wal/     │     │  (S3/NFS)   │
└─────────────┘     └──────────────┘     └─────────────┘
                           │
                    ┌──────┴──────┐
                    │ archive_cmd │
                    │ (copies WAL │
                    │  to storage)│
                    └─────────────┘
```

**Configuration:**

```ini
# postgresql.conf
wal_level = replica           # Required for archiving
archive_mode = on             # Enable archiving
archive_command = 'aws s3 s3://docita-wal-archive/%f copy %p'
# %f = WAL filename, %p = WAL file path
archive_timeout = 300         # Force archive every 5 minutes (even if not full)
```

**The recovery process:**

```bash
# 1. Take a base backup (full database snapshot)
pg_basebackup -D /backup/base -Ft -Xs -P

# 2. If disaster strikes, restore:
tar xf /backup/base/base.tar -C /var/lib/postgresql/data/
cat > /var/lib/postgresql/data/postgresql.auto.conf << 'EOF'
restore_command = 'aws s3 s3://docita-wal-archive/%f %p'
recovery_target_time = '2026-08-20 14:30:00+00'
recovery_target_action = 'promote'
EOF
touch /var/lib/postgresql/data/recovery.signal
# Start PostgreSQL — it replays WAL to the target time
```

**Recovery targets:**

| Target | Use Case |
|--------|----------|
| `recovery_target_time` | Recover to specific moment (e.g., before a bad migration) |
| `recovery_target_xid` | Recover to specific transaction |
| `recovery_target_lsn` | Recover to specific WAL position |
| `recovery_target_name` | Recover to named restore point |

**Impact on Docita:**

WAL archiving enables HIPAA-compliant recovery ("restore patient data to any point in last 7 years"), migration safety ("restore to before the migration if it fails"), and forensic analysis ("what did the database look like at 2:00 AM?"). RPO = archive_timeout (5 minutes).

**30-second answer:** WAL archiving copies completed WAL segments to external storage. Combined with a base backup, it enables point-in-time recovery — restoring the database to any specific moment. Configure `archive_command`, set `archive_timeout` for your RPO, and use `pg_basebackup` for base backups.

---

**Q11: "What is synchronous vs asynchronous replication? What's the trade-off?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
**Answer (full):**

The choice between synchronous and asynchronous replication is one of the most important trade-offs in database architecture. It directly impacts your RPO (Recovery Point Objective) and write latency.

**Synchronous replication:**

```
Flow:
1. Primary receives COMMIT
2. Primary sends WAL to standby
3. Primary WAITS for standby to confirm receipt
4. Primary returns success to client
5. RPO = 0 (zero data loss guaranteed)
```

**Asynchronous replication:**

```
Flow:
1. Primary receives COMMIT
2. Primary writes WAL to local disk
3. Primary returns success to client immediately
4. WAL is sent to standby in background
5. RPO > 0 (potential data loss on crash)
```

**Performance comparison:**

| Metric | Sync | Async |
|--------|------|-------|
| Write latency | +1-10ms (network RTT) | Same as local |
| Throughput | Lower (waits for standby) | Higher |
| Data loss risk | None (RPO = 0) | Up to last WAL segment |
| Standby lag | Always 0 | Seconds to minutes |

**PostgreSQL configuration:**

```ini
# Synchronous replication
synchronous_standby_names = 'standby1'

# Asynchronous replication (default)
synchronous_standby_names = ''

# Hybrid: synchronous_commit = remote_write (not remote_apply)
# Waits for standby to write WAL to OS cache (not apply it)
# Good balance between safety and performance
```

**30-second answer:** Synchronous replication waits for the standby to confirm WAL receipt — zero data loss but higher write latency. Asynchronous replication doesn't wait — faster but risks data loss. Use sync for critical data (bookings), async for read replicas. PostgreSQL supports `synchronous_commit` levels to fine-tune this trade-off.

---

**Q12: "How does WAL relate to streaming replication? How do you set up a read replica?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
**Answer (full):**

Streaming replication is PostgreSQL's method for replicating data from a primary to one or more replicas in real-time via the WAL (Write-Ahead Log).

**How it works:**

```
Primary → WAL Sender process → Network → WAL Receiver process → Standby

1. Primary generates WAL records for every write operation
2. WAL Sender process reads WAL and streams it over the network
3. WAL Receiver on standby receives WAL records
4. Standby applies WAL to its local copy of the database
5. Standby can serve read queries (read replica)
```

**Setup:**

```bash
# Primary (postgresql.conf):
wal_level = replica
max_wal_senders = 5
wal_keep_size = 1GB

# Primary (pg_hba.conf):
# Allow standby to connect
host replication replicator 10.0.1.0/24 md5

# Standby:
pg_basebackup -h primary_host -U replicator -D /var/lib/postgresql/data/ -Fp -Xs -P
# Create standby.signal:
touch /var/lib/postgresql/data/standby.signal
# Configure primary_conninfo in postgresql.auto.conf
```

**Replication lag monitoring:**

```sql
-- On primary:
SELECT
  client_addr,
  state,
  sent_lsn,
  write_lsn,
  flush_lsn,
  replay_lsn,
  pg_wal_lsn_diff(sent_lsn, replay_lsn) AS lag_bytes,
  pg_size_pretty(pg_wal_lsn_diff(sent_lsn, replay_lsn)) AS lag_pretty
FROM pg_stat_replication;

-- On standby:
SELECT * FROM pg_stat_wal_receiver;
```

**Impact on Docita:**

Docita uses streaming replication to:
1. Serve all dashboard queries from the read replica (offload primary)
2. Enable automatic failover if primary crashes (high availability)
3. Provide zero-downtime backups (backup from replica, not primary)

**30-second answer:** Streaming replication sends WAL records from primary to standby in real-time. The standby applies WAL to stay current, serving read queries and providing failover. Monitor replication lag with `pg_stat_replication`. Configure `max_wal_senders` and `wal_keep_size` on the primary.

---

**Q13: "What is a WAL segment? How does checkpointing work?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
**Answer (full):**

WAL segments are the physical storage units for the Write-Ahead Log. Understanding them is critical for performance tuning and disaster recovery.

**WAL segment structure:**

```
WAL directory: pg_wal/
├── 000000010000000000000001  (Segment 1: 16MB)
├── 000000010000000000000002  (Segment 2: 16MB)
├── 000000010000000000000003  (Segment 3: 16MB)
└── ...

Each segment: 16MB (default, configurable with --with-wal-segsize)
Each segment divided into pages: 8KB each
```

**Checkpointing:**

```
What is a checkpoint?
- PostgreSQL periodically writes all dirty pages to disk
- This is called a checkpoint
- After checkpoint, old WAL segments can be recycled/deleted

Why checkpointing matters:
- Without checkpoints, WAL segments accumulate forever
- Recovery time = time to replay WAL since last checkpoint
- More frequent checkpoints = faster recovery, but more I/O

Checkpoint triggers:
1. max_wal_size reached (default: 1GB)
2. checkpoint_timeout reached (default: 5min)
3. Manual CHECKPOINT command
```

**Checkpoint tuning:**

```sql
-- postgresql.conf:
checkpoint_timeout = '5min'      -- How often to checkpoint
max_wal_size = '1GB'             -- Max WAL before forced checkpoint
min_wal_size = '80MB'            -- Min WAL to keep
checkpoint_completion_target = 0.9  -- Spread I/O over 90% of interval

-- Too frequent checkpointing: High I/O, slower writes
-- Too infrequent: Long recovery time, WAL accumulation

-- Monitor checkpoint frequency:
SELECT * FROM pg_stat_bgwriter;
-- checkpoints_timed: 100 (scheduled)
-- checkpoints_req: 5 (forced by WAL size)
-- If checkpoints_req >> checkpoints_timed, increase max_wal_size
```

**Impact on Docita:**

With checkpoint_timeout=5min and max_wal_size=1GB:
- Recovery time: <5 minutes (replay at most 1GB of WAL)
- Write performance: Good (checkpoints spread over time)
- Disk usage: Controlled (old WAL recycled after checkpoint)

**30-second answer:** WAL segments are 16MB files containing WAL records. Checkpoints periodically flush dirty pages to disk, allowing old WAL to be recycled. Tune with `checkpoint_timeout` (how often) and `max_wal_size` (trigger threshold). More frequent checkpoints = faster recovery but higher I/O.

---

### 1.3 VACUUM and Bloat

**Q14: "What is VACUUM? How does it reclaim dead tuple space?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
**Answer (full):**

VACUUM is PostgreSQL's garbage collector. Without it, every UPDATE and DELETE would permanently bloat your tables.

**What VACUUM does:**

```
Before VACUUM:
┌──────────────────────────────────┐
│ Page (8KB)                       │
├──────────────────────────────────┤
│ Tuple A (alive)                  │
│ Tuple B (dead — xmax set)        │  ← Wasted space
│ Tuple C (dead — xmax set)        │  ← Wasted space
│ Tuple D (alive)                  │
│ Free Space: 2KB                  │
└──────────────────────────────────┘

After VACUUM:
┌──────────────────────────────────┐
│ Page (8KB)                       │
├──────────────────────────────────┤
│ Tuple A (alive)                  │
│ Tuple D (alive)                  │
│ Free Space: 4KB                  │  ← Space reclaimed for reuse
└──────────────────────────────────┘
```

**VACUUM modes:**

| Mode | Lock | Space returned to OS |
|------|------|---------------------|
| VACUUM | ShareUpdateExclusiveLock (allows reads) | No (reusable within table) |
| VACUUM FULL | AccessExclusiveLock (blocks ALL) | Yes |
| VACUUM ANALYZE | ShareUpdateExclusiveLock | No |
| autovacuum | ShareUpdateExclusiveLock | No |

**Monitoring VACUUM:**

```sql
SELECT
  schemaname, relname,
  last_vacuum, last_autovacuum,
  last_analyze, last_autoanalyze,
  n_dead_tup, n_live_tup,
  round(n_dead_tup::numeric / nullif(n_live_tup, 0) * 100, 1) AS dead_pct
FROM pg_stat_user_tables
ORDER BY n_dead_tup DESC LIMIT 10;
```

**30-second answer:** VACUUM reclaims space from dead tuples (UPDATEs/DELETEs). It marks space as reusable but doesn't return it to the OS. VACUUM FULL compacts but takes an exclusive lock. Autovacuum runs automatically. Monitor with `pg_stat_user_tables` and configure aggressively for hot tables.

---

**Q15: "What is VACUUM FULL? When would you use it vs regular VACUUM?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
**Answer (full):**

VACUUM FULL is a nuclear option that most DBAs avoid in production.

**VACUUM vs VACUUM FULL:**

| Aspect | VACUUM | VACUUM FULL |
|--------|--------|-------------|
| Lock | ShareUpdateExclusiveLock (allows reads) | AccessExclusiveLock (blocks EVERYTHING) |
| Space reuse | Marks space for internal reuse | Returns space to OS |
| Table rewrite | No | Yes |
| Use case | Routine maintenance | After bulk deletes, severe bloat |

**When VACUUM FULL is justified:**

```sql
-- 1. After bulk delete:
DELETE FROM audit_logs WHERE created_at < '2024-01-01';
VACUUM FULL audit_logs;  -- Actually shrinks the table

-- 2. Before pg_dump (backup):
VACUUM FULL table_name;  -- Makes dump smaller and faster

-- 3. Severe bloat (>50% dead tuples):
SELECT * FROM pgstattuple('appointments');
```

**Safer alternative — pg_repack:**

```bash
# Rebuilds table WITHOUT exclusive lock
pg_repack -d docita -t appointments
# Creates new table, copies data, swaps indexes, drops old table
```

**30-second answer:** VACUUM FULL rewrites the entire table, returning space to the OS, but takes an exclusive lock. Almost never needed in production — use pg_repack instead. Only consider after bulk deletes or severe bloat.

---

**Q16: "What is autovacuum? How does it decide when to run?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
**Answer (full):**

Autovacuum is PostgreSQL's automatic garbage collector. Without it, your database would bloat and eventually run out of disk space.

**How it decides when to run:**

```sql
-- Trigger formula:
-- dead_tuples > autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor × total_tuples + autovacuum_vacuum_threshold

-- Default: scale_factor = 0.20 (20%), threshold = 50
-- For 100,000 rows: vacuum when dead_tuples > 20,050
```

**Tuning per table:**

```sql
-- Hot table (appointments):
ALTER TABLE appointments SET (
  autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor = 0.05,  -- 5% dead → vacuum
  autovacuum_vacuum_threshold = 50,
  autovacuum_analyze_scale_factor = 0.02,
  autovacuum_vacuum_cost_limit = 400      -- More aggressive
);

-- Cold table (audit_logs):
ALTER TABLE audit_logs SET (
  autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor = 0.50,
  autovacuum_enabled = false              -- Append-only, no updates
);
```

**Monitoring:**

```sql
SELECT * FROM pg_stat_progress_vacuum;
SHOW autovacuum_max_workers;      -- Default: 3
SHOW autovacuum_naptime;          -- Default: 1min
```

**30-second answer:** Autovacuum is PostgreSQL's automatic garbage collector. Configure per-table: aggressive for hot tables (5% scale factor), relaxed for cold tables (50%). Monitor with `pg_stat_progress_vacuum`. NEVER disable it in production.

---

**Q17: "What is table bloat? How do you detect and fix it?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
**Answer (full):**

Table bloat is when a table consumes far more disk space than its actual data requires, due to dead tuples from UPDATEs and DELETEs.

**What causes bloat:**

```
After 10,000 UPDATEs:
┌──────────────────────────────────┐
│ Table on disk: 1GB               │
│ Actual data:   100MB             │
│ Dead tuples:   900MB (90% waste) │
│ Bloat ratio:   10x              │
└──────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Detecting bloat:**

```sql
SELECT
  schemaname, relname,
  pg_size_pretty(pg_total_relation_size(relid)) AS total_size,
  n_live_tup, n_dead_tup,
  round(n_dead_tup::numeric / nullif(n_live_tup + n_dead_tup, 0) * 100, 1) AS dead_pct
FROM pg_stat_user_tables
WHERE n_dead_tup > 1000
ORDER BY n_dead_tup DESC;
```

**Fixing bloat:**

```sql
-- Safe (no lock): pg_repack
pg_repack -d docita -t appointments

-- Dangerous (exclusive lock): VACUUM FULL
VACUUM FULL appointments;
```

**Preventing bloat:** Aggressive autovacuum + monitor dead tuple ratio + periodic pg_repack.

**30-second answer:** Table bloat is wasted disk space from dead tuples. Detect with `pg_stat_user_tables` (n_dead_tup). Fix with pg_repack (no lock) or VACUUM FULL (exclusive lock). Prevent with aggressive autovacuum.

---

**Q18: "What is index bloat? How does it affect query performance?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
**Answer (full):**

Index bloat is when B-tree indexes consume more disk space than necessary due to page splits and dead entries.

**Detecting index bloat:**

```sql
CREATE EXTENSION pgstatindex;
SELECT * FROM pgstatindex('idx_appointments_practitioner');
-- leaf_fragmentation > 20% → bloat exists
```

**Fixing:**

```sql
-- Safe: REINDEX CONCURRENTLY (PostgreSQL 12+)
REINDEX INDEX CONCURRENTLY idx_appointments_practitioner;

-- Safe: pg_repack
pg_repack -d docita -t appointments --index idx_appointments_practitioner
```

**Preventing:** Lower fillfactor (70-80) for hot indexes.

```sql
CREATE INDEX idx_appointments_practitioner
ON appointments (practitioner_id, slot_start)
WITH (fillfactor = 70);  -- 30% free space for updates
```

**30-second answer:** Index bloat is wasted space in B-tree indexes. Detect with `pgstatindex` (leaf_fragmentation > 20%). Fix with REINDEX CONCURRENTLY or pg_repack. Prevent with fillfactor=70-80 for hot indexes.

---

### 1.4 Connection Pooling

**Q19: "Why is PgBouncer necessary? What happens when you exhaust PostgreSQL connections?"**

**Answer (full):**

This is one of the most practical questions for production systems. Understanding connection pooling is essential for any scaled application.

**The problem: each PostgreSQL connection is expensive.**

Unlike most databases, PostgreSQL spawns a **separate OS process** for every connection (not a thread). Each process:
- Allocates ~5-10MB of memory (work_mem, maintenance_work_mem, stack)
- Consumes a file descriptor
- Requires context switching when the OS scheduler runs it
- Competes for CPU time with all other connections

**The math:**

```
1 connection  = ~10MB memory
100 connections = ~1GB memory
500 connections = ~5GB memory (just for connections, not queries!)

Plus: each query requires a slot in the OS process scheduler
At 500 connections, context switching alone degrades throughput by 30-50%
```

**What happens when you exhaust connections:**

```
1. New connection request arrives
2. PostgreSQL checks max_connections (default: 100)
3. If all slots are taken: connection is REJECTED
4. Application gets "FATAL: too many connections" error
5. Connection pooler retries or fails the request
6. Under load: cascading failures as retries consume more connections
```

**How PgBouncer solves this:**

```
                    PgBouncer (transaction mode)
Application         ┌─────────────┐         PostgreSQL
┌─────────┐        │ Pool: 20    │        ┌─────────┐
│ App #1  │───┐    │ connections │    ┌───│ Backend │
│ App #2  │───┤    │             │    │   │ Process │
│ App #3  │───┤    │ 100 app conns──→ 20 DB conns  │
│ ...     │───┤    │             │    │   │         │
│ App #100│───┘    │             │    └───│         │
└─────────┘        └─────────────┘        └─────────┘

100 app connections → multiplexed → 20 PostgreSQL connections
```

PgBouncer in **transaction mode**:
1. App opens a connection to PgBouncer
2. PgBouncer assigns a PostgreSQL connection from the pool
3. When the transaction completes, PgBouncer returns the PostgreSQL connection to the pool
4. The next transaction gets a (possibly different) PostgreSQL connection

**Why transaction mode for Docita:**

```typescript
// Prisma uses short-lived transactions:
await prisma.$transaction(async (tx) => {
  // This transaction lasts ~10-50ms
  // PgBouncer can reuse the connection immediately after
  const patient = await tx.patient.findUnique({ ... });
  const appointment = await tx.appointment.create({ ... });
});

// With transaction mode:
// Request 1 gets DB connection #1
// Request 2 gets DB connection #2 (different connection, same pool)
// Request 1's connection is returned to pool after commit
```

**Connection pool sizing formula:**

```
connections = (CPU cores × 2) + effective_spindle_count

For SSDs: connections ≈ (cores × 2) + 1
For a 4-core server: ~9 connections per PgBouncer instance
```

The counterintuitive insight: **more connections ≠ better performance.** Beyond a threshold (typically 2-3x CPU cores), context switching hurts more than parallelism helps.

**Monitoring PgBouncer:**

```sql
-- PgBouncer admin console
SHOW POOLS;    -- Active connections per pool
SHOW STATS;    -- Query throughput, avg latency
SHOW CLIENTS;  -- Connected clients
SHOW SERVERS;  -- Active server connections

-- Key metrics:
-- cl_active = clients currently running a query
-- cl_waiting = clients waiting for a server connection (BAD if > 0)
-- sv_active = servers currently running a query
-- sv_idle = servers ready for use
```

**What NOT to claim:** "PgBouncer is a load balancer" (it's a connection pooler, not a load balancer). "You need one PgBouncer per app instance" (one PgBouncer per database instance is typical).

---

**Q20: "What is connection pooling mode — session vs transaction vs statement? Which does Docita use?"**

**Answer (full):**

PgBouncer supports three pooling modes that determine when a client gets and releases a database connection.

**The three modes:**

```
Session mode:
  Client connects → gets DB connection → holds it until disconnect
  Pros: Supports all PostgreSQL features (PREPARE, LISTEN/NOTIFY, etc.)
  Cons: Connection held even when idle (wastes DB connections)
  
Transaction mode:
  Client connects → gets DB connection for transaction → returns it
  Pros: Connections reused across requests (efficient)
  Cons: Some features don't work (prepared statements, session variables)
  
Statement mode:
  Client connects → gets DB connection per statement → returns it
  Pros: Maximum connection reuse
  Cons: Breaks multi-statement transactions (not usable with Prisma)
```

**Visual comparison:**

```
Session mode:
  Client A: [──────DB connection held──────]
  Client B:           [──────DB connection held──────]
  DB connections: 2 (even during idle time)

Transaction mode:
  Client A: [txn1][txn2][txn3]
  Client B:    [txn1][txn2]
  DB connections: 1-2 (reused between transactions)

Statement mode:
  Client A: [s1][s2][s3][s4]
  Client B: [s1][s2]
  DB connections: 1 (maximum reuse, but breaks transactions)
```

**Why Docita uses transaction mode:**

```typescript
// Prisma uses short-lived transactions:
await prisma.$transaction(async (tx) => {
  const patient = await tx.patient.findUnique({ ... });
  const appointment = await tx.appointment.create({ ... });
});
// Transaction lasts ~10-50ms
// PgBouncer returns the DB connection immediately after commit

// With transaction mode:
// Request 1 gets DB connection #1 → releases after commit
// Request 2 gets DB connection #2 → releases after commit
// Connection #1 is available for Request 3
```

**What doesn't work in transaction mode:**

```typescript
// ❌ Prepared statements (PgBouncer in transaction mode can't handle)
await prisma.$executeRaw`PREPARE my_plan AS SELECT * FROM patients WHERE id = $1`;
// PgBouncer may route the PREPARE to connection #1
// and the EXECUTE to connection #2 → ERROR

// ✅ Use parameterized queries instead
await prisma.patient.findUnique({ where: { id: patientId } });
// Prisma handles parameterization safely
```

**What NOT to claim:** "Session mode is always best" (it wastes connections). "Statement mode works with Prisma" (it breaks transactions). "Transaction mode has no limitations" (prepared statements don't work).

---

**Q21: "How do you tune connection pool size? What's the formula?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
**Answer (full):**

Connection pool sizing is critical — too few connections cause queuing, too many cause resource exhaustion.

**The formula:**

```
connections = (CPU cores × 2) + effective_spindle_count

For SSDs (effective_spindle_count = 1):
connections = (4 × 2) + 1 = 9

For a dedicated PostgreSQL server with 8 cores, SSD:
connections = (8 × 2) + 1 = 17

This is the MAXIMUM for a single PostgreSQL instance.
```

**Why not more connections?**

```
Each PostgreSQL connection:
- Uses ~10MB RAM (stack + work_mem + temp buffers)
- Requires its own process (fork model)
- Competes for CPU time slices
- Lock contention increases quadratically with connections

At 100 connections on a 4-core server:
- Context switching overhead > actual query execution
- Lock contention becomes the bottleneck
- Throughput actually DECREASES
```

**PgBouncer configuration:**

```ini
# pgbouncer.ini
[databases]
docita = host=localhost port=5432 dbname=docita

[pgbouncer]
pool_mode = transaction      # Best for NestJS (short transactions)
default_pool_size = 20       # Per user/database pair
max_client_conn = 1000       # Accept many client connections
min_pool_size = 5            # Keep 5 connections ready
reserve_pool_size = 5        # Extra connections for spikes
reserve_pool_timeout = 3     # Wait 3s before using reserve

# Timeouts
query_timeout = 30           # Kill queries after 30s
client_idle_timeout = 600    # Disconnect idle clients after 10min
server_idle_timeout = 300    # Return idle connections after 5min
```

**Impact on Docita:**

With 25 clinics, 5 NestJS instances, each with 10 connections = 150 client connections. PgBouncer multiplexes these onto 20 database connections (transaction mode). This prevents PostgreSQL from being overwhelmed.

**30-second answer:** Use the formula: connections = (CPU cores × 2) + 1 for SSDs. For a 4-core server, max 9 connections. Use PgBouncer in transaction mode to multiplex many client connections onto fewer database connections. Never exceed PostgreSQL's connection limit.

---

**Q22: "What happens when a connection hangs? How does PgBouncer handle it?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
**Answer (full):**

PgBouncer provides several timeout mechanisms to prevent resource leaks and hung connections.

**Key timeouts:**

```ini
[pgbouncer]
# Kill queries that run too long
query_timeout = 30           # 30 seconds max per query

# Disconnect clients that are idle too long
client_idle_timeout = 600    # 10 minutes

# Return idle server connections to the pool
server_idle_timeout = 300    # 5 minutes

# Kill transactions that run too long
query_wait_timeout = 120     # 2 minutes max waiting for a server connection

# Cancel queries that take too long (PostgreSQL 14+)
cancel_after = 10            # Cancel after 10 seconds
```

**Failure modes:**

| Scenario | What happens | Fix |
|----------|-------------|-----|
| Long-running query | query_timeout kills it | Set appropriate timeout |
| Idle client (connection leak) | client_idle_timeout disconnects | Set based on app behavior |
| Connection pool exhaustion | query_wait_timeout kills waiting clients | Increase pool size or optimize queries |
| Transaction left open | server_idle_timeout returns connection | Use connection: close in NestJS |

**Monitoring:**

```sql
SHOW POOLS;    -- Active connections per pool
SHOW STATS;    -- Query statistics
SHOW CLIENTS;  -- Connected clients
SHOW SERVERS;  -- Active server connections
```

**30-second answer:** PgBouncer timeouts prevent resource leaks: `query_timeout` kills long queries, `client_idle_timeout` disconnects idle clients, `server_idle_timeout` returns idle connections to the pool. Set `query_timeout` based on your slowest acceptable query (30s for most APIs).

---

### 1.5 Query Planning and Indexes

**Q23: "How does the query planner choose between seq scan, index scan, and bitmap scan? Show me the EXPLAIN ANALYZE output for a patient list query."**

**Answer skeleton:**
**Answer (full):**

PostgreSQL's query planner chooses between different scan strategies based on cost estimates. Understanding these decisions is critical for query optimization.

**Scan types:**

```
Sequential Scan (Seq Scan):
┌────────────────────────────┐
│ Read every row in table    │
│ Cost: O(n)                 │
│ Good for: <10% of rows,   │
│   small tables, low selectivity│
└────────────────────────────┘

Index Scan:
┌────────────────────────────┐
│ Use B-tree index to find   │
│ matching rows, then fetch  │
│ from heap                  │
│ Cost: O(log n + k)         │
│ Good for: <10% of rows,   │
│   high selectivity         │
└────────────────────────────┘

Bitmap Scan:
┌────────────────────────────┐
│ 1. Index scan → bitmap of  │
│    matching row locations  │
│ 2. Heap fetch in physical  │
│    order (reduces I/O)     │
│ Cost: O(log n + k)         │
│ Good for: 10-50% of rows  │
└────────────────────────────┘
```

**EXPLAIN ANALYZE example:**

```sql
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT * FROM appointments
WHERE practitioner_id = 'dr_1'
  AND slot_start::date = '2026-08-25';

-- Seq Scan cost: 1000 (reads all 100K rows)
-- Index Scan cost: 5 (reads 3 matching rows)
-- Planner chooses Index Scan when cost is lower
```

**When the planner chooses wrong:**

```sql
-- Problem: Statistics are stale
ANALYZE appointments;  -- Update statistics

-- Problem: Correlated data (practitioner_id clusters with slot_start)
-- Planner thinks index scan is efficient, but it's not
-- Fix: Create a composite index:
CREATE INDEX idx_appt_prac_slot ON appointments (practitioner_id, slot_start);

-- Problem: Large LIMIT with ORDER BY
-- Seq scan + sort may be faster than index scan
-- Fix: Use a covering index with INCLUDE:
CREATE INDEX idx_appt_covering ON appointments (practitioner_id, slot_start)
INCLUDE (patient_id, status, note);
```

**Impact on Docita:**

The booking query `WHERE practitioner_id = 'dr_1' AND slot_start::date = '2026-08-25'` returns ~3 rows from 100K. The planner correctly chooses Index Scan (cost: 5 vs Seq Scan: 1000). But after 6 months without ANALYZE, statistics become stale and the planner might choose Seq Scan. Regular ANALYZE (via autovacuum) prevents this.

**30-second answer:** The planner chooses Seq Scan for small tables or high selectivity (>10% of rows), Index Scan for low selectivity (<10%), and Bitmap Scan for medium selectivity. Use EXPLAIN ANALYZE to see the chosen plan. Update statistics with ANALYZE if the planner chooses wrong.

---

**Q24: "When does a composite index on (a, b) work but (b, a) doesn't?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
**Answer (full):**

Composite index ordering determines which queries can use the index. This is one of the most important indexing concepts.

**The B-tree leftmost prefix rule:**

```
Index: (a, b)

Can satisfy:
✅ WHERE a = 1                    (uses column a)
✅ WHERE a = 1 AND b = 2          (uses both columns)
✅ WHERE a = 1 ORDER BY b         (uses a for filter, b for sort)
✅ WHERE a = 1 AND b > 5 AND b < 10  (range on b)

Cannot satisfy:
❌ WHERE b = 2                    (skips column a — can't use index)
❌ WHERE a > 1 AND b = 2          (range on a prevents using b)
❌ WHERE a = 1 OR b = 2           (OR prevents index usage)
```

**Why ordering matters:**

```
Index (practitioner_id, slot_start):
- WHERE practitioner_id = 'dr_1' → efficient (leftmost prefix)
- WHERE practitioner_id = 'dr_1' AND slot_start > now() → efficient
- WHERE slot_start > now() → INEFFICIENT (skips practitioner_id)

Index (slot_start, practitioner_id):
- WHERE slot_start > now() → efficient
- WHERE slot_start > now() AND practitioner_id = 'dr_1' → efficient
- WHERE practitioner_id = 'dr_1' → INEFFICIENT (skips slot_start)
```

**For Docita's booking query:**

```sql
-- Query: "Find all bookings for Dr. Smith after 2 PM"
WHERE practitioner_id = 'dr_1' AND slot_start > '2026-08-25 14:00'

-- Best index: (practitioner_id, slot_start)
-- Why: practitioner_id is equality (high selectivity)
--       slot_start is range (comes after equality in index)
-- This index satisfies both the filter AND the sort
```

**Covering indexes:**

```sql
-- If the query only needs a few columns, use INCLUDE:
CREATE INDEX idx_appt_covering
ON appointments (practitioner_id, slot_start)
INCLUDE (patient_id, status);

-- This index satisfies:
SELECT patient_id, status FROM appointments
WHERE practitioner_id = 'dr_1' AND slot_start > now();

-- WITHOUT needing to touch the heap (index-only scan)
-- 2-5x faster than a regular index scan
```

**30-second answer:** A composite index (a, b) can only be used when the query filters on the leftmost prefix (a, or a+b). Put the equality column first, range column second. Use INCLUDE for covering indexes to avoid heap access.

---

**Q25: "What is a partial index? When would you use one on the appointments table?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
**Answer (full):**

A partial index indexes only a subset of rows, making it smaller and faster than a full index.

**How it works:**

```sql
-- Full index (indexes ALL appointments):
CREATE INDEX idx_appointments_status ON appointments (status);
-- Size: 5MB (indexes 100K rows)

-- Partial index (indexes ONLY pending appointments):
CREATE INDEX idx_appointments_pending ON appointments (practitioner_id, slot_start)
WHERE status = 'pending';
-- Size: 200KB (indexes only 2K pending rows)
-- 25x smaller, 25x faster!
```

**When to use partial indexes:**

```sql
-- 1. Low-cardinality status columns:
CREATE INDEX idx_appointments_pending ON appointments (practitioner_id, slot_start)
WHERE status = 'pending';

-- 2. Recent data only:
CREATE INDEX idx_appointments_recent ON appointments (practitioner_id)
WHERE created_at > '2026-01-01';

-- 3. Soft deletes (exclude deleted rows):
CREATE INDEX idx_active_patients ON patients (email)
WHERE deleted_at IS NULL;

-- 4. Unique constraints on subset:
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX idx_unique_active_email ON patients (email)
WHERE deleted_at IS NULL;
```

**Query must match the WHERE clause:**

```sql
-- This query USES the partial index:
SELECT * FROM appointments
WHERE status = 'pending' AND practitioner_id = 'dr_1';

-- This query DOES NOT use it:
SELECT * FROM appointments
WHERE status = 'confirmed' AND practitioner_id = 'dr_1';
-- (doesn't match WHERE status = 'pending')
```

**Impact on Docita:**

The appointments table has 100K rows but only 2K are pending. A partial index on pending appointments is 50x smaller and faster than a full index. This makes the booking availability check (which only looks at pending slots) extremely fast.

**30-second answer:** A partial index indexes only rows matching a WHERE clause. It's smaller and faster than a full index. Use it for low-cardinality status columns, recent data, or soft deletes. The query must match the index's WHERE clause to use it.

---

## Section 2: Distributed Systems (20 questions)

### 2.1 CAP Theorem and Consistency

**Q26: "Apply CAP theorem to Docita. Is it CP or AP? What happens when the database is partitioned?"**

**Answer (full):**

CAP theorem states that a distributed system can only guarantee two of three properties: Consistency, Availability, and Partition tolerance. Since network partitions are unavoidable, the real choice is between CP and AP.

**CAP theorem explained:**

```
        Consistency
           /\
          /  \
         /    \
        / CP   \
       /  (Docita)\
      /____________\
     /              \
    / AP             \
   /  (Cassandra)     \
  /____________________\
Availability    Partition Tolerance
```

| System | Choice | Why |
|--------|--------|-----|
| **Docita** | CP | Healthcare requires consistency (no stale patient data) |
| **Cassandra** | AP | Social media tolerates stale data for availability |
| **ZooKeeper** | CP | Coordination requires consistency |
| **DynamoDB** | AP (default) | Configurable per-operation |

**What CP means for Docita:**

When a network partition occurs between the API and PostgreSQL:

```
Normal operation:
  API ──network──▶ PostgreSQL
  ✅ Writes succeed
  ✅ Reads return current data

Partition:
  API ──X──▶ PostgreSQL (network split)
  ❌ Writes FAIL (return error)
  ❌ Reads FAIL (return error)
  
  Docita sacrifices AVAILABILITY to preserve CONSISTENCY
  Users see "Service temporarily unavailable" instead of stale data
```

**Why CP is correct for healthcare:**

```
Scenario: A clinician checks a patient's medication allergies

AP (Availability): Return stale data (allergy list from yesterday)
  → Clinician prescribes medication that interacts with allergy
  → Patient harm

CP (Consistency): Return error ("Unable to verify allergies")
  → Clinician checks paper records or calls pharmacy
  → Patient safe

For healthcare: error > stale data, always
```

**How Docita handles partitions:**

```
1. API returns 503 Service Unavailable
2. User sees "Connection issue — please retry"
3. Outbox events are NOT lost (they're in PostgreSQL, which is unreachable)
4. When connectivity restores:
   a. Queued API requests retry automatically
   b. Outbox relay resumes processing
   c. Worker resumes processing events
5. No data is lost, but there's a delay
```

**The outbox pattern as partition tolerance:**

```typescript
// Even if the worker is unreachable, the outbox event is safe
await prisma.$transaction(async (tx) => {
  await tx.appointment.create({ data: { ... } });
  await tx.outboxEvent.create({ data: { ... } });
  // Both committed to PostgreSQL — safe even if network partitions later
});

// When connectivity restores, the relay processes the backlog
```

**What NOT to claim:** "Docita is CA" (CA is impossible in a distributed system — you must choose CP or AP). "Docita never goes down" (it does during partitions, by design — CP sacrifices availability).

---

**Q27: "What is eventual consistency? How do you test for it in Docita's outbox pattern?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
**Answer (full):**

Eventual consistency is a guarantee that if no new updates are made, all replicas will eventually converge to the same value. It's the fundamental trade-off in distributed systems.

**The CAP theorem context:**

```
CAP Theorem: You can only guarantee 2 of 3:
- Consistency: Every read returns the most recent write
- Availability: Every request gets a response
- Partition tolerance: System works despite network failures

In practice, partitions WILL happen (networks fail)
So you choose: CP (consistent but may be unavailable) or AP (available but may be inconsistent)

Eventual consistency = AP: Available during partitions, but reads may be stale
```

**How eventual consistency works:**

```
Time 0: Write x=1 to primary
Time 1: Primary confirms to client
Time 2: WAL sent to replica 1 (lag: 100ms)
Time 3: WAL sent to replica 2 (lag: 500ms)

Between Time 1 and Time 3:
- Replica 1: x=0 (stale)
- Replica 2: x=0 (stale)
- Primary: x=1 (current)

After Time 3:
- All replicas: x=1 (converged)
```

**Testing eventual consistency in Docita's outbox:**

```sql
-- Test: Write to appointments table, immediately read from replica
-- Expected: Read may return stale data for up to replica_lag seconds

-- Monitor replication lag:
SELECT
  client_addr,
  pg_wal_lsl_diff(sent_lsn, replay_lsn) AS lag_bytes
FROM pg_stat_replication;

-- If lag > 1 second, investigate:
-- 1. Network latency between primary and replica
-- 2. Replica CPU/memory saturation
-- 3. Long-running queries on replica blocking WAL apply
```

**When eventual consistency is acceptable:**

```
✅ Dashboard queries (stale by 1 second is fine)
✅ Search results (stale by 5 seconds is fine)
✅ Analytics queries (stale by 1 minute is fine)

❌ Booking confirmation (must read current data)
❌ Payment processing (must read current balance)
❌ Inventory check (must read current stock)
```

**30-second answer:** Eventual consistency means replicas may serve stale reads for a short time after a write, but will eventually converge. It's the AP choice in CAP theorem. Acceptable for dashboards and search, not for booking confirmations or payments. Monitor replication lag to ensure convergence time stays within SLA.

---

**Q28: "What is linearizability? When would you need it vs eventual consistency?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
**Answer (full):**

Linearizability is the strongest consistency model. Every operation appears to take effect atomically at some point between its invocation and completion.

**How it differs from other models:**

```
Sequential consistency:
- Operations appear in some valid order
- But NOT necessarily real-time order
- Example: A writes x=1, B reads x=0, then A completes
  (B's read happened before A's write was visible)

Linearizability:
- Operations appear in real-time order
- Example: A writes x=1 at T1, B reads at T2 > T1
  B MUST see x=1 (or a later value)
```

**When you need linearizability:**

```sql
-- 1. Unique constraints (booking a slot):
-- Two users try to book the same slot
-- Without linearizability: both could succeed (double booking)

-- 2. Leader election:
-- Without linearizability: two nodes think they're leader (split brain)

-- 3. Counters:
-- Without linearizability: increment may be lost (lost update)
```

**PostgreSQL's linearizability:**

```
PostgreSQL provides linearizability within a single database:
- SERIALIZABLE isolation level guarantees linearizable execution
- All reads and writes appear in real-time order

But NOT across replicas:
- Asynchronous replication may serve stale reads
- Even synchronous replication has a window (WAL apply delay)
```

**Impact on Docita:**

The booking overlap check uses `SELECT FOR UPDATE` within a serializable transaction. This provides linearizability for the booking operation — only one user can book a given slot. No double bookings are possible.

**30-second answer:** Linearizability means every operation appears to take effect atomically at a specific point in real-time. It's the strongest consistency model, needed for leader election, unique constraints, and counters. PostgreSQL provides it within SERIALIZABLE isolation, but not across asynchronous replicas.

---

**Q29: "What is read-your-writes consistency? How does Docita ensure a user sees their own booking immediately?"**

**Answer (full):**

Read-your-writes consistency guarantees that after a user makes a write, all subsequent reads by that user see the write. Without this, a user could book an appointment and then see "no appointments" on the next page load.

**The problem:**

```
Timeline:
  T1: User books appointment (writes to primary)
  T2: User refreshes page (reads from replica — lagging by 100ms)
  T3: Replica hasn't received the write yet
  Result: User sees "no appointments" — confusing and broken
```

**How Docita ensures read-your-writes:**

```typescript
// Option 1: Return the created resource in the response (simplest)
const appointment = await prisma.appointment.create({ data: { ... } });
return {
  id: appointment.id,
  status: 'booked',
  // User sees the appointment immediately — no read needed
};

// Option 2: Read from primary after write
await prisma.appointment.create({ data: { ... } });
// Next read goes to primary (not replica)
const appointments = await prisma.appointment.findMany({
  where: { patientId, clinicId },
  // Force primary read (Prisma: { rejectOnNotFound: true } or custom routing)
});

// Option 3: Session stickiness (load balancer routes to same instance)
// The instance that processed the write also handles subsequent reads
// Trade-off: uneven load distribution
```

**The trade-off:**

| Approach | Consistency | Performance | Complexity |
|----------|------------|-------------|------------|
| **Return in response** | ✅ Perfect | ✅ Fast (no extra read) | Low |
| **Read from primary** | ✅ Perfect | ⚠️ Primary overload | Medium |
| **Session stickiness** | ✅ Good (within session) | ⚠️ Uneven load | Medium |
| **Accept staleness** | ❌ User may see stale data | ✅ Best (read from replica) | Low |

**For Docita:** The first approach (return the created resource) is the simplest and most effective. After booking, the API returns the full appointment object. The user sees it immediately without needing to read from the database.

**What NOT to claim:** "Docita uses session stickiness" (it returns the created resource in the response). "Read-your-writes is automatic with PostgreSQL" (it's not — you must design for it).

---

**Q30: "What is causal consistency? How does it relate to event ordering in the notification system?"**

**Answer (full):**

Causal consistency is a middle ground between strong consistency and eventual consistency. It guarantees that causally related events are seen in order, but unrelated events may be seen out of order.

**The consistency spectrum:**

```
Strong consistency:     All events seen in total order
                        (slow, expensive)

Causal consistency:     Causally related events in order
                        (moderate, practical)

Eventual consistency:   All events eventually seen
                        (fast, may be out of order)

No consistency:         Events may never be seen
                        (unusable)
```

**What "causally related" means:**

```
Event A causes Event B if:
  1. A happened before B in real time, AND
  2. B was triggered by A (or A's result was used in B)

Example in Docita:
  A: Appointment booked (creates appointment_123)
  B: Notification sent (references appointment_123)
  
  A causes B — B cannot happen without A
  All users must see A before B

Counter-example:
  A: Clinic A books appointment
  B: Clinic B books appointment
  
  A and B are independent — no causal relationship
  Order doesn't matter
```

**How Docita ensures causal consistency:**

```
Approach 1: Single service (simplest)
  Both A (booking) and B (notification) go through the same API
  The API processes them sequentially
  Causal order is guaranteed by the single service

Approach 2: Outbox pattern (Docita's approach)
  A (booking) and outbox_event are in the same transaction
  Outbox relay publishes events in creation order
  Worker processes events in order
  Causal order is preserved by the outbox

Approach 3: Vector clocks (for distributed systems)
  Each event carries a vector clock: { node_A: 1, node_B: 0 }
  Receiver checks: "Is this event's cause already seen?"
  If not, buffer the event until the cause arrives
  Used in: Dynamo, Riak, Cassandra
```

**For Docita's notification system:**

```
Causal chain:
  1. User books appointment (event A)
  2. Outbox event created (event B, caused by A)
  3. Worker sends email (event C, caused by B)
  
  All three events are causally linked
  They must be seen in order: A → B → C

How Docita guarantees this:
  A and B are in the same PostgreSQL transaction (atomic)
  B is published by the relay in creation order
  C is processed by the worker in event order
  
  Causal consistency is guaranteed by the outbox pattern
```

**What NOT to claim:** "Causal consistency is the same as strong consistency" (it's weaker — unrelated events can be out of order). "Docita uses vector clocks" (it uses the outbox pattern for causal ordering).

---

**Q31: "What is the PACELC theorem? How does it extend CAP?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
**Answer (full):**

PACELC extends the CAP theorem to cover the normal (non-partition) case. It's a more complete framework for evaluating distributed databases.

**The PACELC framework:**

```
If Partition (P):
  Choose Availability (A) or Consistency (C)

Else (E) — normal operation:
  Choose Latency (L) or Consistency (C)

This gives us 4 combinations:
PA/EL: Available + Low latency (e.g., Cassandra, DynamoDB)
PA/EC: Available + Consistent (e.g., CockroachDB)
PC/EL: Consistent during partition + Low latency (e.g., MongoDB)
PC/EC: Consistent always (e.g., Spanner, YugabyteDB)
```

**Real-world examples:**

| Database | PACELC | Trade-off |
|----------|--------|-----------|
| PostgreSQL | PC/EC | Consistent always, higher latency |
| CockroachDB | PA/EC | Available during partition, consistent normally |
| Cassandra | PA/EL | Available + low latency (tunable consistency) |
| DynamoDB | PA/EL | Available + low latency |
| Spanner | PC/EC | Consistent always (uses TrueTime) |

**Impact on Docita:**

PostgreSQL (PC/EC) means:
- During network partition: The primary becomes unavailable to replicas (writes block)
- Normal operation: Consistent reads, but higher latency than eventual consistency
- This is the right choice for healthcare (consistency > availability)

**30-second answer:** PACELC extends CAP: if Partition, choose A or C; Else, choose L or C. PostgreSQL is PC/EC (consistent always). Cassandra is PA/EL (available + low latency). For healthcare, PC/EC is correct — consistency matters more than latency.

---

### 2.2 Distributed Transactions

**Q32: "Explain the outbox pattern in detail. What are the failure modes at each step?"**

**Answer (full):**

The outbox pattern solves the dual-write problem: you need to write to both the database and trigger asynchronous work (notifications, analytics, external API calls) atomically. Without the outbox, you face an impossible choice: write to DB first (if the async trigger fails, you've lost the event) or trigger first (if the DB write fails, you've published a ghost event).

**The architecture:**

```
┌─────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     ┌─────────────┐     ┌──────────┐
│    API       │     │   Outbox     │     │   Worker    │     │ Provider │
│  (write)     │     │   (table)    │     │  (process)  │     │ (SendGrid│
│              │────▶│  ┌────────┐  │────▶│             │────▶│  Twilio) │
│  Transaction │     │  │ event  │  │     │  Consume +  │     │          │
│  includes:   │     │  │ status │  │     │  Process +  │     │          │
│  - business  │     │  └────────┘  │     │  Mark done  │     │          │
│  - outbox    │     └──────────────┘     └─────────────┘     └──────────┘
└─────────────┘           ▲
                          │
                    Outbox relay
                    (polls every 5s)
```

**Step-by-step flow with failure modes:**

**Step 1: API writes business data + outbox event in one transaction**

```typescript
// The critical transaction — both writes must succeed or both must fail
await prisma.$transaction(async (tx) => {
  // Business write
  const appointment = await tx.appointment.create({
    data: { patientId, practitionerId, slotStart, slotEnd, clinicId }
  });
  
  // Outbox write (same transaction!)
  await tx.outboxEvent.create({
    data: {
      aggregateType: 'Appointment',
      aggregateId: appointment.id,
      eventType: 'APPOINTMENT_CREATED',
      payload: JSON.stringify({ appointmentId: appointment.id, clinicId }),
      status: 'pending',
    }
  });
  
  return appointment;
  // COMMIT: both appointment and outbox event are written atomically
});
```

| Failure | What happens | Recovery |
