|---------|-------------|----------|
| DB disk full | Transaction rolls back | User retries |
| Network timeout | Transaction rolls back | User retries |
| Application crash before commit | Transaction rolls back | User retries |
| **Success** | Both rows committed | Relay picks up event |

**Step 2: Outbox relay polls for unprocessed events**

```typescript
// Relay runs as a background loop
async function outboxRelay() {
  while (true) {
    const events = await prisma.outboxEvent.findMany({
      where: { status: 'pending' },
      orderBy: { createdAt: 'asc' },
      take: 100,
    });
    
    for (const event of events) {
      try {
        await eventBus.publish(event.eventType, event.payload);
        await prisma.outboxEvent.update({
          where: { id: event.id },
          data: { status: 'published' }
        });
      } catch (error) {
        // Will retry on next poll cycle
        console.error('Failed to publish event:', error);
      }
    }
    
    await sleep(5000); // Poll every 5 seconds
  }
}
```

| Failure | What happens | Recovery |
|---------|-------------|----------|
| Relay crashes | Events stay in DB with status='pending' | Relay restarts, processes backlog |
| Relay loses DB connection | Poll fails, events stay pending | Connection restored, processes backlog |
| Relay publishes but crashes before marking published | Event published but status still 'pending' | Worker processes it (idempotent), relay re-publishes (idempotent) |

**Step 3: Worker processes the event**

```typescript
// Worker subscribes to event bus
async function handleAppointmentCreated(event: OutboxEvent) {
  const payload = JSON.parse(event.payload);
  
  // Idempotency check: have we already processed this event?
  const existing = await prisma.processedEvent.findUnique({
    where: { eventId: event.id }
  });
  if (existing) {
    console.log('Event already processed, skipping');
    return; // Already processed — skip
  }
  
  // Process: send confirmation email
  await sendGrid.send({
    to: payload.patientEmail,
    template: 'appointment-confirmation',
    data: { appointmentId: payload.appointmentId }
  });
  
  // Mark as processed (atomically with the effect)
  await prisma.processedEvent.create({
    data: { eventId: event.id, processedAt: new Date() }
  });
}
```

| Failure | What happens | Recovery |
|---------|-------------|----------|
| Provider (SendGrid) down | Email not sent | Worker retries with backoff |
| Worker crashes mid-processing | Event partially processed | Idempotency check prevents duplicate on retry |
| Worker processes but crashes before marking done | Event reprocessed | Idempotent handler: duplicate email is same effect |
| Provider returns error | Event stays in retry queue | Worker retries up to N times, then DLQ |

**Step 4: Worker marks event as processed**

```typescript
// Atomic: the business effect + the processed marker must both succeed
await prisma.$transaction(async (tx) => {
  // The business effect (already done above)
  
  // Mark as processed
  await tx.processedEvent.create({
    data: { eventId: event.id, processedAt: new Date() }
  });
});
```

| Failure | What happens | Recovery |
|---------|-------------|----------|
| DB write fails | Event stays 'pending', relay re-publishes | Idempotent handler skips it |
| Transaction rolls back | Event stays 'pending', relay re-publishes | Idempotent handler skips it |

**The key insight: idempotency at every step.**

Every step can fail and retry. The system is designed so that retrying any step is safe:
- Step 1: Transactional (atomic or nothing)
- Step 2: At-least-once delivery (relay may publish twice)
- Step 3: Idempotent handler (processing twice = same effect)
- Step 4: Idempotent marker (creating twice = same effect)

**Why the outbox over alternatives:**

| Approach | Consistency | Complexity | Reliability |
|----------|------------|------------|-------------|
| **Outbox pattern** | Strong (atomic DB write) | Medium | High (reliable delivery) |
| **Direct publish** | Weak (dual-write problem) | Low | Low (events can be lost) |
| **CDC (Debezium)** | Strong (WAL-based) | High | High (but operational overhead) |
| **Event sourcing** | Strong (events ARE the data) | Very high | Very high (but requires major refactor) |

**What NOT to claim:** "The outbox pattern guarantees exactly-once delivery" (it guarantees at-least-once; idempotency makes it effectively-once). "The outbox is a message queue" (it's a database table that acts as a staging area; the message queue is separate).

---

**Q33: "What is a saga? How does it differ from two-phase commit? When would you use each?"**

**Answer (full):**

Both sagas and 2PC solve the problem of maintaining consistency across multiple services or databases. They take fundamentally different approaches.

**Two-Phase Commit (2PC):**

```
Phase 1 (Prepare):
  Coordinator → Participant A: "Prepare to commit"
  Coordinator → Participant B: "Prepare to commit"
  Participant A: "Ready" (locks resources)
  Participant B: "Ready" (locks resources)
  
Phase 2 (Commit):
  Coordinator → Participant A: "Commit"
  Coordinator → Participant B: "Commit"
  Both commit and release locks
```

**The problem with 2PC:**

```
Coordinator crashes between Phase 1 and Phase 2:
  Participant A: "Ready" (holding lock, waiting)
  Participant B: "Ready" (holding lock, waiting)
  
  Both participants are blocked until the coordinator recovers
  Resources are locked — no other transactions can proceed
```

**Saga:**

```
Step 1: Book appointment (local transaction)
  Success → Step 2
  Failure → Done (nothing to compensate)

Step 2: Process payment (local transaction)
  Success → Step 3
  Failure → Compensate Step 1: Cancel appointment

Step 3: Send notification (local transaction)
  Success → Done
  Failure → Compensate Step 2: Refund payment
           Compensate Step 1: Cancel appointment
```

**Detailed comparison:**

| Aspect | 2PC | Saga |
|--------|-----|------|
| **Consistency** | Strong (atomic across all participants) | Eventual (each step is independent) |
| **Locking** | Global locks held during entire transaction | No global locks |
| **Blocking** | Participants block if coordinator fails | Never blocks |
| **Performance** | Low (locks held for duration) | High (no locks) |
| **Complexity** | Medium (coordinator + participants) | High (compensating actions) |
| **Use case** | Short-lived atomic operations | Long-running business processes |
| **Failure handling** | Automatic rollback (if coordinator is up) | Manual compensation logic |

**When to use each:**

**Use 2PC when:**
- You need strong consistency across two databases
- The transaction is short-lived (milliseconds)
- You can tolerate the blocking risk
- Example: transferring money between two accounts in different databases

**Use Saga when:**
- The business process spans multiple services
- The process is long-running (seconds to minutes)
- You can tolerate eventual consistency
- You need compensating actions (refund, cancel)
- Example: booking → payment → notification in Docita

**For Docita specifically:**

Docita uses a **saga-like pattern** with the outbox:

```
Step 1: Book appointment (transaction: appointment + outbox event)
  Success → Step 2
  Failure → Transaction rolls back (nothing happens)

Step 2: Worker processes notification (async)
  Success → Done
  Failure → Retry with backoff (no compensation needed)
  Permanent failure → DLQ + manual intervention
```

The key insight: Docita's saga is simplified because:
1. Step 1 is transactional (atomic)
2. Step 2 is eventually consistent (notification can be delayed)
3. There's no need for compensation (if notification fails, the appointment still exists)

**What NOT to claim:** "Docita uses 2PC" (it uses the outbox pattern, which is saga-like). "Sagas always require compensation" (some sagas, like Docita's, have steps that can simply be retried).

---

**Q34: **Answer (full):**

The outbox pattern ensures reliable event publishing without dual-write problems. There are three implementation strategies, each with different trade-offs.

**Strategy 1: Transactional Outbox + Polling Publisher:**

```
┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐
│ Service  │────▶│ Database │────▶│ Polling  │
│ (write)  │     │ (outbox) │     │ Publisher│
└──────────┘     └──────────┘     └──────────┘
     │                                  │
     │ Single transaction               │ Separate process
     │ (business + outbox)              │ polls every 1s
     │                                  │
     └──────────────────────────────────┘
```

```sql
-- Business write + outbox in same transaction:
BEGIN;
INSERT INTO appointments (...) VALUES (...);
INSERT INTO outbox (aggregate_type, aggregate_id, event_type, payload)
VALUES ('appointment', 'apt_123', 'APPOINTMENT_CREATED', '{"id":"apt_123",...}');
COMMIT;

-- Polling publisher (separate process):
SELECT * FROM outbox WHERE published = false ORDER BY created_at LIMIT 100;
-- Publish to Kafka, then mark as published
UPDATE outbox SET published = true WHERE id IN (...);
```

**Strategy 2: Transactional Outbox + CDC (Change Data Capture):**

```
┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐
│ Service  │────▶│ Database │────▶│ Debezium │────▶│ Kafka    │
│ (write)  │     │ (outbox) │     │ (CDC)    │     │          │
└──────────┘     └──────────┘     └──────────┘     └──────────┘
```

```sql
-- Debezium reads the WAL (not polling the table!)
-- It detects INSERT on outbox table and publishes to Kafka
-- Much more efficient than polling (no load on database)
-- Near-real-time (latency: 100ms vs 1s for polling)
```

**Strategy 3: Polling Publisher with LISTEN/NOTIFY:**

```
┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐
│ Service  │────▶│ Database │────▶│ Listener │
│ (write)  │     │ (NOTIFY) │     │ (wake up)│
└──────────┘     └──────────┘     └──────────┘
```

```sql
-- After inserting outbox row:
NOTIFY outbox_channel, 'new_event';

-- Listener process:
LISTEN outbox_channel;
-- Wakes up immediately, no polling delay
```

**Comparison:**

| Strategy | Latency | DB Load | Complexity | Reliability |
|----------|---------|---------|------------|-------------|
| Polling | 1-5s | High (frequent queries) | Low | High |
| CDC (Debezium) | 100ms | Low (reads WAL) | High | Very High |
| LISTEN/NOTIFY | 10ms | Low | Medium | Medium (missed notifications) |

**Impact on Docita:**

Docita uses Strategy 1 (Polling) for simplicity. The polling interval is 1 second, which is acceptable for appointment events. For real-time notifications (WebSocket), LISTEN/NOTIFY is used alongside the outbox.

**30-second answer:** Three strategies: (1) Polling — simple but adds DB load, (2) CDC with Debezium — efficient but complex, (3) LISTEN/NOTIFY — low latency but may miss notifications. Choose based on your latency requirements and operational complexity budget.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Transactional outbox: write event to outbox table in same transaction as business data. Polling publisher: separate process polls the outbox table and publishes to the message broker. CDC-based publisher: use Change Data Capture (Debezium) to capture changes from the WAL and publish to the broker. Trade-offs: transactional outbox is simplest but adds DB load from polling. CDC is most efficient but adds operational complexity (Debezium, Kafka Connect)."

---

**Q35: **Answer (full):**

Compensating transactions are the undo mechanism for sagas. Since sagas don't use distributed transactions (2PC), each step must have a reverse operation that can undo its effects.

**What compensating transactions are:**

```
Saga: Book appointment → Send notification → Process payment

If payment fails, we need to:
1. Undo the notification (cancel email)
2. Undo the appointment booking (cancel the slot)

These "undo" operations are compensating transactions.
They don't literally reverse the database changes —
they bring the system to an equivalent state.
```

**Implementation pattern:**

```typescript
// Saga with compensating transactions
const bookAppointmentSaga = new Saga({
  steps: [
    {
      name: 'bookAppointment',
      execute: async (ctx) => {
        const apt = await this.appointmentService.create(ctx.dto);
        ctx.appointmentId = apt.id;
        return apt;
      },
      compensate: async (ctx) => {
        // This is the compensating transaction
        if (ctx.appointmentId) {
          await this.appointmentService.cancel(ctx.appointmentId, {
            reason: 'Saga compensation: payment failed'
          });
        }
      }
    },
    {
      name: 'sendNotification',
      execute: async (ctx) => {
        await this.notificationService.send(ctx.appointmentId);
      },
      compensate: async (ctx) => {
        // Cancel the notification
        await this.notificationService.cancel(ctx.appointmentId);
      }
    },
    {
      name: 'processPayment',
      execute: async (ctx) => {
        await this.paymentService.charge(ctx.patientId, ctx.amount);
      }
      // No compensate needed — if this fails, the saga rolls back
    }
  ]
});
```

**Compensation patterns:**

| Pattern | Example | When to use |
|---------|---------|-------------|
| Semantic undo | Cancel appointment | Most common |
| Counteraction | Send refund | Financial operations |
| Semantic rollback | Mark as cancelled | When true undo isn't possible |
| Exception | Log and alert | When compensation is impossible |

**Key principles:**

1. Compensations must be idempotent (safe to retry)
2. Compensations must be commutative (order doesn't matter)
3. Compensations must be fault-tolerant (handle failures gracefully)
4. Some operations can't be compensated (emails sent) — use best-effort

**30-second answer:** Compensating transactions are the "undo" mechanism for sagas. Each saga step has a corresponding compensation that brings the system to an equivalent state. Compensations must be idempotent and commutative. For operations that can't be undone (like emails), use best-effort compensation and log for manual review.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"A partially completed saga needs compensating actions to undo completed steps. Example: booking → payment → notification. If payment succeeds but notification fails, the saga compensates: refund the payment, cancel the booking. Compensating transactions must be idempotent (calling them twice has the same effect as calling them once). Design principle: every step in the saga must have a compensating action, and the compensation must be idempotent."

---

**Q36: "What is the dual-write problem? How does the outbox pattern solve it?"**

**Answer (full):**

The dual-write problem is one of the hardest challenges in distributed systems. It occurs when you need to update two systems atomically but they don't share a transaction.

**The problem:**

```
Scenario: Book an appointment AND send a notification

Approach 1: Write to DB first, then publish event
  1. DB: INSERT appointment ✅
  2. Broker: PUBLISH notification ❌ (SendGrid is down)
  Result: Appointment exists, but notification is LOST
  User booked but never received confirmation

Approach 2: Publish event first, then write to DB
  1. Broker: PUBLISH notification ✅
  2. DB: INSERT appointment ❌ (DB is full)
  Result: Notification sent for appointment that DOESN'T EXIST
  User receives confirmation for a booking that never happened
```

**Both approaches are broken.** The fundamental issue: you can't atomically write to two different systems.

**How the outbox pattern solves it:**

```
Step 1: Write BOTH to the database (one transaction)
  DB Transaction:
    INSERT appointment → ✅
    INSERT outbox_event → ✅
  COMMIT → Both committed atomically

Step 2: Asynchronously publish from outbox
  Outbox relay: polls for unprocessed events
  Worker: processes event, sends notification

  If relay fails: events stay in DB (safe)
  If worker fails: events stay in DB (safe)
  If provider fails: worker retries (safe)
```

**The key insight:** The database is the single source of truth. The outbox event is just a flag saying "this appointment needs a notification." The actual notification is sent asynchronously by the worker.

**Implementation:**

```typescript
// The outbox transaction
await prisma.$transaction(async (tx) => {
  // Business write
  const appointment = await tx.appointment.create({
    data: { patientId, practitionerId, slotStart, slotEnd, clinicId }
  });
  
  // Outbox write (same transaction — atomic!)
  await tx.outboxEvent.create({
    data: {
      aggregateType: 'Appointment',
      aggregateId: appointment.id,
      eventType: 'APPOINTMENT_CREATED',
      payload: JSON.stringify({
        appointmentId: appointment.id,
        patientEmail: patient.email,
        clinicId,
      }),
      status: 'pending',
    }
  });
  
  // COMMIT: both rows written atomically
  // If either fails, both roll back — no ghost events
});
```

**Why this works:**

| Scenario | Without Outbox | With Outbox |
|----------|---------------|-------------|
| DB commits, broker fails | Notification lost | Event stays in DB, relay retries |
| Broker publishes, DB fails | Ghost notification | Event never created (transaction rolled back) |
| Both succeed | Works | Works |
| App crashes before commit | Transaction rolls back | Transaction rolls back |

**What NOT to claim:** "The outbox pattern guarantees exactly-once delivery" (it guarantees at-least-once; idempotency makes it effectively-once). "The outbox is a message queue" (it's a database table that acts as a staging area).

---

### 2.3 Clock Skew and Ordering

**Q37: **Answer (full):**

Clock skew is the difference in time between clocks on different machines. In distributed systems, clocks can drift by milliseconds to seconds, causing subtle and hard-to-debug issues.

**What breaks with clock skew:**

```
Scenario 1: TTL-based caching
- Client A: "Cache this for 60 seconds" (clock: 10:00:00)
- Client B: "Read cache" (clock: 10:00:01)
- Client B sees stale data (cache was created 1 second ago, but TTL says 60s)
- Problem: If client B's clock is 2 minutes ahead, cache appears expired

Scenario 2: Token expiration
- Server issues JWT expiring at 10:01:00
- Client clock is 5 minutes ahead: 10:05:00
- Client rejects the token as expired (but server thinks it's valid)

Scenario 3: Event ordering
- Event A: timestamp 10:00:01 (on server 1)
- Event B: timestamp 10:00:00 (on server 2)
- Event B has EARLIER timestamp, but happened AFTER Event A
- This breaks causal ordering
```

**Solutions:**

```
1. NTP synchronization:
   - Keep all clocks synchronized via NTP
   - Typical accuracy: 1-10ms
   - Not sufficient for sub-millisecond ordering

2. Hybrid Logical Clocks (HLC):
   - Combines physical clock with logical counter
   - Preserves causal ordering across machines
   - Used by CockroachDB, YugabyteDB

3. TrueTime (Google Spanner):
   - GPS + atomic clocks
   - Provides uncertainty interval [earliest, latest]
   - Guarantees linearizability across datacenters

4. Application-level solutions:
   - Use database timestamps (not client timestamps)
   - Use monotonic clocks for duration measurements
   - Use UUID v7 (timestamp-ordered) for event ordering
```

**Impact on Docita:**

Docita uses NTP for clock synchronization and database timestamps for all business logic. Event ordering uses UUID v7 (timestamp-ordered) to avoid clock skew issues. Token expiration uses server-side time, not client time.

**30-second answer:** Clock skew breaks TTLs, token expiration, and event ordering. Fix with NTP (basic), Hybrid Logical Clocks (better), or TrueTime (best). Use database timestamps for business logic, not client timestamps. Use UUID v7 for event ordering.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Clock skew breaks: (1) TTL-based cache expiration, (2) token expiry validation, (3) log correlation across services, (4) timeout detection. Solutions: (1) use logical clocks (Lamport timestamps) for ordering, (2) use NTP for rough synchronization, (3) use vector clocks for causal ordering, (4) never depend on wall-clock time for correctness — use monotonic clocks for durations."

---

**Q38: **Answer (full):**

Logical clocks provide a way to establish causality (which event happened before another) without relying on physical time. They solve the problem of clock skew in distributed systems.

**Lamport's Logical Clock:**

```
Rules:
1. Increment counter before each local event
2. Include counter in every message
3. On receive: counter = max(local, received) + 1

Timeline:
Server A: [1] → [2] → [3] ──msg──▶ Server B: [max(3,?) + 1 = 4] → [5]
Server B: [1] → [2] ←──msg── Server A: [max(2,?) + 1 = 3] → [4]

Result: All events have a total order
```

**Limitation:** Lamport clocks can't detect concurrent events. If A and B happen "at the same time" (no message between them), Lamport assigns arbitrary order.

**Vector Clocks:**

```
Vector clock = [A, B, C] (one counter per server)

Rules:
1. Increment your own position before each event
2. Send full vector clock with each message
3. On receive: take element-wise max, then increment own position

Example:
Server A: [1,0,0] → [2,0,0] ──msg──▶ Server B: [2,1,0] → [2,2,0]
Server B: [0,1,0] → [0,2,0] ←──msg── Server C: [0,2,1]

If vector A < vector B (element-wise): A happened before B
If neither A < B nor B < A: A and B are concurrent (no causal relationship)
```

**Impact on Docita:**

Docita uses database sequence numbers (monotonically increasing) for event ordering within a single database. For cross-service events, Kafka provides ordering guarantees via partition keys. Vector clocks are not needed for Docita's current scale.

**30-second answer:** Logical clocks establish causality without physical time. Lamport clocks provide total order but can't detect concurrency. Vector clocks detect concurrent events by tracking per-server counters. Use them when clock skew would break your ordering assumptions.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"A logical clock is a counter that increments on each event, providing a partial ordering without relying on wall-clock time. A vector clock is a map of {node → counter} that tracks the happened-before relationship across all nodes. If VC(a) < VC(b) for all elements, then a happened before b. Vector clocks detect concurrent events (neither happened before the other). Used for conflict detection in distributed databases (Dynamo, Riak)."

---

**Q39: "How does Kafka maintain ordering? What are the limits?"**

**Answer (full):**

Kafka's ordering guarantee is per-partition, not global. Understanding this is critical for designing event-driven systems.

**The guarantee:**

```
Within a partition: ORDERED
  Partition 0: [e1, e2, e3, e4, e5] ← always in this order
  
Across partitions: NOT ORDERED
  Partition 0: [e1, e3, e5]
  Partition 1: [e2, e4, e6]
  
  Consumer reading from both partitions might see: e1, e2, e3, e4, e5, e6
  Or: e2, e1, e4, e3, e6, e5 (no guarantee!)
```

**How to get per-entity ordering:**

```typescript
// Partition by entity ID — all events for one entity go to same partition
await producer.send({
  topic: 'appointments',
  messages: [{
    key: event.patientId,  // Partition key
    value: JSON.stringify(event),
  }],
});
// Kafka hashes patientId to determine partition
// All events for patient_123 go to the same partition
// Within that partition, events are ordered
```

**The trade-off:**

| Partitions | Ordering | Parallelism | Complexity |
|-----------|----------|-------------|-----------|
| 1 | Global | Low (1 consumer) | Low |
| 10 | Per-entity | Medium (10 consumers) | Medium |
| 100 | Per-entity | High (100 consumers) | High |
| 1000 | Per-entity | Very high | Very high |

**When global ordering is needed:**

```
Use case: Audit log (every event must be in chronological order)
Solution: Single partition (limits throughput)
  Topic: audit-log (1 partition)
  Consumer: single consumer (processes in order)
  Throughput: limited to ~10 MB/s
  
Alternative: Application-level ordering
  Consumer reads from multiple partitions
  Sorts events by timestamp before processing
  Trade-off: higher latency (must wait for events to arrive)
```

**What NOT to claim:** "Kafka guarantees global ordering" (it guarantees per-partition ordering only). "More partitions are always better" (more partitions = more overhead; tune for your needs).

---

**Q40: **Answer (full):**

Event ordering across services is one of the hardest problems in distributed systems. Here's how to handle it.

**The problem:**

```
Service A: Creates appointment (event: APPOINTMENT_CREATED)
Service B: Sends notification (event: NOTIFICATION_SENT)
Service C: Processes payment (event: PAYMENT_PROCESSED)

If events arrive out of order:
- NOTIFICATION_SENT before APPOINTMENT_CREATED → notification references non-existent appointment
- PAYMENT_PROCESSED before APPOINTMENT_CREATED → payment has no appointment to link to
```

**Solution 1: Kafka partition keys:**

```
// All events for the same appointment go to the same partition
await kafka.producer.send({
  topic: 'docita-events',
  messages: [
    { key: 'apt_123', value: APPOINTMENT_CREATED },
    { key: 'apt_123', value: NOTIFICATION_SENT },
    { key: 'apt_123', value: PAYMENT_PROCESSED }
  ]
});

// Partition 0: [APT_CREATED, NOTIF_SENT, PAYMENT_PROCESSED]
// Partition 1: [other events]
// Partition 2: [other events]

// Each partition maintains strict ordering
// Consumers process events in partition order
```

**Solution 2: Causal ordering via event IDs:**

```typescript
// Each event references the event that caused it
const appointmentCreated = {
  eventId: 'evt_001',
  causationId: 'evt_001',  // Caused by this event
  correlationId: 'req_abc',  // Original request
  type: 'APPOINTMENT_CREATED'
};

const notificationSent = {
  eventId: 'evt_002',
  causationId: 'evt_001',  // Caused by APPOINTMENT_CREATED
  correlationId: 'req_abc',
  type: 'NOTIFICATION_SENT'
};

// Consumer can check: "Is causationId already processed?"
// If not, buffer the event until causation is processed
```

**Solution 3: Outbox pattern with sequence numbers:**

```sql
-- Each outbox event has a sequence number per aggregate
INSERT INTO outbox (aggregate_id, sequence, event_type, payload)
VALUES ('apt_123', 1, 'APPOINTMENT_CREATED', '...');
INSERT INTO outbox (aggregate_id, sequence, event_type, payload)
VALUES ('apt_123', 2, 'NOTIFICATION_SENT', '...');

-- Consumer processes events in sequence order
-- If sequence N is missing, buffer until it arrives
```

**Impact on Docita:**

Docita uses Kafka partition keys (practitioner_id) to ensure events for the same practitioner are ordered. For appointment-specific ordering, the aggregate_id (appointment_id) is used as the partition key. This guarantees that APPOINTMENT_CREATED is processed before NOTIFICATION_SENT.

**30-second answer:** Use Kafka partition keys to ensure events for the same aggregate are ordered within a partition. Use event IDs (causationId) for causal ordering across partitions. Use sequence numbers per aggregate for gap detection. The key insight: order within a partition is guaranteed; order across partitions is not.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Options: (1) Single partition with one consumer — guarantees ordering but limits throughput. (2) Partition by entity ID — orders per entity, parallel across entities. (3) Causal ordering via vector clocks — detects concurrent events. (4) Application-level ordering — consumer sorts events by timestamp before processing. The trade-off: strict global ordering limits parallelism. Most systems need per-entity ordering, not global ordering."

---

### 2.4 Failure Modes

**Q41: "What is split-brain? How do you prevent it in a PostgreSQL cluster?"**

**Answer (full):**

Split-brain is one of the most dangerous failure modes in distributed databases. It occurs when two nodes both believe they are the primary and accept writes independently, creating divergent data that is extremely difficult to reconcile.

**The problem visualized:**

```
Normal state:
  Primary (Node A) ──replication──▶ Standby (Node B)
  All writes go to A, B is read-only

Network partition:
  Primary (Node A) ──X── Standby (Node B)
  A continues accepting writes
  B promotes itself to primary (automatic failover)
  B also accepts writes
  
  Now: Two primaries, divergent data
  
After partition heals:
  A and B both have writes the other doesn't
  Which data is correct? Neither — you have corruption
```

**Why split-brain is catastrophic for healthcare:**

```
Clinic A books appointment on Node A
Clinic B books same slot on Node B
After partition heals: double booking
  → Patient harm
  → Legal liability
  → Regulatory violation
```

**Prevention mechanisms:**

**1. Fencing (STONITH — Shoot The Other Node In The Head)**

```
When Node B promotes itself:
  1. B sends STONITH signal to A
  2. A is forcefully shut down (power off, fence agent)
  3. B is now the sole primary
  4. When A recovers, it starts as standby (not primary)
```

**2. Quorum / Witness node**

```
3-node cluster:
  Node A (primary)
  Node B (standby)
  Node C (witness — lightweight, no data)

Partition between A and B:
  A checks: "Can I reach the witness?" → Yes (A + C = 2/3 quorum)
  B checks: "Can I reach the witness?" → No (B alone = 1/3)
  A stays primary, B stays standby
  
  Quorum prevents split-brain: only the side with majority can be primary
```

**3. Synchronous replication**

```
Primary waits for standby confirmation before committing:
  1. Client writes to Primary
  2. Primary sends to Standby
  3. Standby confirms receipt
  4. Primary commits
  5. Primary returns success to client

If network partitions:
  Primary cannot get standby confirmation
  Primary CANNOT commit (blocks or fails)
  No divergent data possible
```

**4. Consensus-based leader election (Patroni + etcd)**

```
Patroni: HA solution for PostgreSQL
  - Uses etcd/ZooKeeper as consensus store
  - Leader key with TTL in etcd
  - Leader must renew TTL periodically
  - If leader fails to renew, new leader elected
  
Split-brain prevention:
  - Old leader's etcd key expires
  - New leader acquires key
  - Old leader detects key loss, demotes itself
```

**For Docita:**

| Scale | Prevention Strategy | Cost |
|-------|-------------------|------|
| 25 clinics | Automated RDS failover (AWS handles fencing) | ~$50/month |
| 100 clinics | Multi-AZ RDS (synchronous replication) | ~$500/month |
| 500 clinics | Patroni + etcd (self-managed HA) | ~$2000/month |

**What NOT to claim:** "Split-brain is impossible" (it's prevented, not impossible). "PostgreSQL handles split-brain automatically" (you need external tooling like Patroni or managed services like RDS).

---

**Q42: "Explain circuit breaker pattern. When does it trip? How does it recover? What's the half-open state?"**

**Answer (full):**

The circuit breaker prevents cascading failures by wrapping external calls with a state machine that detects failures and fast-fails when a dependency is unhealthy.

**The three states:**

```
                    Success
    ┌──────────┐  ─────────▶  ┌──────────┐
    │  CLOSED  │              │  CLOSED  │
    │          │  ◀─────────  │          │
    └────┬─────┘   Failure    └──────────┘
         │                    (resets counter)
         │ N failures
         ▼
    ┌──────────┐   Timeout    ┌───────────┐
    │   OPEN   │  ─────────▶  │ HALF-OPEN │
    │          │              │           │
    └──────────┘              └─────┬─────┘
         ▲                          │
         │           Failure        │ Success
         └──────────────────────────┘
```

**State transitions:**

| State | Behavior | Transition |
|-------|----------|-----------|
| **Closed** | Calls pass through normally. Failures are counted. | After N consecutive failures → Open |
| **Open** | All calls fail immediately (fast-fail). No calls reach the dependency. | After timeout → Half-Open |
| **Half-Open** | One test call is allowed through. | Success → Closed; Failure → Open |

**Implementation:**

```typescript
class CircuitBreaker {
  private state: 'closed' | 'open' | 'half-open' = 'closed';
  private failureCount = 0;
  private lastFailureTime = 0;
  private readonly failureThreshold = 5;
  private readonly resetTimeout = 30000; // 30 seconds

  async call<T>(fn: () => Promise<T>): Promise<T> {
    if (this.state === 'open') {
      if (Date.now() - this.lastFailureTime > this.resetTimeout) {
        this.state = 'half-open';
      } else {
        throw new CircuitOpenError('Circuit is open — dependency is unhealthy');
      }
    }

    try {
      const result = await fn();
      
      // Success
      if (this.state === 'half-open') {
        this.state = 'closed'; // Recovery confirmed
        this.failureCount = 0;
      }
      this.failureCount = 0;
      return result;
      
    } catch (error) {
      this.failureCount++;
      this.lastFailureTime = Date.now();
      
      if (this.failureCount >= this.failureThreshold) {
        this.state = 'open'; // Trip the circuit
      }
      
      throw error;
    }
  }
}

// Usage in Docita
const sendGridBreaker = new CircuitBreaker();

async function sendEmail(to: string, template: string) {
  try {
    return await sendGridBreaker.call(() => 
      sendGrid.send({ to, template })
    );
  } catch (error) {
    if (error instanceof CircuitOpenError) {
      // Don't even try — SendGrid is down
      // Queue the email for later processing
      await queueEmailForLater(to, template);
      return { queued: true };
    }
    throw error;
  }
}
```

**Why circuit breaker matters for Docita:**

Without a circuit breaker, if SendGrid is down:
1. Every notification request waits for SendGrid's timeout (30 seconds)
2. Worker threads are blocked waiting
3. Worker can't process other events
4. Outbox events accumulate
5. Eventually the worker crashes or becomes unresponsive

With a circuit breaker:
1. After 5 failed SendGrid calls, circuit opens
2. Subsequent calls fail immediately (milliseconds, not 30 seconds)
3. Worker continues processing other events
4. After 30 seconds, one test call is made
5. If SendGrid is back, circuit closes; if not, circuit stays open

**Monitoring the circuit breaker:**

```typescript
// Expose circuit breaker state as a metric
prometheus.register({
  name: 'circuit_breaker_state',
  help: 'Circuit breaker state (0=closed, 1=open, 2=half-open)',
  labelNames: ['dependency'],
  collect() {
    this.set({ dependency: 'sendgrid' }, 
      sendGridBreaker.state === 'closed' ? 0 : 
      sendGridBreaker.state === 'open' ? 1 : 2
    );
  }
});
```

**What NOT to claim:** "Circuit breaker prevents all failures" (it prevents cascading failures, not individual failures). "The circuit breaker retries automatically" (retries are a separate concern; the circuit breaker decides whether to attempt the call at all).

---

**Q43: "What is graceful degradation? How would Docita degrade if the notification service is down?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Graceful degradation: the system continues to function with reduced capabilities. If the notification service is down: (1) appointment booking still works (notification is queued, not blocking), (2) in-app notifications still work (they're in the same database), (3) email/SMS notifications are delayed (outbox events accumulate, processed when the service recovers), (4) the dashboard shows a 'notifications temporarily unavailable' banner. The key: the core workflow (booking) is never blocked by a non-critical dependency."

---

**Q44: "How do you handle partial failures in a microservices architecture?"**

**Answer (full):**

Partial failures are the defining characteristic of distributed systems. One service is down, others are up. The question is: how does the system behave?

**The reality:**

```
Microservices architecture:
  ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐
  │   API    │────▶│   DB     │     │ SendGrid │
  │  (up)    │     │  (up)    │     │  (DOWN!) │
  └──────────┘     └──────────┘     └──────────┘
       │
       └────────▶ ┌──────────┐
                  │  Redis   │
                  │  (up)    │
                  └──────────┘

User books appointment:
  1. API → DB: INSERT appointment ✅
  2. API → SendGrid: Send email ❌ (DOWN!)
  
  Without handling: API hangs for 30 seconds, then crashes
  With handling: API returns "email will be sent shortly"
```

**The 5 patterns for partial failure:**

**Pattern 1: Circuit Breaker (fast-fail)**

```typescript
// Don't wait for a service that's probably down
const sendGridBreaker = new CircuitBreaker({
  failureThreshold: 5,      // Trip after 5 failures
  resetTimeout: 30000,       // Try again after 30 seconds
});

try {
  await sendGridBreaker.call(() => sendGrid.send(email));
} catch (error) {
  if (error instanceof CircuitOpenError) {
    // Don't even try — queue for later
    await queueEmailForLater(email);
  }
}
```

**Pattern 2: Fallback (degraded response)**

```typescript
// Return cached data or a default when service is down
async function getPatientWithFallback(patientId: string) {
  try {
    const patient = await patientService.get(patientId);
    await cache.set(`patient:${patientId}`, patient, { ttl: 300 });
    return patient;
  } catch (error) {
    // Service is down — try cache
    const cached = await cache.get(`patient:${patientId}`);
    if (cached) return cached; // Stale but available
    
    // No cache either — return minimal data
    return { id: patientId, name: 'Patient data temporarily unavailable' };
  }
}
```

**Pattern 3: Timeout (never wait forever)**

```typescript
// Always set a timeout on external calls
const response = await fetch('https://api.sendgrid.com/v3/mail/send', {
  signal: AbortSignal.timeout(5000), // 5 second timeout
});

// Without timeout: hangs indefinitely
// With timeout: fails fast, allows retry or fallback
```

**Pattern 4: Retry with backoff (transient failures)**

```typescript
// Retry transient failures with exponential backoff
async function sendWithRetry(fn: () => Promise<any>, maxRetries = 3) {
  for (let i = 0; i < maxRetries; i++) {
    try {
      return await fn();
    } catch (error) {
      if (i === maxRetries - 1) throw error; // Final attempt failed
      
      const delay = Math.min(1000 * Math.pow(2, i), 30000); // 1s, 2s, 4s, max 30s
      const jitter = Math.random() * 1000; // Add jitter to prevent thundering herd
      await sleep(delay + jitter);
    }
  }
}
```

**Pattern 5: Bulkhead (isolate failures)**

```typescript
// Separate connection pools per service
const notificationPool = new Pool({ max: 10 }); // SendGrid connections
const paymentPool = new Pool({ max: 10 });      // Stripe connections
const dbPool = new Pool({ max: 20 });           // PostgreSQL connections

// If SendGrid is down, only the notification pool is affected
// Payment and DB pools continue working
```

**For Docita:**

| Dependency | Failure Mode | Handling |
|-----------|-------------|----------|
| **PostgreSQL** | Connection exhausted | PgBouncer + connection pool limits |
| **SendGrid** | API down | Circuit breaker + queue for later |
| **Twilio** | API down | Circuit breaker + queue for later |
| **Redis** | Connection lost | Cache-aside falls back to DB |
| **Stripe** | API down | Retry with backoff + queue payment |

**What NOT to claim:** "Partial failure is rare" (it's the norm in distributed systems). "Circuit breaker prevents all failures" (it prevents cascading failures, not individual failures).

---

**Q45: "What is the bulkhead pattern? How does it prevent cascading failures?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Bulkhead isolates components so a failure in one doesn't bring down the whole system. Implementation: (1) separate connection pools per dependency (notification provider gets 10 connections, payment provider gets 10), (2) separate thread pools per service, (3) separate rate limits per tenant. If the notification pool is exhausted, payment still works. The metaphor: ship bulkheads prevent one hull breach from flooding the entire ship."

---

## Section 3: Caching Deep-Dive (15 questions)

### 3.1 Cache Patterns

**Q46: "Explain cache-aside vs read-through vs write-through vs write-behind. Which does Docita use and why?"**

**Answer (full):**

Caching patterns define who controls the cache: the application, the cache itself, or a combination. Choosing the wrong pattern leads to stale data, cache stampedes, or data inconsistency.

**The four patterns:**

```
CACHE-ASIDE (Lazy Loading):
  App → Cache (miss?) → DB → App writes to cache
  App controls everything: what to cache, when to invalidate, TTL

READ-THROUGH:
  App → Cache → Cache reads DB on miss → Cache returns to App
  App only talks to cache; cache handles DB fallback

WRITE-THROUGH:
  App → Cache + DB simultaneously (sync)
  Cache is always in sync with DB

WRITE-BEHIND (Write-Back):
  App → Cache (async flush to DB)
  Fast writes, eventual consistency with DB
```

**Detailed comparison:**

| Pattern | Read Path | Write Path | Consistency | Complexity | Use Case |
|---------|-----------|-----------|-------------|------------|----------|
| **Cache-aside** | App checks cache, misses → reads DB → populates cache | App writes DB → invalidates cache | Eventual (TTL) | Medium | Most web apps |
| **Read-through** | App reads cache only; cache handles DB | App writes DB → invalidates cache | Eventual (TTL) | Low | Simple read-heavy |
| **Write-through** | App reads cache | App writes cache + DB (sync) | Strong | High | Financial data |
| **Write-behind** | App reads cache | App writes cache; DB updated async | Eventual | High | Write-heavy, can tolerate delay |

**Why Docita uses cache-aside:**

```typescript
// Cache-aside implementation for practitioner schedules
async function getSchedule(practitionerId: string, clinicId: string) {
  const cacheKey = `cache:clinic:${clinicId}:schedule:${practitionerId}`;
  
  // Step 1: Check cache
  const cached = await redis.get(cacheKey);
  if (cached) {
    return JSON.parse(cached); // Cache HIT — no DB query
  }
  
  // Step 2: Cache MISS — read from DB
  const schedule = await prisma.practitionerSchedule.findMany({
    where: { practitionerId, clinicId }
  });
  
  // Step 3: Populate cache with TTL
  await redis.setex(cacheKey, 30, JSON.stringify(schedule)); // 30s TTL
  
  return schedule;
}

// Invalidation on write
async function updateSchedule(practitionerId: string, clinicId: string, data: any) {
  await prisma.practitionerSchedule.update({ where: { id: practitionerId }, data });
  
  // Invalidate cache — next read will repopulate
  const cacheKey = `cache:clinic:${clinicId}:schedule:${practitionerId}`;
  await redis.del(cacheKey);
}
```

**Why cache-aside over the others:**

1. **Control:** The app decides exactly what to cache and when to invalidate. With read-through, the cache decides — less flexible.
2. **Simplicity:** Write-through requires the cache to write to the DB, which adds coupling. Cache-aside keeps the DB as the single source of truth.
3. **Failure modes:** If Redis is down, cache-aside falls back to DB (slower but correct). Write-through would fail because the cache can't write to DB.
4. **Healthcare fit:** Patient data must never be cached (PHI sensitivity). Cache-aside lets you explicitly skip caching for sensitive data.

**When you WOULD use other patterns:**

- **Write-through:** If you need strong consistency (e.g., financial transactions where the cache must always reflect the DB). The trade-off: every write hits both cache and DB, increasing write latency.
- **Write-behind:** If you have extremely high write throughput and can tolerate eventual consistency (e.g., analytics counters). The trade-off: if the cache crashes before flushing, you lose recent writes.
- **Read-through:** If you want simpler code and the cache handles DB fallback. The trade-off: less control over TTL and invalidation.

**What NOT to claim:** "Cache-aside is always the best pattern" (it depends on consistency requirements). "Docita caches patient data" (patient data is never cached due to PHI sensitivity).

---

**Q47: "What is cache stampede? How do you prevent it when a popular clinic's schedule is accessed?"**

**Answer (full):**

Cache stampede (also called "thundering herd" on cache miss) is one of the most common failure modes in caching systems. It happens when a popular cache key expires and many concurrent requests all try to repopulate it simultaneously.

**The problem visualized:**

```
Time 0:     Cache key "clinic:123:schedule" expires
            Redis returns MISS

Time 0.001: Request 1 → cache miss → hits DB
Time 0.001: Request 2 → cache miss → hits DB
Time 0.001: Request 3 → cache miss → hits DB
Time 0.002: Request 4 → cache miss → hits DB
...
Time 0.005: Request 50 → cache miss → hits DB

Result: 50 concurrent DB queries for the same data
        DB connection pool exhausted
        Other queries (bookings, patient lookups) fail
```

**Prevention strategies:**

**Strategy 1: Mutex / Single-Flight (one request repopulates)**

```typescript
import { Mutex } from 'async-mutex';

const scheduleMutex = new Mutex();

async function getSchedule(practitionerId: string, clinicId: string) {
  const cacheKey = `cache:clinic:${clinicId}:schedule:${practitionerId}`;
  
  // Check cache first
  const cached = await redis.get(cacheKey);
  if (cached) return JSON.parse(cached);
  
  // Only one request repopulates; others wait
  const release = await scheduleMutex.acquire();
  try {
    // Double-check: another request may have populated while we waited
    const cachedAgain = await redis.get(cacheKey);
    if (cachedAgain) return JSON.parse(cachedAgain);
    
    // Populate from DB
    const schedule = await prisma.practitionerSchedule.findMany({
      where: { practitionerId, clinicId }
    });
    await redis.setex(cacheKey, 30, JSON.stringify(schedule));
    return schedule;
  } finally {
    release();
  }
}
```

**Strategy 2: Stale-While-Revalidate (return stale, refresh in background)**

```typescript
async function getScheduleSWR(practitionerId: string, clinicId: string) {
  const cacheKey = `cache:clinic:${clinicId}:schedule:${practitionerId}`;
  
  const cached = await redis.get(cacheKey);
  if (cached) {
    const data = JSON.parse(cached);
    
    // Check if stale (e.g., older than 25s of 30s TTL)
    if (data.cachedAt > Date.now() - 25000) {
      return data.schedule; // Fresh — return immediately
    }
    
    // Stale — return stale data AND refresh in background
    refreshScheduleInBackground(practitionerId, clinicId);
    return data.schedule;
  }
  
  // No cache at all — must wait for DB
  return await refreshSchedule(practitionerId, clinicId);
}

async function refreshScheduleInBackground(practitionerId: string, clinicId: string) {
  // Non-blocking background refresh
  setImmediate(async () => {
    const schedule = await prisma.practitionerSchedule.findMany({
      where: { practitionerId, clinicId }
    });
    await redis.setex(
      `cache:clinic:${clinicId}:schedule:${practitionerId}`,
      30,
      JSON.stringify({ schedule, cachedAt: Date.now() })
    );
  });
}
```

**Strategy 3: Probabilistic Early Expiration**

```typescript
async function getScheduleProbabilistic(practitionerId: string, clinicId: string) {
  const cacheKey = `cache:clinic:${clinicId}:schedule:${practitionerId}`;
  const ttl = 30; // seconds
  
  const cached = await redis.get(cacheKey);
  const ttlRemaining = await redis.ttl(cacheKey);
  
  if (cached) {
    // Probabilistically refresh before expiry
    // Higher load = higher chance of early refresh
    const refreshProbability = 1 - (ttlRemaining / ttl);
    
    if (Math.random() < refreshProbability) {
      // This request refreshes; others still get cached data
      refreshScheduleInBackground(practitionerId, clinicId);
    }
    
    return JSON.parse(cached);
  }
  
  return await refreshSchedule(practitionerId, clinicId);
}
```

**Strategy comparison:**

| Strategy | Complexity | DB Load | User Latency | Best For |
|----------|-----------|---------|-------------|----------|
| **Mutex** | Low | Minimal (1 query) | Slightly higher (wait) | Low-traffic keys |
| **Stale-while-revalidate** | Medium | Background refresh | Zero (stale data) | High-traffic keys |
| **Probabilistic** | Medium | Distributed refresh | Zero (cached) | Very high-traffic keys |

**For Docita:** Stale-while-revalidate is the best fit. Clinicians viewing a schedule can tolerate 5 seconds of staleness (the schedule doesn't change that fast). The background refresh ensures the cache is always warm for the next request.

**What NOT to claim:** "We use Redis distributed locks for cache stampede" (too heavy for this use case). "Cache stampede only happens with TTL-based expiration" (it also happens with explicit invalidation under high traffic).

---

**Q48: "What is the Thundering Herd problem? How does it relate to cache expiration?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Thundering Herd: when a resource becomes available (e.g., a cache key is repopulated), all waiting requests wake up simultaneously and compete for the resource. Similar to cache stampede but broader. Prevention: (1) jitter — add random delay to cache TTLs so keys don't expire simultaneously, (2) single-flight — deduplicate concurrent requests for the same key, (3) request coalescing — merge multiple requests into one DB query."

---

**Q49: "How do you handle cache invalidation in a multi-tenant system? What's the key strategy?"**

**Answer (full):**

Cache invalidation in a multi-tenant system has an additional dimension: you must invalidate per-tenant, not globally. Invalidating a global cache key would evict data for all clinics.

**The key principle: tenant-scoped cache keys**

```typescript
// ❌ Bad: global cache key (invalidates all clinics' data)
const cacheKey = `cache:schedule:${practitionerId}`;

// ✅ Good: tenant-scoped cache key
const cacheKey = `cache:clinic:${clinicId}:schedule:${practitionerId}`;
```

**Invalidation strategies:**

**Strategy 1: TTL-based (accept staleness)**

```typescript
// Set TTL, let the cache expire naturally
await redis.setex(cacheKey, 30, JSON.stringify(schedule));
// After 30 seconds, the cache expires and is repopulated on next read

// Pros: Simple, no invalidation logic needed
// Cons: Up to 30 seconds of staleness
// Best for: Schedule data (changes infrequently)
```

**Strategy 2: Write-through invalidation**

```typescript
// Invalidate on every write
async function updateSchedule(practitionerId: string, clinicId: string, data: any) {
  await prisma.schedule.update({ where: { id: practitionerId }, data });
  
  // Invalidate immediately
  const cacheKey = `cache:clinic:${clinicId}:schedule:${practitionerId}`;
  await redis.del(cacheKey);
}

// Pros: Cache is always fresh after a write
// Cons: May over-invalidate (if write doesn't affect cached data)
// Best for: Patient data (must be fresh)
```

**Strategy 3: Event-driven invalidation**

```typescript
// Outbox event triggers cache invalidation
async function handleScheduleUpdated(event: ScheduleEvent) {
  const cacheKey = `cache:clinic:${event.clinicId}:schedule:${event.practitionerId}`;
  await redis.del(cacheKey);
}

// Pros: Precise invalidation (only invalidates when data actually changes)
// Cons: Eventual consistency (invalidation happens after event processing)
// Best for: Cross-service cache invalidation
```

**Strategy comparison:**

| Strategy | Freshness | Complexity | Over-Invalidation | Best For |
|----------|-----------|-----------|-------------------|----------|
| **TTL** | Up to TTL delay | Low | None | Read-heavy, stale-tolerant |
| **Write-through** | Immediate | Low | Possible | Write-heavy, fresh data |
| **Event-driven** | Eventual | Medium | None | Cross-service, precise |

**For Docita:**

| Data Type | Strategy | TTL | Why |
|-----------|----------|-----|-----|
| Practitioner schedules | TTL (30s) | 30 seconds | Changes infrequently, stale is OK |
| Clinic configuration | TTL (5min) | 5 minutes | Rarely changes |
| Patient data | No cache | N/A | PHI sensitivity, must be fresh |
| Appointment status | No cache | N/A | Must be real-time |

**The anti-pattern: global invalidation**

```typescript
// ❌ Bad: invalidating all clinics' caches when one clinic changes
await redis.del('cache:schedule:*'); // Flushes ALL schedules

// ✅ Good: invalidate only the affected clinic's cache
await redis.del(`cache:clinic:${clinicId}:schedule:${practitionerId}`);
```

**What NOT to claim:** "We use cache invalidation for patient data" (patient data is never cached). "TTL is always sufficient" (some data must be fresh immediately after write).

---

**Q50: "What is cache warming? When would you proactively populate the cache?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Cache warming: proactively populate the cache before it's needed. Use cases: (1) after a deploy — warm the cache with the most popular clinic schedules, (2) after a cache flush — pre-populate known hot keys, (3) for predictable traffic — warm the cache before morning clinic hours. The trade-off: warming uses DB resources proactively; if you guess wrong, you waste resources on cold data."

---

### 3.2 Cache Architecture

**Q51: "How would you design a caching layer for Docita? What to cache, what not to cache?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Cache: (1) practitioner schedules (read-heavy, changes infrequently), (2) clinic configuration (rarely changes), (3) permission/role lookups (changes on admin action). Don't cache: (1) patient records (PHI sensitivity, freshness requirement), (2) appointment status (must be real-time), (3) audit logs (write-heavy, append-only). Key strategy: `cache:{clinicId}:{entity}:{id}`. TTL: 30s for schedules, 5min for config, 0 for patient data."

---

**Q52: "What is CDN edge caching? How does it work for a healthcare SPA?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"CDN caches static assets (JS, CSS, images) at edge nodes close to users. For Docita's Vite SPA: the HTML shell, JS bundles, and CSS are cacheable (fingerprinted filenames). API responses are not CDN-cached (they're tenant-specific and dynamic). Trade-off: CDN reduces latency for asset delivery but doesn't help with API response time. Healthcare consideration: never cache API responses containing PHI at the CDN edge."

---

**Q53: "How do you handle cache consistency across multiple services?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Options: (1) distributed cache (Redis) — single source of truth, all services read/write the same cache, (2) per-service cache with invalidation events — each service has its own cache, invalidation is propagated via events, (3) read-through with TTL — accept eventual consistency. For Docita: shared Redis cache for schedule data (read by API and worker), per-service cache for service-specific data. Invalidation: event-driven for schedule changes, TTL for everything else."

---

**Q54: "What is the N+1 caching problem? How does it differ from the N+1 query problem?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"N+1 query: one query for the list, then N queries for each item's details. N+1 caching: one cache miss triggers N cache misses for related data. Example: cache miss for clinic schedule → miss for each practitioner's schedule → miss for each appointment. Prevention: batch cache lookups (mget in Redis), or cache the aggregated result (clinic schedule with all practitioners pre-included)."

---

**Q55: "How do you choose a TTL? What are the trade-offs for patient data vs schedule data?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"TTL = freshness requirement / acceptable staleness. Patient data: TTL = 0 (never cache, must be real-time). Schedule data: TTL = 30s (clinician can tolerate 30s stale availability). Clinic config: TTL = 5min (changes rarely, staleness acceptable). The trade-off: shorter TTL = more DB hits, longer TTL = more staleness. Monitor cache hit rate: if >95%, TTL is probably too short; if <80%, TTL might be too long."

---

### 3.3 Cache Failure Modes

**Q56: "What happens when Redis goes down? How does Docita degrade?"**

**Answer (full):**

Redis failure should be transparent to users — the system continues to function, just slower.

**The degradation path:**

```
Redis UP (normal):
  Request → Redis (0.5ms) → Cache HIT → Response (1ms)
  Request → Redis (0.5ms) → Cache MISS → DB (10ms) → Response (11ms)

Redis DOWN:
  Request → Redis (timeout 100ms) → FALL THROUGH → DB (10ms) → Response (110ms)
  
  Every request hits the database
  Latency increases 10x
  But the system still works
```

**Implementation:**

```typescript
// Cache-aside with Redis failure handling
async function getSchedule(practitionerId: string, clinicId: string) {
  const cacheKey = `cache:clinic:${clinicId}:schedule:${practitionerId}`;
  
  try {
    // Try Redis first
    const cached = await redis.get(cacheKey);
    if (cached) return JSON.parse(cached);
  } catch (error) {
    // Redis is down — log and continue
    console.warn('Redis unavailable, falling back to DB', { error: error.message });
  }
  
  // Cache miss or Redis down — read from DB
  const schedule = await prisma.schedule.findMany({
    where: { practitionerId, clinicId }
  });
  
  // Try to populate cache (will fail if Redis is down, that's OK)
  try {
    await redis.setex(cacheKey, 30, JSON.stringify(schedule));
  } catch (error) {
    // Redis write failed — skip caching
  }
  
  return schedule;
}
```

**Monitoring:**

```typescript
// Circuit breaker on Redis
const redisBreaker = new CircuitBreaker({
  failureThreshold: 5,
  resetTimeout: 30000,
});

// Expose Redis health as a metric
prometheus.register({
  name: 'redis_available',
  help: 'Redis availability (1=up, 0=down)',
  collect() {
    this.set(redisBreaker.isOpen() ? 0 : 1);
  }
});
```

**What NOT to claim:** "Redis failure causes downtime" (it causes degradation, not downtime). "We have Redis Sentinel" (describe what you WOULD do at scale).

---

**Q57: "How do you handle a cache that's returning stale data?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Stale data scenarios: (1) TTL too long — reduce TTL, (2) invalidation missed — add invalidation event, (3) race condition between write and invalidation — use cache-aside with versioned keys. Detection: (1) compare cache value with DB value periodically, (2) track cache hit/miss ratio, (3) user reports of inconsistent data. Fix: flush the specific cache key and reduce TTL."

---

**Q58: "What is a cache avalanche? How do you prevent it?"**

**Answer (full):**

Cache avalanche occurs when a large number of cache keys expire at the same time, causing a thundering herd of requests to the database.

**The problem:**

```
Scenario: Cache warmed at 9:00 AM with 30-minute TTL
  100 clinic schedules cached at 9:00 AM
  All expire at 9:30 AM

At 9:30 AM:
  9:30:00 - Cache key "clinic:1:schedule" expires
  9:30:00 - Cache key "clinic:2:schedule" expires
  9:30:00 - Cache key "clinic:3:schedule" expires
  ...
  9:30:00 - Cache key "clinic:100:schedule" expires

  100 concurrent requests all miss cache
  100 concurrent DB queries
  DB connection pool exhausted
  Other queries (bookings, patient lookups) fail
```

**Visual:**

```
Cache TTL timeline (all expire at same time):
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  9:00 AM                                    9:30 AM
  [All keys populated with 30min TTL]
                                              ↓
                                         ALL EXPIRE
                                              ↓
                                         100 DB queries
                                         (avalanche!)
```

**Prevention strategies:**

**Strategy 1: TTL Jitter (add randomness)**

```typescript
// ❌ Bad: all keys expire at the same time
await redis.setex(key, 1800, data); // Exactly 30 minutes

// ✅ Good: add random jitter (30min ± 5min)
const baseTTL = 1800; // 30 minutes
const jitter = Math.floor(Math.random() * 600); // 0-10 minutes
await redis.setex(key, baseTTL + jitter, data);
// Keys expire at different times: 30-40 minutes
```

**Strategy 2: Staggered expiration**

```typescript
// ❌ Bad: warm all keys at the same time
for (const clinic of clinics) {
  await warmCache(clinic.id); // All expire at the same time
}

// ✅ Good: warm keys with offsets
for (let i = 0; i < clinics.length; i++) {
  const offset = i * 10; // 10 second offset per clinic
  setTimeout(() => warmCache(clinic[i].id), offset * 1000);
}
```

**Strategy 3: Background refresh (proactive)**

```typescript
// Refresh before expiration
async function refreshBeforeExpiration(key: string, ttl: number) {
  const refreshAt = ttl * 0.8; // Refresh at 80% of TTL
  
  setInterval(async () => {
    const data = await fetchFromDB();
    const jitter = Math.floor(Math.random() * 300); // 0-5 min jitter
    await redis.setex(key, ttl + jitter, data);
  }, refreshAt * 1000);
}
```

**Strategy 4: Circuit breaker on cache miss**

```typescript
async function getSchedulesWithCircuitBreaker(clinicId: string) {
  const cached = await redis.get(`clinic:${clinicId}:schedule`);
  if (cached) return JSON.parse(cached);
  
  // Cache miss — check circuit breaker
  if (dbCircuitBreaker.isOpen()) {
    // DB is overloaded — return stale data if available
    const stale = await redis.get(`clinic:${clinicId}:schedule:stale`);
    if (stale) return JSON.parse(stale);
    throw new ServiceUnavailable('Schedule data temporarily unavailable');
  }
  
  // Circuit closed — safe to query DB
  const data = await prisma.schedule.findMany({ where: { clinicId } });
  await redis.setex(`clinic:${clinicId}:schedule`, 1800, JSON.stringify(data));
  return data;
}
```

**What NOT to claim:** "Cache avalanche only happens with TTL-based expiration" (it also happens with explicit invalidation under high traffic). "Jitter eliminates the problem" (it reduces it, but doesn't eliminate it — background refresh is more reliable).

---

**Q59: "How do you test caching behavior? What are the failure modes in tests?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Test: (1) cache hit — assert DB is not queried, (2) cache miss — assert DB is queried and cache is populated, (3) cache invalidation — assert cache is cleared after write, (4) cache failure — assert DB fallback works, (5) stale data — assert TTL is respected. Failure modes: (1) tests pass with caching disabled but fail with caching enabled, (2) tests don't account for cache propagation delay, (3) tests mock Redis incorrectly."

---

**Q60: "What is the cache-aside race condition? How do you handle two concurrent writes?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Race condition: Thread A writes to DB, Thread B writes to DB, Thread A invalidates cache, Thread B invalidates cache, Thread A reads cache (miss) and repopulates with old data, Thread B reads cache (hit) and gets stale data from Thread A. Fix: (1) write-through — write to cache and DB simultaneously, (2) versioned cache keys — include a version number, (3) short TTL — accept brief staleness, (4) single-flight — serialize cache repopulation."

---

## Section 4: Message Queue Internals (15 questions)

### 4.1 Kafka Internals

**Q61: "Explain Kafka partitions. How does partitioning affect ordering and throughput?"**

**Answer (full):**

Kafka partitions are the fundamental unit of parallelism and ordering in a Kafka topic. Understanding partitions is essential for designing event-driven systems.

**What a partition is:**

```
Topic: appointments (3 partitions)

Partition 0: [event_1, event_4, event_7, event_10]  ← ordered, immutable
Partition 1: [event_2, event_5, event_8, event_11]  ← ordered, immutable
Partition 2: [event_3, event_6, event_9, event_12]  ← ordered, immutable
```

Each partition is:
- An ordered, immutable sequence of records (append-only log)
- Stored on a single broker (not distributed across brokers)
- Replicated across brokers for fault tolerance
- Identified by an offset (0, 1, 2, 3, ...)

**Ordering guarantees:**

| Scope | Ordering Guaranteed? | Why |
|-------|---------------------|-----|
| Within a partition | ✅ Yes | Events are appended in order |
| Across partitions | ❌ No | Partitions are independent; no global order |
| Per entity (if partitioned by entity) | ✅ Yes | All events for one entity go to the same partition |

**How partitioning affects throughput:**

```
1 partition:
  - 1 producer writes sequentially
  - 1 consumer reads sequentially
  - Max throughput: ~10 MB/s

3 partitions:
  - 3 producers write in parallel
  - 3 consumers read in parallel
  - Max throughput: ~30 MB/s (3x)

100 partitions:
  - 100 producers write in parallel
  - 100 consumers read in parallel
  - Max throughput: ~1 GB/s (but overhead increases)
```

**The trade-off:**

| Partitions | Throughput | Ordering | Overhead | Best For |
|-----------|-----------|----------|---------|----------|
| 1 | Low | Global | Minimal | Simple event streams |
| 10-100 | High | Per-entity | Moderate | Most applications |
| 100-1000 | Very high | Per-entity | High | High-throughput systems |
| 1000+ | Maximum | Per-entity | Very high | Specialized use cases |

**For Docita:**

```typescript
// Partition by clinic_id for clinic-specific events
// All events for one clinic go to the same partition
// This ensures ordering per clinic (not globally)

const producer = kafka.producer();
await producer.send({
  topic: 'appointments',
  messages: [
    {
      key: event.clinicId,  // Partition key
      value: JSON.stringify(event),
    }
  ],
});
// Kafka hashes clinicId to determine which partition receives the event
// All events for clinic_abc go to the same partition
// Within that partition, events are ordered
```

**Why per-entity ordering is sufficient for Docita:**

Docita doesn't need global ordering (appointment_1 before appointment_2 across all clinics). It needs per-clinic ordering (appointment_1 before appointment_2 within the same clinic). This is achieved by partitioning by `clinicId`.

**What NOT to claim:** "Kafka guarantees global ordering" (it guarantees per-partition ordering only). "More partitions are always better" (more partitions = more overhead; tune for your throughput needs).

---

**Q62: "What are consumer groups? How does rebalancing work?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"A consumer group is a set of consumers that jointly consume a topic. Each partition is assigned to exactly one consumer in the group. Rebalancing: when a consumer joins or leaves, partitions are redistributed. During rebalance, consumption pauses briefly. Rebalance triggers: (1) consumer joins, (2) consumer leaves (crash or graceful), (3) new partitions added. Minimize rebalance frequency: use static membership, longer session timeouts."

---

**Q63: "Explain offset management. What happens when a consumer crashes mid-processing?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Each consumer tracks its position (offset) in each partition. Offsets are committed periodically (not after every message). If a consumer crashes: uncommitted messages are reprocessed by the new consumer. This means at-least-once delivery. For exactly-once: commit offsets transactionally with the business effect (Kafka transactions). The trade-off: more frequent commits = less reprocessing on crash but more overhead."

---

**Q64: "What is exactly-once semantics in Kafka? How is it achieved?"**

**Answer (full):**

Exactly-once semantics (EOS) means each message is processed exactly once, even in the presence of retries, network failures, and consumer crashes. This is one of the hardest problems in distributed systems.

**The three delivery semantics:**

| Semantics | Guarantee | Failure Mode | Use Case |
|-----------|-----------|-------------|----------|
| **At-most-once** | Process 0 or 1 times | May lose messages | Metrics, logs |
| **At-least-once** | Process 1 or more times | May process duplicates | Most applications |
| **Exactly-once** | Process exactly 1 time | No loss, no duplicates | Financial, healthcare |

**How Kafka achieves exactly-once:**

**Component 1: Idempotent Producer**

```typescript
// Idempotent producer assigns sequence numbers to each partition
const producer = kafka.producer({
  idempotent: true,  // Enable idempotency
  transactionalId: 'appointment-producer',  // Required for transactions
});

// If the producer retries (network failure), Kafka detects the duplicate
// via sequence numbers and discards it
await producer.send({
  topic: 'appointments',
  messages: [{ key: clinicId, value: JSON.stringify(event) }],
});
```

**Component 2: Transactional Producer**

```typescript
// Atomic writes across multiple partitions
const transaction = await producer.transaction();
try {
  await transaction.send({
    topic: 'appointments',
    messages: [{ key: clinicId, value: JSON.stringify(appointment) }],
  });
  
  await transaction.send({
    topic: 'notifications',
    messages: [{ key: clinicId, value: JSON.stringify(notification) }],
  });
  
  // Both writes succeed or both fail — no partial writes
  await transaction.commit();
} catch (error) {
  await transaction.abort();
}
```

**Component 3: Consumer Offset Commit**

```typescript
// Commit offsets transactionally with processing
const consumer = kafka.consumer({ groupId: 'notification-worker' });

await consumer.subscribe({ topic: 'appointments' });

await consumer.run({
  eachMessage: async ({ message }) => {
    // Process the message
    await sendEmail(JSON.parse(message.value));
    
    // The offset is committed transactionally
    // If the consumer crashes after processing but before committing,
    // the message is reprocessed (at-least-once)
    // With transactions, the processing and offset commit are atomic
  },
});
```

**The practical reality: at-least-once + idempotent consumers**

In practice, most systems don't use Kafka's full EOS. Instead:

```typescript
// At-least-once delivery + idempotent consumer = effectively-once
async function handleAppointmentCreated(event: AppointmentEvent) {
  // Idempotency check
  const existing = await db.processedEvent.findUnique({
    where: { eventId: event.id }
  });
  if (existing) return; // Already processed — skip
  
  // Process
  await sendEmail(event.patientEmail);
  
  // Mark as processed
  await db.processedEvent.create({
    data: { eventId: event.id }
  });
}
```

**Why at-least-once + idempotent is preferred:**

| Approach | Complexity | Latency | Reliability |
|----------|-----------|---------|------------|
| **At-least-once + idempotent** | Low | Low | High (effectively-once) |
| **Kafka EOS** | High | Higher (transactional overhead) | Very high (exactly-once) |

For Docita: at-least-once + idempotent consumers is sufficient. The notification email is idempotent (sending the same email twice has the same effect as sending it once). The overhead of Kafka EOS is not justified.

**What NOT to claim:** "Kafka provides exactly-once by default" (you must enable idempotent producer and transactions). "Exactly-once means no retries" (it means no duplicates, even with retries).

---

**Q65: "How does schema evolution work with Avro/Protobuf?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Avro/Protobuf schemas are versioned. Schema evolution rules: (1) add optional fields with defaults (backward compatible), (2) never remove required fields, (3) never change field types. Schema registry stores schemas and enforces compatibility. Producers write with the latest schema; consumers read with their own schema version. The registry handles the translation. Trade-off: schema evolution adds operational complexity but prevents breaking changes."

---

**Q66: "What is backpressure in a message queue? How do you implement it?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Backpressure: the consumer is slower than the producer. Implementation: (1) consumer pulls messages at its own pace (Kafka default), (2) rate limiting on the producer, (3) bounded queue with overflow policy (reject oldest, drop, block), (4) adaptive batch size — consumer processes larger batches when behind. Kafka handles backpressure naturally via pull-based consumption: the consumer requests messages when it's ready."

---

**Q67: "How do you handle message ordering guarantees across partitions?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"You can't guarantee ordering across partitions. Solutions: (1) single partition — guarantees global ordering but limits throughput, (2) partition by entity ID — orders per entity, (3) application-level ordering — consumer sorts by timestamp. For Docita: partition by clinic_id for clinic-specific events (ordering per clinic is sufficient), single partition for global events (e.g., system-wide announcements)."

---

**Q68: "What is the difference between Kafka and RabbitMQ? When would you choose each?"**

**Answer (full):**

Kafka and RabbitMQ solve different problems. Confusing them is one of the most common architectural mistakes.

**The fundamental difference:**

```
Kafka: Distributed log (messages persist forever)
  Producer → [Partition 0: e1, e2, e3] → Consumer A (reads from offset 2)
                                      → Consumer B (reads from offset 0)
  Messages are NOT deleted after consumption

RabbitMQ: Message queue (messages deleted after processing)
  Producer → [Queue: e1, e2, e3] → Consumer (receives e1, e2, e3)
  Messages ARE deleted after acknowledgment
```

**Detailed comparison:**

| Aspect | Kafka | RabbitMQ |
|--------|-------|----------|
| **Model** | Distributed log | Message broker |
| **Message retention** | Permanent (configurable) | Deleted after processing |
| **Consumer model** | Pull (consumer requests messages) | Push (broker delivers messages) |
| **Ordering** | Per-partition | Per-queue (FIFO) |
| **Throughput** | Very high (100K+ msg/s) | High (10K-50K msg/s) |
| **Latency** | Higher (batch-oriented) | Lower (individual messages) |
| **Replay** | ✅ Yes (re-read from any offset) | ❌ No (messages are deleted) |
| **Routing** | Topic + partition key | Exchanges, bindings, headers |
| **Consumer groups** | ✅ Native | ✅ Via competing consumers |
| **Protocol** | Custom (binary) | AMQP (standard) |
| **Operations** | More complex (ZooKeeper/KRaft) | Simpler (single binary) |

**When to choose Kafka:**

```
✅ Event sourcing (replay events to rebuild state)
✅ Log aggregation (collect logs from multiple services)
✅ High-throughput streaming (100K+ events/second)
✅ Stream processing (Flink, Spark Streaming)
✅ Audit trail (events persist forever)
✅ Multiple consumers read the same events
```

**When to choose RabbitMQ:**

```
✅ Task queues (background jobs, email sending)
✅ Complex routing (header-based, topic-based)
✅ Request-reply patterns (RPC over message queue)
✅ Low latency for individual messages
✅ Simple producer-consumer patterns
✅ When you don't need message replay
```

**For Docita:**

Docita could use either, but the choice depends on the use case:

| Use Case | Recommendation | Why |
|----------|---------------|-----|
| **Event streaming** (audit, analytics) | Kafka | Replay capability, persistence, high throughput |
| **Task queue** (email, SMS, report generation) | BullMQ (Redis-based) | Simpler, lower overhead, sufficient throughput |
| **Webhook delivery** | BullMQ | Task queue pattern, retry with backoff |
| **Real-time notifications** | WebSocket + Redis Pub/Sub | Low latency, bidirectional |

At Docita's current scale (25 clinics, 1000 appointments/month), BullMQ (Redis-based) is sufficient for task queuing. Kafka would be overkill. But if Docita scales to 100+ clinics with event sourcing needs, Kafka becomes the right choice.

**What NOT to claim:** "Kafka is always better than RabbitMQ" (it depends on the use case). "RabbitMQ can't handle high throughput" (it can, but Kafka is better at very high throughput). "Docita uses Kafka" (Docita uses BullMQ for task queuing at current scale).

---

### 4.2 Dead Letter Queues and Replay

**Q69: "Explain dead letter queues. When do you use them? How do you replay messages?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"DLQ: a queue where messages go after max retry attempts. Use when: (1) message is poison (unparseable, permanently failing), (2) business logic rejects the message, (3) message exceeds retry budget. Replay: (1) fix the root cause, (2) move messages from DLQ back to the main queue (manual or scripted), (3) reprocess. Monitoring: alert on DLQ depth > 0, dashboard shows DLQ rate."

---

**Q70: "What is a poison message? How do you detect and handle it?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Poison message: a message that causes the consumer to crash or fail every time it's processed. Detection: (1) message fails N times (retry budget), (2) consumer crashes on specific message pattern. Handling: (1) move to DLQ after N retries, (2) log the message content (without PHI), (3) alert the on-call engineer, (4) analyze and fix the root cause, (5) replay from DLQ after fix."

---

**Q71: "How do you implement DLQ monitoring and alerting?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Metrics: (1) DLQ depth (current number of messages), (2) DLQ rate (messages per minute entering DLQ), (3) DLQ age (oldest unprocessed message). Alerting: (1) DLQ depth > 0 for > 5 minutes (immediate), (2) DLQ rate > 10/minute (trend), (3) DLQ age > 1 hour (stale). Dashboard: DLQ depth over time, DLQ by topic, DLQ by error type."

---

**Q72: "What is the retry budget? How do you decide when to move to DLQ?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Retry budget: max retries before giving up. Factors: (1) transient vs permanent failure — network timeout (retry) vs validation error (don't retry), (2) retry cost — each retry consumes resources, (3) user impact — how long is the user waiting? For Docita: 3 retries with exponential backoff (1s, 2s, 4s) for transient failures, immediate DLQ for permanent failures (validation, authorization). Total max wait: ~7 seconds."

---

### 4.3 Worker Patterns

**Q73: "How do you design a worker that processes jobs at-least-once with idempotent consumers?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"At-least-once: the worker processes every message at least once, possibly more. Idempotent consumers: processing the same message twice has the same effect as processing it once. Implementation: (1) idempotency key per message (event ID), (2) check if already processed before executing, (3) mark as processed atomically with the business effect. The trade-off: idempotency adds code complexity but enables safe retries."

---

**Q74: "What is the outbox relay pattern vs the change data capture pattern?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Outbox relay: a process polls the outbox table and publishes messages. Simple, explicit, but adds DB polling load. CDC (Change Data Capture): a process reads the WAL and publishes changes. More efficient (no polling), but adds operational complexity (Debezium, Kafka Connect). For Docita: outbox relay is simpler and sufficient at current scale. CDC would be considered at 10x scale."

---

**Q75: "How do you handle worker scaling? What's the relationship between partitions and consumers?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Kafka: each partition is assigned to one consumer in the group. More consumers than partitions = idle consumers. More partitions than consumers = consumers handle multiple partitions. Scaling: add partitions for more parallelism, add consumers for more throughput. Non-Kafka (BullMQ): workers pull from a shared queue, scaling is automatic. For Docita's PostgreSQL-backed worker: scale by adding worker instances, each pulling from the outbox table with row-level locking to prevent double-processing."

---

## Section 5: API Design at Scale (15 questions)

### 5.1 Versioning and Evolution

**Q76: "How do you version a REST API? URL vs header vs query parameter? Trade-offs?"**

**Answer (full):**

API versioning is about managing breaking changes without breaking existing clients. The choice of strategy affects discoverability, caching, and developer experience.

**The three approaches:**

```
URL versioning:
  GET /api/v1/patients
  GET /api/v2/patients

Header versioning:
  GET /api/patients
```
