```
  Accept: application/vnd.api+json;version=2

Query parameter:
  GET /api/patients?version=2
```

**Detailed comparison:**

| Aspect | URL | Header | Query Param |
|--------|-----|--------|-------------|
| **Discoverability** | ✅ Visible in URL | ❌ Hidden in header | ⚠️ Visible but cluttered |
| **Cacheability** | ✅ Different URLs = different cache keys | ⚠️ Vary header needed | ✅ Different params = different cache keys |
| **Browser testing** | ✅ Paste in browser | ❌ Need curl/Postman | ✅ Paste in browser |
| **Clean URLs** | ❌ `/v1/` in every URL | ✅ Clean URLs | ⚠️ Params in URL |
| **Breaking changes** | ✅ Clear boundary | ⚠️ Harder to see | ⚠️ Easy to forget |
| **Industry adoption** | Most common (Google, GitHub) | Used by some (Stripe) | Rare |

**For Docita: URL versioning**

```
/api/v1/patients          ← Current version
/api/v1/appointments
/api/v2/patients          ← New version (breaking changes)

// Internal clients (clinician app, internal app)
// Migrate to v2 at their own pace
// v1 continues to work during migration
```

**When to create a new version:**

| Change Type | New Version? | Example |
|-------------|-------------|---------|
| Adding a field | ❌ No | Add `middleName` to patient response |
| Removing a field | ✅ Yes | Remove `legacyId` from response |
| Changing field type | ✅ Yes | `date` → `datetime` |
| Changing URL structure | ✅ Yes | `/patients/:id/appointments` → `/appointments?patientId=:id` |
| Adding endpoint | ❌ No | Add `GET /patients/:id/insurance` |
| Changing error format | ✅ Yes | `{ error: "msg" }` → `{ error: { code: "msg" } }` |

**Deprecation strategy:**

```typescript
// Add deprecation headers to old version responses
app.use('/api/v1/*', (req, res, next) => {
  res.setHeader('Deprecation', 'true');
  res.setHeader('Sunset', '2027-01-01');
  res.setHeader('Link', '</api/v2>; rel="successor-version"');
  next();
});
```

**What NOT to claim:** "Docita has multiple API versions" (it currently has one version). "URL versioning is always best" (it depends on your client ecosystem).

---

**Q77: "What is API deprecation strategy? How do you migrate clients without breaking them?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Strategy: (1) announce deprecation with timeline (e.g., 6 months), (2) add deprecation headers to old version responses, (3) log usage of deprecated endpoints, (4) notify clients proactively, (5) remove old version after timeline. For Docita's internal clients (clinician app, internal app): coordinate deprecation with app releases. For external clients: longer deprecation window with migration guides."

---

**Q78: "How do you handle backward-compatible schema evolution in API responses?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Rules: (1) add new fields (clients ignore unknown fields), (2) never remove fields without deprecation period, (3) never change field types, (4) use nullable/optional for new fields. Implementation: (1) version the response schema, (2) use OpenAPI to document changes, (3) test backward compatibility in CI (backward compatibility checker). For Docita: add new fields to DTOs, never remove without deprecation."

---

**Q79: "What is the expand/contract pattern for API changes?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Expand: add new field/endpoint alongside the old one. Both work simultaneously. Contract: migrate clients to the new field/endpoint. Remove the old one. Example: rename `clinic_id` to `tenant_id`: (1) add `tenant_id` field (expand), (2) write to both fields (dual-write), (3) migrate clients to read `tenant_id`, (4) stop writing to `clinic_id` (contract), (5) remove `clinic_id`. This enables zero-downtime migrations."

---

**Q80: "How do you version a GraphQL schema? How does it differ from REST versioning?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"GraphQL: no explicit versioning. Instead, deprecate fields with `@deprecated` and add new fields. Clients choose which fields to request. The schema evolves additively. Trade-off: no breaking changes possible (clients control their query), but schema grows over time. For Docita: if using GraphQL, deprecate fields instead of removing them, and use schema directives for documentation."

---

### 5.2 Rate Limiting

**Q81: "Explain rate limiting algorithms. Token bucket vs sliding window vs fixed window."**

**Answer (full):**

Rate limiting protects your system from abuse and ensures fair resource allocation across tenants. The choice of algorithm affects burst handling, memory usage, and accuracy.

**The three algorithms:**

```
Fixed Window:
  [0-60s]: 100 requests allowed
  [60-120s]: 100 requests allowed
  Problem: 100 requests at 0:59 + 100 at 1:00 = 200 in 1 second

Sliding Window:
  Rolling 60-second window
  No burst problem — counts requests in the last 60 seconds
  Problem: More memory (store timestamps of all requests)

Token Bucket:
  Bucket holds 100 tokens
  Tokens refill at 100/60 = 1.67 tokens/second
  Each request consumes 1 token
  Allows bursts (up to 100) then throttles to refill rate
```

**Visual comparison:**

```
Fixed Window (burst problem):
  Window 1:  |_______________________|
              100 requests at 0:59
  Window 2:  |_______________________|
              100 requests at 1:00
  Total: 200 requests in 1 second (burst!)

Sliding Window (smooth):
  Rolling 60s: ──────────────────────
              100 requests spread evenly
  Total: ~100 requests per 60 seconds (smooth)

Token Bucket (allows bursts):
  Bucket: [●●●●●●●●●●] 100 tokens
  Burst: [●●●●●●●●○○] 80 consumed (allowed!)
  After: [●●○○○○○○○○] 20 tokens (throttled)
```

**Detailed comparison:**

| Algorithm | Burst Handling | Memory | Accuracy | Complexity |
|-----------|---------------|--------|----------|-----------|
| **Fixed Window** | ❌ Burst at boundaries | Low (1 counter) | Low (boundary bursts) | Low |
| **Sliding Window** | ✅ Smooth | High (all timestamps) | High | Medium |
| **Token Bucket** | ✅ Controlled bursts | Medium (1 counter + timestamp) | High | Medium |

**Implementation in Redis (token bucket):**

```lua
-- Lua script for atomic token bucket
local key = KEYS[1]
local max_tokens = tonumber(ARGV[1])
local refill_rate = tonumber(ARGV[2])  -- tokens per second
local now = tonumber(ARGV[3])

-- Get current state
local bucket = redis.call('hmget', key, 'tokens', 'last_refill')
local tokens = tonumber(bucket[1]) or max_tokens
local last_refill = tonumber(bucket[2]) or now

-- Refill tokens
local elapsed = now - last_refill
local new_tokens = math.min(max_tokens, tokens + elapsed * refill_rate)

-- Consume token
if new_tokens >= 1 then
  new_tokens = new_tokens - 1
  redis.call('hmset', key, 'tokens', new_tokens, 'last_refill', now)
  redis.call('expire', key, math.ceil(max_tokens / refill_rate) * 2)
  return 1  -- Allowed
else
  redis.call('hmset', key, 'tokens', new_tokens, 'last_refill', now)
  return 0  -- Denied
end
```

**For Docita: token bucket per clinic**

```typescript
async function checkRateLimit(clinicId: string): Promise<boolean> {
  const key = `ratelimit:${clinicId}`;
  const maxTokens = 100;  // 100 requests per window
  const refillRate = 100 / 60;  // 1.67 tokens/second
  
  const allowed = await redis.eval(LUA_SCRIPT, [key], [maxTokens, refillRate, Date.now()]);
  return allowed === 1;
}

// Middleware
async function rateLimitMiddleware(req, res, next) {
  const clinicId = req.tenantContext.clinicId;
  const allowed = await checkRateLimit(clinicId);
  
  if (!allowed) {
    return res.status(429).json({
      error: 'Rate limit exceeded',
      retryAfter: Math.ceil(1 / (100 / 60)),  // seconds until next token
    });
  }
  
  next();
}
```

**Why token bucket for Docita:**

Clinic booking patterns are bursty — 10 receptionists might book 50 appointments in 10 minutes, then nothing for 30 minutes. Token bucket allows this burst (up to 100 tokens) while preventing sustained abuse (>100 requests/minute continuously).

**What NOT to claim:** "We use fixed window rate limiting" (it has the burst problem). "Rate limiting prevents DDoS" (rate limiting prevents abuse; DDoS requires infrastructure-level protection).

---

**Q82: "How do you implement rate limiting across multiple API instances?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Centralized rate limiting: all instances share a Redis counter. Each request decrements the counter atomically (Redis Lua script). Trade-off: Redis latency adds to every request. Distributed rate limiting: each instance tracks its own counter, periodically synced. Faster but less accurate. For Docita: centralized via Redis is fine (low latency, accurate). At scale: use local rate limiting with periodic sync for hot paths."

---

**Q83: "What is per-tenant rate limiting? How do you prevent one clinic from consuming all resources?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Per-tenant rate limiting: each clinic gets its own rate limit (e.g., 100 requests/minute). Key: `ratelimit:{clinicId}:{window}`. This prevents one busy clinic from starving others. Implementation: Redis key per clinic, atomic decrement. Monitoring: track which clinics hit limits, adjust limits based on tier. For Docita: rate limit by clinic_id, with higher limits for premium clinics."

---

**Q84: "How do you handle rate limit headers (X-RateLimit-Remaining, Retry-After)?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Headers: `X-RateLimit-Limit` (max requests), `X-RateLimit-Remaining` (current remaining), `X-RateLimit-Reset` (window reset time), `Retry-After` (seconds until retry). Implementation: (1) check rate limit before processing, (2) if exceeded, return 429 with Retry-After header, (3) always include rate limit headers in response. Client behavior: respect Retry-After, implement exponential backoff."

---

**Q85: "What is the difference between rate limiting and throttling?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Rate limiting: reject requests that exceed the limit (429 error). Throttling: slow down requests that exceed the limit (queue them, add delay). Rate limiting is simpler and more predictable. Throttling provides better UX (no errors, just slower). For Docita: rate limiting for API endpoints (hard limit), throttling for background jobs (soft limit, queue and process slowly)."

---

### 5.3 API Patterns

**Q86: "When would you choose gRPC over REST? Over GraphQL? For Docita specifically?"**

**Answer (full):**

Each protocol excels in different scenarios. The choice depends on your clients, performance needs, and team expertise.

**The three protocols:**

```
REST: "Here are the resources, use standard HTTP methods"
  GET /api/patients/:id
  POST /api/appointments
  PUT /api/patients/:id

GraphQL: "Tell me exactly what data you want"
  POST /graphql
  { query { patient(id: "123") { name, appointments { date } } } }

gRPC: "Here's the contract, call the function"
  service PatientService {
    rpc GetPatient (GetPatientRequest) returns (Patient);
    rpc CreateAppointment (CreateAppointmentRequest) returns (Appointment);
  }
```

**Detailed comparison:**

| Aspect | REST | GraphQL | gRPC |
|--------|------|---------|------|
| **Protocol** | HTTP/1.1 | HTTP/1.1 | HTTP/2 |
| **Data format** | JSON (text) | JSON (text) | Protobuf (binary) |
| **Schema** | OpenAPI (optional) | Schema (required) | Proto (required) |
| **Type safety** | ⚠️ Optional | ✅ Schema-based | ✅ Proto-based |
| **Over-fetching** | ⚠️ Common | ✅ Client chooses fields | ✅ Client chooses fields |
| **Under-fetching** | ⚠️ Multiple requests | ✅ Single request | ⚠️ Multiple requests |
| **Browser support** | ✅ Native | ✅ Native | ⚠️ Requires gRPC-Web |
| **Streaming** | ⚠️ SSE/WebSocket | ⚠️ Subscriptions | ✅ Native bidirectional |
| **Performance** | Good | Good | Best (binary, HTTP/2) |
| **Developer experience** | ✅ Simple | ✅ Great (Playground) | ⚠️ Steeper learning curve |
| **Industry adoption** | Universal | Growing (50%+) | Growing (microservices) |

**When to choose each:**

**Choose REST when:**
- Building a simple API with standard CRUD operations
- Clients are browsers or mobile apps
- Team is familiar with HTTP/REST
- You want maximum compatibility
- Example: Docita's clinician app API

**Choose GraphQL when:**
- Clients have wildly different data needs
- You want to avoid over-fetching/under-fetching
- You have a complex data graph (patients → appointments → prescriptions → lab orders)
- You want a single endpoint for all queries
- Example: A patient portal where different screens need different data

**Choose gRPC when:**
- Service-to-service communication (not client-facing)
- You need high performance (binary protocol, HTTP/2 multiplexing)
- You need bidirectional streaming
- You want strong typing across services
- Example: Docita API → Worker communication

**For Docita specifically:**

| Communication | Protocol | Why |
|---------------|----------|-----|
| **Clinician app → API** | REST | Simple, browser-native, well-understood |
| **Internal app → API** | REST | Same reasons |
| **API → Worker** | REST (or BullMQ) | Sufficient at current scale |
| **API → SendGrid** | REST (provider's API) | Provider's choice |
| **API → future microservice** | gRPC | Strong typing, performance |

GraphQL would be overkill for Docita because:
1. The data graph is relatively simple (not deeply nested)
2. The clients have similar data needs (not wildly different)
3. REST with `select` and `include` in Prisma handles field selection well
4. Adding GraphQL adds complexity (schema, resolvers, N+1 prevention)

**What NOT to claim:** "gRPC is always faster than REST" (it depends on payload size and network). "GraphQL replaces REST" (they solve different problems). "Docita uses gRPC" (it uses REST at current scale).

---

**Q87: "Explain the BFF (Backend for Frontend) pattern. When is it useful?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"BFF: a separate backend for each frontend. The clinician app has its own BFF that aggregates data from multiple microservices. The internal app has its own BFF optimized for admin workflows. Use when: (1) different frontends have different data needs, (2) you want to avoid over-fetching, (3) you want to aggregate multiple service calls into one. For Docita: useful if the clinician app and internal app need different API shapes."

---

**Q88: "How do you handle API authentication in a multi-service architecture?"**

**Answer (full):**

Authentication in a multi-service architecture requires deciding who validates tokens and how trust is established between services.

**The three approaches:**

```
Approach 1: API Gateway validates, services trust
  Client → Gateway (validates JWT) → Service (trusts gateway)
  ✅ Simple — services don't need JWT logic
  ❌ Gateway is SPOF — if bypassed, services are unprotected

Approach 2: Each service validates independently
  Client → Gateway → Service (validates JWT itself)
  ✅ No trust required — each service is self-sufficient
  ❌ Duplicated validation logic

Approach 3: Service-to-service via mTLS/API keys
  Service A → mTLS → Service B
  ✅ Strong identity verification
  ❌ Certificate management complexity
```

**For Docita:**

```typescript
// API Gateway validates JWT and attaches user context
@Injectable()
export class AuthGuard implements CanActivate {
  async canActivate(context: ExecutionContext): Promise<boolean> {
    const request = context.switchToHttp().getRequest();
    const token = this.extractToken(request);
    
    // Validate JWT
    const payload = await this.jwtService.verifyAsync(token);
    
    // Attach user context to request headers (for downstream services)
    request.headers['x-user-id'] = payload.sub;
    request.headers['x-clinic-id'] = payload.clinicId;
    request.headers['x-user-role'] = payload.role;
    
    return true;
  }
}

// Worker trusts the headers (set by API gateway)
// But ALSO validates independently (defense in depth)
@Injectable()
export class WorkerAuthGuard {
  async validate(headers: Record<string, string>) {
    // Primary: trust API gateway headers
    const userId = headers['x-user-id'];
    const clinicId = headers['x-clinic-id'];
    
    // Secondary: verify JWT if present (defense in depth)
    if (headers['authorization']) {
      const payload = await this.jwtService.verifyAsync(
        headers['authorization'].replace('Bearer ', '')
      );
      // Verify headers match JWT
      if (payload.sub !== userId) throw new UnauthorizedException();
    }
    
    return { userId, clinicId };
  }
}
```

**The trade-off:**

| Approach | Security | Complexity | Performance |
|----------|---------|------------|-------------|
| **Gateway validates, services trust** | Medium (SPOF) | Low | Best (no redundant validation) |
| **Each service validates** | High (no trust) | Medium | Good (duplicate validation) |
| **mTLS between services** | Highest (crypto identity) | High | Good (TLS overhead) |

**What NOT to claim:** "Docita uses mTLS between services" (it trusts the API gateway at current scale). "API keys are sufficient for service-to-service auth" (API keys are weaker than JWT or mTLS).

---

**Q89: "What is API gateway pattern? How does it relate to service mesh?"**

**Answer (full):**

API gateway and service mesh solve different problems at different layers.

**The difference:**

```
API Gateway (north-south traffic):
  Client → Gateway → Service
  
  Gateway handles:
  - Authentication (validate JWT)
  - Routing (which service handles this request?)
  - Rate limiting (per-client, per-endpoint)
  - SSL termination (HTTPS → HTTP internally)
  - Request transformation (add headers, modify body)

Service Mesh (east-west traffic):
  Service A → Mesh proxy → Service B
  
  Mesh handles:
  - mTLS (service-to-service encryption)
  - Retries (automatic retry on failure)
  - Circuit breaking (prevent cascading failures)
  - Observability (metrics, traces, logs)
  - Traffic management (canary, A/B testing)
```

**Visual:**

```
                    API Gateway
                    ┌─────────────┐
Client ──HTTPS────▶│  Auth        │
                    │  Rate Limit  │
                    │  Routing     │
                    └──────┬──────┘
                           │ HTTP
              ┌────────────┼────────────┐
              ▼            ▼            ▼
         ┌────────┐  ┌────────┐  ┌────────┐
         │ API    │  │ Worker │  │ Notif  │
         │ Service│  │ Service│  │ Service│
         └────────┘  └────────┘  └────────┘
              │            │            │
              └──── Service Mesh ───────┘
                    (mTLS, retries,
                     circuit breaking)
```

**When to use each:**

| Scenario | API Gateway | Service Mesh | Both |
|----------|------------|-------------|------|
| **Client → Service** | ✅ Required | ❌ Not needed | ✅ |
| **Service → Service** | ❌ Not needed | ✅ Optional | ✅ |
| **<5 services** | ✅ Yes | ❌ Overkill | ❌ |
| **>10 services** | ✅ Yes | ✅ Yes | ✅ |
| **Docita (2 services)** | ✅ Yes | ❌ Overkill | ❌ |

**For Docita:** API gateway for client traffic (NestJS guards handle auth, rate limiting). No service mesh — too complex for 2 services (API + worker).

**What NOT to claim:** "Docita uses a service mesh" (it has 2 services; mesh is overkill). "API gateway and service mesh are the same thing" (they solve different problems at different layers).

---

**Q90: "How do you design API error responses? What's the standard error shape?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Standard error shape: `{ error: { code: 'VALIDATION_ERROR', message: 'Human-readable', details: [{ field: 'email', message: 'Invalid format' }], requestId: 'abc-123' } }`. Rules: (1) consistent shape across all endpoints, (2) machine-readable error code, (3) human-readable message, (4) request ID for debugging, (5) never expose internal errors (stack traces, SQL errors) to clients. For Docita: NestJS exception filter that formats all errors consistently."

---

## Section 6: Observability and Reliability (20 questions)

### 6.1 SLI/SLO/SLA

**Q91: "Explain SLI, SLO, and SLA. How do you define them for Docita?"**

**Answer (full):**

SLI, SLO, and SLA form a hierarchy of reliability management. Understanding the difference is critical for any production system.

**The hierarchy:**

```
SLA (Agreement): "We guarantee 99.9% uptime"
  └── SLO (Objective): "API latency p95 < 500ms for 99% of days"
        └── SLI (Indicator): "Measure p95 latency for /api/appointments"
```

**Detailed breakdown:**

| Term | What it is | Example for Docita | Who cares |
|------|-----------|-------------------|-----------|
| **SLI** | What you measure | API latency p95, error rate, booking success rate | Engineers |
| **SLO** | The target you set | p95 < 500ms, error rate < 0.1%, booking > 99.9% | Engineering leads |
| **SLA** | Contractual commitment | 99.9% uptime, 4-hour response for SEV1 | Business/legal |

**How to define SLIs for Docita:**

```typescript
// SLI definitions (what to measure)

// 1. Availability SLI: percentage of successful requests
const availabilitySLI = {
  metric: 'http_requests_total{status!~"5.."} / http_requests_total',
  window: '30 days',
  target: '99.9%',
};

// 2. Latency SLI: percentage of requests under threshold
const latencySLI = {
  metric: 'histogram_quantile(0.95, http_request_duration_seconds)',
  window: '30 days',
  target: '< 500ms',
};

// 3. Correctness SLI: percentage of successful bookings
const correctnessSLI = {
  metric: 'bookings_successful / bookings_attempted',
  window: '30 days',
  target: '99.9%',
};
```

**How to define SLOs:**

```
For a healthcare dashboard:
  Availability: 99.9% (8.76 hours downtime/year)
  Latency:      p95 < 500ms (clinician sees results in half a second)
  Correctness:  99.99% (booking must never silently fail)
  
For background jobs (notifications):
  Availability: 99.5% (more tolerant)
  Latency:      p95 < 30 seconds (email can be delayed)
  Correctness:  99.99% (every notification must eventually be sent)
```

**Error budgets:**

```
SLO = 99.9% availability
Error budget = 1 - 0.999 = 0.001 = 0.1%
Monthly budget = 30 days × 24 hours × 0.1% = 7.2 minutes

If you've used 5 minutes of budget in the first week:
  → Slow down releases
  → Focus on reliability
  → No new features until budget recovers

If you've used 30 seconds of budget:
  → Ship faster
  → Take more risks
  → Use the budget for innovation
```

**For Docita specifically:**

| Metric | SLI | SLO | SLA (if applicable) |
|--------|-----|-----|-------------------|
| **API availability** | Successful requests / total requests | 99.9% over 30 days | 99.9% (contractual) |
| **API latency** | p95 request duration | < 500ms for 99% of days | < 1s (contractual) |
| **Booking success** | Successful bookings / attempted bookings | 99.99% | 99.9% (contractual) |
| **Notification delivery** | Notifications delivered / notifications sent | 99.9% over 7 days | Best effort |
| **Data durability** | Zero data loss events | 100% | 100% (regulatory) |

**What NOT to claim:** "We have SLAs with all our clinics" (SLAs are contractual — only claim what's signed). "SLI = monitoring" (SLIs are specific metrics tied to user experience, not generic monitoring).

---

**Q92: "What is an error budget? How do you use it to make release decisions?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Error budget = 1 - SLO. If SLO is 99.9% uptime, error budget is 0.1% (~43 minutes/month). If you've consumed 50% of the budget in the first week, you slow down releases and focus on reliability. If you've consumed <10%, you can ship faster. The trade-off: error budgets balance velocity and reliability. For Docita: track error budget per service, review monthly, adjust release cadence accordingly."

---

**Q93: "How do you set an SLO for API latency? What's the target for a healthcare dashboard?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Measure current latency, then set target based on user experience. For a healthcare dashboard: p50 < 100ms (snappy), p95 < 500ms (acceptable), p99 < 2s (slow but functional). SLO: p95 < 500ms for 99% of days. The trade-off: tighter SLO = more engineering effort to maintain. Start with a realistic target based on current performance, then tighten over time."

---

**Q94: "What is the difference between availability SLO and latency SLO?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Availability SLO: the percentage of time the service is operational (e.g., 99.9% = 8.76 hours downtime/year). Latency SLO: the percentage of requests that meet the latency target (e.g., 99% of requests < 500ms). Availability measures uptime; latency measures responsiveness. A service can be available but slow (100% uptime, p95 = 5s). Both matter for healthcare: clinicians need the system to be up AND fast."

---

**Q95: "How do you report SLO compliance to non-technical stakeholders?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Translate technical metrics to business impact: (1) 'Our system was available 99.95% last month, exceeding our 99.9% target,' (2) 'Average appointment booking time was 200ms, well under our 500ms target,' (3) 'We had 0 data loss incidents.' Use dashboards with green/yellow/red status. Avoid jargon: say 'uptime' not 'availability SLO,' say 'response time' not 'p95 latency.'"

---

### 6.2 Distributed Tracing

**Q96: "Explain distributed tracing. How does OpenTelemetry work?"**

**Answer (full):**

Distributed tracing tracks a single request as it flows through multiple services, giving you visibility into where time is spent and where failures occur.

**The problem it solves:**

```
Without tracing:
  User reports: "Booking is slow"
  You check: API logs → looks fine
  You check: Worker logs → looks fine
  You check: SendGrid logs → looks fine
  
  But the end-to-end flow takes 5 seconds
  Where is the time spent? You don't know.

With tracing:
  Trace: api.booking.create (200ms)
    → db.appointment.insert (50ms)
    → db.outbox.insert (10ms)
    → worker.process.event (3000ms)
      → sendgrid.send (2800ms) ← BOTTLENECK FOUND
    → response.return (5ms)
  
  Total: 3265ms
  Bottleneck: SendGrid API latency
```

**How OpenTelemetry works:**

```
1. Instrument: Add spans to your code
2. Propagate: Pass trace context across service boundaries
3. Export: Send traces to a backend (Jaeger, Tempo, Datadog)
4. Analyze: Visualize traces to find bottlenecks
```

**Implementation in Docita:**

```typescript
// 1. Setup OpenTelemetry
import { NodeSDK } from '@opentelemetry/sdk-node';
import { JaegerExporter } from '@opentelemetry/exporter-jaeger';

const sdk = new NodeSDK({
  traceExporter: new JaegerExporter(),
  serviceName: 'docita-api',
});
sdk.start();

// 2. Auto-instrument NestJS (HTTP, Prisma, etc.)
import '@opentelemetry/instrumentation-http';
import '@opentelemetry/instrumentation-express';
import '@opentelemetry/instrumentation-prisma';

// 3. Add custom spans for business logic
import { trace } from '@opentelemetry/api';

const tracer = trace.getTracer('docita-api');

async function bookAppointment(dto: BookAppointmentDto) {
  return tracer.startActiveSpan('book-appointment', async (span) => {
    try {
      span.setAttribute('clinic.id', dto.clinicId);
      span.setAttribute('patient.id', dto.patientId);
      
      // This Prisma query is automatically traced
      const patient = await prisma.patient.findUnique({ ... });
      
      span.addEvent('overlap-check-started');
      const overlapping = await prisma.$queryRaw`...`;
      span.addEvent('overlap-check-completed', { 
        overlapping: overlapping.length 
      });
      
      const appointment = await prisma.appointment.create({ ... });
      
      span.setAttribute('appointment.id', appointment.id);
      span.setStatus({ code: SpanStatusCode.OK });
      return appointment;
    } catch (error) {
      span.setStatus({ 
        code: SpanStatusCode.ERROR, 
        message: error.message 
      });
      throw error;
    } finally {
      span.end();
    }
  });
}
```

**Trace context propagation (across services):**

```
API creates trace:
  Trace ID: abc-123
  Span ID: span-1 (booking)
  
API calls worker (via outbox):
  HTTP Header: traceparent: 00-abc-123-span-1-01
  
Worker receives trace context:
  Creates child span: span-2 (notification)
  Trace ID: abc-123 (same!)
  Parent Span ID: span-1 (from API)
  
Full trace:
  Trace abc-123:
    span-1: api.booking.create (200ms)
      span-2: worker.notification.send (3000ms)
        span-3: sendgrid.api.call (2800ms)
```

**What to trace in Docita:**

| Component | What to Trace | Why |
|-----------|--------------|-----|
| **API requests** | HTTP method, path, status code | Request-level visibility |
| **Prisma queries** | Query text, duration, rows returned | Database performance |
| **ABAC decisions** | Subject, resource, decision, reason | Security auditing |
| **Provider calls** | Provider name, duration, status | External dependency health |
| **Outbox events** | Event type, processing duration | Async job performance |

**What NOT to claim:** "We use distributed tracing in production" (describe what you WOULD do). "Tracing adds no overhead" (it adds 1-5% CPU overhead). "OpenTelemetry is the only option" (there's also Datadog, New Relic, proprietary solutions).

---

**Q97: "How do you trace a request across API → worker → provider?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Trace context propagation: (1) API creates a trace when the request arrives, (2) API writes the outbox event with trace context, (3) worker reads the event and continues the trace, (4) worker calls the provider and adds a child span. The full trace shows: API processing → outbox write → worker pickup → provider call → completion. Use OpenTelemetry context propagation via W3C Trace Context headers."

---

**Q98: "What is a trace span? How do you instrument a Prisma query?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"A span is a timed unit of work within a trace. Prisma instrumentation: (1) use OpenTelemetry Prisma instrumentation library, (2) it automatically creates spans for each Prisma query, (3) span attributes include: query text (sanitized), duration, rows affected. Manual instrumentation: `tracer.startSpan('prisma.query').setAttribute('db.statement', query)`. For Docita: instrument Prisma, HTTP requests, and external provider calls."

---

**Q99: "How do you handle tracing in a healthcare context (PHI in spans)"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"PHI must never appear in trace spans. Sanitize: (1) remove patient names from query text, (2) remove appointment details from attributes, (3) use abstract identifiers (clinic ID, not clinic name). Implementation: OpenTelemetry span processor that scrubs PHI before export. Testing: assert that trace export contains no PHI patterns (name formats, medical terms). The trade-off: less detail in traces, but HIPAA/GDPR compliance."

---

**Q100: "What is the difference between sampling strategies — head-based vs tail-based?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Head-based: decide to trace at request start (e.g., trace 1% of requests). Simple, low overhead, but may miss interesting traces. Tail-based: trace all requests, decide to keep/export after processing. More storage, but captures interesting traces (errors, slow requests). For Docita: head-based sampling at 10% for normal traffic, 100% for error responses. This balances cost and visibility."

---

### 6.3 Alerting and Dashboards

**Q101: "How do you design alerting rules? What makes a good alert vs noise?"**

**Answer (full):**

Alerting is the bridge between monitoring and action. Bad alerts create noise that people ignore; good alerts wake you up for real problems.

**The difference between good alerts and noise:**

```
Good alert (actionable):
  "API error rate > 1% for 5 minutes"
  → Someone can investigate and fix it
  → Clear symptom, clear threshold, clear timeframe

Noise alert (not actionable):
  "CPU > 80% for 1 minute"
  → CPU spikes are normal during batch processing
  → No action needed
  → Fires 50 times/day, people stop paying attention
```

**The 4 rules of good alerting:**

**Rule 1: Alert on symptoms, not causes**

```yaml
# ❌ Bad: alerting on cause (CPU)
- alert: HighCPU
  expr: node_cpu_seconds_total > 80%
  # CPU spikes are normal — this fires too often

# ✅ Good: alerting on symptom (error rate)
- alert: HighErrorRate
  expr: |
    rate(http_requests_total{status=~"5.."}[5m]) 
    / rate(http_requests_total[5m]) > 0.01
  # Errors directly affect users — this matters
```

**Rule 2: Use multi-window alerts (reduce false positives)**

```yaml
# ❌ Bad: single window (fires on transient spikes)
- alert: HighLatency
  expr: histogram_quantile(0.95, http_request_duration_seconds) > 0.5
  for: 1m  # Fires after just 1 minute of high latency

# ✅ Good: multi-window (requires sustained degradation)
- alert: HighLatency
  expr: |
    histogram_quantile(0.95, http_request_duration_seconds) > 0.5
    and
    histogram_quantile(0.95, http_request_duration_seconds) > 0.5
    offset 1d  # Also high compared to yesterday
  for: 5m  # Must be sustained for 5 minutes
```

**Rule 3: Set appropriate thresholds**

| Metric | Threshold | Rationale |
|--------|-----------|-----------|
| **API error rate** | > 1% for 5 min | Users are affected |
| **API latency p95** | > 1s for 5 min | Clinicians notice slowness |
| **Booking success rate** | < 99% for 5 min | Core workflow broken |
| **DB connection pool** | > 80% for 5 min | About to exhaust |
| **Outbox depth** | > 100 for 10 min | Worker falling behind |
| **DLQ depth** | > 0 for 5 min | Permanent failures |

**Rule 4: Route alerts to the right team**

```yaml
# PagerDuty routing
routes:
  - match: { severity: "critical" }
    escalate: on-call-engineer
    
  - match: { severity: "warning" }
    notify: slack-engineering
    
  - match: { service: "database" }
    notify: dba-team
    
  - match: { service: "security" }
    notify: security-team
```

**The RED/USE method for alerting:**

```
RED (for services):
  Rate: requests per second
  Errors: error rate
  Duration: latency distribution
  
USE (for infrastructure):
  Utilization: % of resource in use
  Saturation: queued work
  Errors: error count

For Docita:
  RED: Alert on error rate > 1%, latency p95 > 1s
  USE: Alert on DB connection pool > 80%, CPU > 90%
```

**Alert fatigue prevention:**

| Problem | Solution |
|---------|----------|
| Too many alerts | Consolidate related alerts into one |
| Transient alerts | Increase `for` duration (5 min, not 1 min) |
| False positives | Use multi-window (current + historical comparison) |
| No auto-resolve | Alert auto-resolves when condition clears |
| No review | Monthly alert review — remove unused alerts |

**What NOT to claim:** "We have zero alert fatigue" (everyone has some). "Alerting is fully automated" (humans still make the decisions).

---

**Q102: "What is the RED method (Rate, Errors, Duration)? How do you apply it?"**

**Answer (full):**

RED is a monitoring methodology for request-driven services. It measures the three most important things about every endpoint.

**The three metrics:**

```
Rate: How many requests per second?
  → Tells you: Is traffic normal? Spiking? Dropping?
  
Errors: What percentage failed?
  → Tells you: Is the service healthy? Degrading?
  
Duration: How long do requests take?
  → Tells you: Is the service responsive? Slow?
```

**Applying RED to Docita:**

```yaml
# Prometheus metrics (what to measure)
- http_requests_total{method="POST", endpoint="/appointments", status="200"}
- http_requests_total{method="POST", endpoint="/appointments", status="500"}
- http_request_duration_seconds{method="POST", endpoint="/appointments"}

# RED for the booking endpoint:
Rate: rate(http_requests_total{endpoint="/appointments"}[5m]) = 10 req/s
Errors: rate(http_requests_total{endpoint="/appointments",status=~"5.."}[5m]) 
        / rate(http_requests_total{endpoint="/appointments"}[5m]) = 0.1%
Duration: histogram_quantile(0.95, http_request_duration_seconds{endpoint="/appointments"}) = 200ms
```

**RED dashboards:**

```
Dashboard: Docita API RED

Row 1: Rate
  [Booking rate: 10 req/s] [Schedule rate: 50 req/s] [Patient rate: 20 req/s]

Row 2: Errors
  [Booking errors: 0.1%] [Schedule errors: 0.05%] [Patient errors: 0.2%]

Row 3: Duration (p95)
  [Booking: 200ms] [Schedule: 150ms] [Patient: 100ms]
```

**RED alerts:**

```yaml
# Alert on error rate
- alert: HighBookingErrorRate
  expr: |
    rate(http_requests_total{endpoint="/appointments",status=~"5.."}[5m]) 
    / rate(http_requests_total{endpoint="/appointments"}[5m]) > 0.01
  for: 5m
  annotations:
    summary: "Booking error rate > 1%"

# Alert on latency
- alert: HighBookingLatency
  expr: |
    histogram_quantile(0.95, rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket{endpoint="/appointments"}[5m])) > 1.0
  for: 5m
  annotations:
    summary: "Booking p95 latency > 1s"
```

**RED vs USE:**

| Method | For | Metrics |
|--------|-----|---------|
| **RED** | Services (API, workers) | Rate, Errors, Duration |
| **USE** | Infrastructure (CPU, memory, disk) | Utilization, Saturation, Errors |

**For Docita:** RED for API endpoints, USE for PostgreSQL and Redis.

**What NOT to claim:** "RED is the only monitoring method" (USE is better for infrastructure). "RED metrics are automatically collected" (you must instrument your code).

---

**Q103: "What is the USE method (Utilization, Saturation, Errors)? When do you use it?"**

**Answer (full):**

USE is a monitoring methodology for infrastructure resources. It measures the three most important things about every resource.

**The three metrics:**

```
Utilization: What percentage of the resource is busy?
  → Tells you: Is the resource near capacity?
  
Saturation: How much work is queued waiting?
  → Tells you: Is the resource overloaded?
  
Errors: How many errors is the resource producing?
  → Tells you: Is the resource failing?
```

**Applying USE to Docita's infrastructure:**

```yaml
# PostgreSQL
Utilization: pg_stat_activity count / max_connections = 80%
Saturation: pg_stat_activity WHERE wait_event IS NOT NULL = 5 queries waiting
Errors: pg_stat_database deadlocks = 0

# Redis
Utilization: used_memory / maxmemory = 60%
Saturation: blocked_clients = 0
Errors: rejected_connections = 0

# CPU
Utilization: node_cpu_seconds_total = 80%
Saturation: load average / CPU cores = 1.2
Errors: none (CPU doesn't error)

# Disk
Utilization: disk_usage / disk_total = 70%
Saturation: IO wait time = 10ms
Errors: disk_errors_total = 0
```

**USE dashboards:**

```
Dashboard: Docita Infrastructure USE

Row 1: PostgreSQL
  [Connections: 80/200] [Queries waiting: 5] [Deadlocks: 0]

Row 2: Redis
  [Memory: 60%] [Blocked clients: 0] [Rejected: 0]

Row 3: CPU
  [Utilization: 80%] [Load avg: 1.2] [Errors: 0]

Row 4: Disk
  [Usage: 70%] [IO wait: 10ms] [Errors: 0]
```

**USE alerts:**

```yaml
# Alert on connection saturation
- alert: PostgreSQLConnectionSaturation
  expr: |
    pg_stat_activity_count / pg_settings_max_connections > 0.8
  for: 5m
  annotations:
    summary: "PostgreSQL connections > 80%"

# Alert on CPU saturation
- alert: HighCPULoad
  expr: |
    node_load1 / count(node_cpu_seconds_total{mode="idle"}) > 1.5
  for: 10m
  annotations:
    summary: "CPU load average > 1.5x cores"
```

**RED vs USE:**

| Method | For | Metrics | Example |
|--------|-----|---------|---------|
| **RED** | Services | Rate, Errors, Duration | API endpoint health |
| **USE** | Infrastructure | Utilization, Saturation, Errors | PostgreSQL, Redis, CPU |

**For Docita:** RED for API endpoints, USE for PostgreSQL and Redis.

**What NOT to claim:** "USE is better than RED" (they measure different things). "USE metrics are automatically collected" (you must instrument your infrastructure).

---

**Q104: "How do you handle alert fatigue? What's the signal-to-noise ratio target?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Alert fatigue: too many alerts, people stop paying attention. Fixes: (1) consolidate related alerts, (2) increase thresholds, (3) use multi-window alerts (reduce false positives), (4) auto-resolve alerts when condition clears, (5) regular alert review (monthly). Target: <5 actionable alerts per day. If more, tune thresholds or consolidate."

---

**Q105: "How do you design a dashboard for a healthcare SaaS? What metrics matter?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Dashboard layers: (1) executive view — uptime, error rate, active clinics, (2) service view — RED per endpoint, queue depth, worker lag, (3) infrastructure view — CPU, memory, connections, WAL size, (4) business view — bookings/hour, active users, notification delivery rate. Tools: Grafana + Prometheus. PHI: no patient data on dashboards. Refresh: 30s for service metrics, 5min for business metrics."

---

### 6.4 Incident Response

**Q106: "Walk me through your incident response playbook for a healthcare SaaS."**

**Answer (full):**

Incident response in healthcare SaaS has a higher bar than typical SaaS: patient safety is the top priority, PHI exposure is a regulatory matter, and downtime affects clinical workflows.

**The playbook (7 phases):**

```
Phase 1: DETECT (0-5 minutes)
  ├── Alert fires (PagerDuty, Slack, email)
  ├── User reports issue (support ticket, Slack message)
  ├── Automated health check fails
  └── Severity assessment:
        SEV1: Patient safety affected, data breach, complete outage
        SEV2: Major feature broken, significant user impact
        SEV3: Minor feature broken, workaround exists
        SEV4: Cosmetic issue, no user impact

Phase 2: TRIAGE (5-15 minutes)
  ├── Is it patient-safety-critical?
  │     YES → Escalate immediately, all hands on deck
  │     NO  → Continue triage
  ├── What's the blast radius?
  │     All clinics? One clinic? One feature?
  ├── Is it a regression (recent deploy)?
  │     YES → Consider rollback
  │     NO  → Investigate infrastructure
  └── Assign Incident Commander (IC)

Phase 3: CONTAIN (15-30 minutes)
  ├── Disable affected feature (feature flag)
  ├── Route traffic around failure (load balancer)
  ├── Scale up healthy components
  ├── Activate circuit breakers
  └── Preserve evidence (don't delete logs!)

Phase 4: DIAGNOSE (30-60 minutes)
  ├── Check recent deploys (last 24 hours)
  ├── Check infrastructure (CPU, memory, connections, disk)
  ├── Check dependencies (SendGrid, Twilio, Stripe status pages)
  ├── Check database (locks, slow queries, connection pool)
  ├── Trace the failing request (distributed tracing)
  └── Identify root cause

Phase 5: REMEDIATE (varies)
  ├── Option A: Rollback (fastest, safest)
  ├── Option B: Fix forward (if root cause is clear and fix is simple)
  ├── Option C: Feature flag (disable affected feature)
  ├── Option D: Scale (if capacity issue)
  └── Option E: Manual intervention (if data issue)

Phase 6: COMMUNICATE (throughout)
  ├── Internal: Slack #incidents channel
  ├── Status page: update for users
  ├── Support team: prepared response for user inquiries
  ├── Legal/compliance: if PHI exposure suspected
  └── Leadership: SEV1/SEV2 updates every 30 minutes

Phase 7: POSTMORTEM (within 48 hours)
  ├── Blameless review
  ├── Timeline of events
  ├── Root cause analysis
  ├── What went well
  ├── What went wrong
  ├── Action items with owners and deadlines
  └── Share learnings with the team
```

**Healthcare-specific considerations:**

| Scenario | Response | Additional Steps |
|----------|----------|-----------------|
| **PHI exposure** | Contain immediately | Notify legal/compliance within 1 hour; preserve all evidence |
| **Booking system down** | Disable new bookings, keep existing appointments | Clinicians can still view existing appointments |
| **Notification failure** | Queue notifications, send when service recovers | No patient should miss an appointment due to email failure |
| **Database corruption** | Stop writes, restore from backup | Verify backup integrity before restoring |
| **Security breach** | Isolate affected systems, revoke all credentials | Full security audit before restoring service |

**The blameless postmortem template:**

```markdown
# Incident Postmortem: [Title]

## Summary
- **Date:** [Date]
- **Duration:** [Start time] - [End time] ([total duration])
- **Severity:** SEV[1-4]
- **Impact:** [What users experienced]
- **Root Cause:** [Technical root cause]

## Timeline
- [HH:MM] [Event]
- [HH:MM] [Event]
- [HH:MM] [Event]

## What Went Well
- [Thing 1]
- [Thing 2]

## What Went Wrong
- [Thing 1]
- [Thing 2]

## Action Items
| Action | Owner | Deadline | Status |
|--------|-------|----------|--------|
| [Action 1] | [Person] | [Date] | Pending |
| [Action 2] | [Person] | [Date] | Pending |

## Lessons Learned
- [Lesson 1]
- [Lesson 2]
```

**What NOT to claim:** "We have a 24/7 on-call team" (Docita is early-stage; describe what you WOULD do). "We've had zero incidents" (unbelievable; describe your response process instead).

---

**Q107: "How do you handle a suspected PHI exposure incident?"**

**Answer (full):**

PHI (Protected Health Information) exposure is the most serious incident in healthcare SaaS. The response must be immediate, documented, and legally compliant.

**The response playbook:**

```
PHASE 1: CONTAIN (0-15 minutes)
  1. Disable the affected API endpoint or feature
  2. Revoke compromised credentials (API keys, tokens)
  3. Block the attack vector (IP, user, vulnerability)
  4. Preserve ALL evidence (logs, database state, network captures)
  
  ❌ NEVER: Delete logs, restart services, or "clean up"
  ✅ ALWAYS: Preserve evidence for investigation

PHASE 2: ASSESS (15-60 minutes)
  1. What data was exposed?
     - Patient names? Medical records? Insurance info?
  2. How many records?
     - 1 patient? 100 patients? 10,000 patients?
  3. Which patients?
     - Identify affected individuals
  4. How was it exposed?
     - API vulnerability? Misconfigured access? Insider threat?
  5. How long was it exposed?
     - Minutes? Hours? Days?

PHASE 3: NOTIFY (within 1 hour)
  1. Notify legal/compliance team (they own the breach notification)
  2. Notify CISO/security lead
  3. Notify CEO/CTO (if significant)
  4. DO NOT notify patients directly (legal team handles this)

PHASE 4: REMEDIATE (within 24 hours)
  1. Fix the root cause (vulnerability, misconfiguration)
  2. Deploy the fix
  3. Verify the fix works
  4. Monitor for re-occurrence

PHASE 5: DOCUMENT (within 48 hours)
  1. Full incident report:
     - Timeline of events
     - Data exposed (types, volumes, individuals)
     - Root cause analysis
     - Remediation steps taken
     - Prevention measures for future
  2. Regulatory notification (if required):
     - GDPR: 72 hours to supervisory authority
     - HIPAA: 60 days to HHS
     - State laws: vary by jurisdiction
```

**What NOT to claim:** "We've never had a PHI incident" (unbelievable; describe your response process instead). "PHI is encrypted so exposure doesn't matter" (encryption helps, but exposure of encrypted data still requires notification).

---

**Q108: "What is a blameless postmortem? How do you run one?"**

**Answer (full):**

A blameless postmortem focuses on systems and processes, not individuals. The goal is learning, not punishment.

**The principles:**

```
1. Focus on SYSTEMS, not PEOPLE
   ❌ "John forgot to add the WHERE clause"
   ✅ "The system allowed a query without clinic scoping"

2. Focus on PREVENTION, not PUNISHMENT
   ❌ "John should be more careful"
   ✅ "Add a linter rule that enforces clinic scoping"

3. Focus on LEARNING, not BLAME
   ❌ "Who caused this?"
   ✅ "What allowed this to happen?"
```

**The postmortem template:**

```markdown
# Incident Postmortem: Cross-clinic data leak

## Summary
- **Date:** 2026-08-15
- **Duration:** 14:00 - 14:45 UTC (45 minutes)
- **Severity:** SEV1 (PHI exposure)
- **Impact:** 3 patients from Clinic B saw Clinic A's data
- **Root Cause:** Missing clinicId filter in patient search query

## Timeline
- 14:00 - Clinician reports seeing wrong patients
- 14:05 - Support escalates to engineering
- 14:10 - Incident Commander assigned
- 14:15 - Root cause identified: missing WHERE clause
- 14:20 - Hotfix deployed (added clinicId filter)
- 14:30 - Verified fix in production
- 14:45 - Incident resolved

## What Went Well
- Fast detection (clinician reported immediately)
- Quick root cause identification (5 minutes)
- Hotfix deployed in 15 minutes
- No data was modified (read-only exposure)

## What Went Wrong
- Missing clinicId filter in patient search
- No E2E test for cross-clinic search
- No automated detection (manual report only)

## Action Items
| Action | Owner | Deadline | Status |
|--------|-------|----------|--------|
| Add clinicId filter to patient search | @john | 2026-08-16 | Done |
| Add E2E test for cross-clinic search | @jane | 2026-08-18 | Pending |
| Add automated cross-clinic detection | @john | 2026-08-22 | Pending |
| Review all queries for missing clinicId | @team | 2026-08-25 | Pending |

## Lessons Learned
- Defense in depth caught the leak (patient saw "wrong" patients, not "all" patients)
- E2E tests would have caught this before production
- Automated detection would have caught it faster
```

**How to run a postmortem:**

```
1. Schedule within 48 hours of incident
2. Invite: incident responders, affected teams, leadership
3. Review timeline (fact-check with logs)
4. Identify root cause (not blame)
5. List what went well and wrong
6. Create action items with owners and deadlines
7. Share with the team
8. Track action items to completion
```

**What NOT to claim:** "We never have incidents" (unbelievable). "Postmortems are optional" (they're mandatory for SEV1/SEV2).

---

**Q109: "How do you handle a production incident at 2am? What's your triage process?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"2am triage: (1) Check alerts — what's firing? (2) Check dashboards — RED metrics, infrastructure health. (3) Check recent deploys — did something change? (4) Check provider health — is a third-party down? (5) If patient-safety-critical: escalate immediately, (6) If not: contain (disable feature), diagnose in the morning, (7) Communicate — update status page, notify stakeholders. Rule: don't debug at 2am unless it's patient-safety-critical."

---

**Q110: "How do you balance incident response with feature delivery?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Framework: (1) incident response always takes priority over features, (2) postmortem action items are tracked like features (with deadlines), (3) reliability work is allocated 20% of sprint capacity, (4) if incident rate is high, reduce feature velocity until reliability improves. The trade-off: slowing down features feels costly but prevents larger costs (user trust, compliance, revenue)."

---

## Section 7: Security Deep-Dive (20 questions)

### 7.1 Authentication and Authorization

**Q111: "Explain OAuth 2.0 flows. Authorization code vs client credentials vs PKCE."**

**Answer (full):**

OAuth 2.0 is an authorization framework that allows third-party applications to obtain limited access to a service. There are several flows (grant types) for different scenarios.

**The three flows:**

```
Authorization Code Flow (for web apps):
  1. User clicks "Login"
  2. Redirect to auth server: /authorize?client_id=xxx&redirect_uri=xxx
  3. User logs in at auth server
  4. Auth server redirects back with: /callback?code=abc123
  5. App exchanges code for token: POST /token { code: 'abc123' }
  6. Auth server returns: { access_token, refresh_token }
  
  Why secure: Code is short-lived, exchanged server-side

Authorization Code + PKCE (for SPAs/mobile):
  1. App generates code_verifier (random string) and code_challenge (SHA256 hash)
  2. Redirect to auth server: /authorize?code_challenge=xxx
  3. User logs in
  4. Auth server redirects back with: /callback?code=abc123
  5. App exchanges code + code_verifier for token
  6. Auth server verifies: SHA256(code_verifier) === code_challenge
  
  Why secure: Prevents authorization code interception (attacker can't use code without verifier)

Client Credentials (for service-to-service):
  1. Service authenticates with client_id + client_secret
  2. Auth server returns: { access_token }
  
  Why secure: No user involved, service-to-service only
```

**Visual comparison:**

```
Authorization Code (server-side app):
  Browser → Auth Server → Browser → App Server → Auth Server
                                            ↓
                                       Token (server-side)
  
  Token never exposed to browser

Authorization Code + PKCE (SPA):
  Browser → Auth Server → Browser → SPA (in browser)
                                      ↓
                                 Token (in memory)
  
  Token in browser memory (not localStorage)

Client Credentials (service-to-service):
  Service A → Auth Server → Service A
                              ↓
                         Token (service-to-service)
  
  No user involved
```

**For Docita:**

| Client | Flow | Why |
|--------|------|-----|
| **Clinician App (SPA)** | Authorization Code + PKCE | SPA in browser, needs PKCE for security |
| **Internal App (SPA)** | Authorization Code + PKCE | Same as above |
| **API → Worker** | Client Credentials | Service-to-service, no user |
| **API → SendGrid** | API Key | Provider's choice (not OAuth) |

**What NOT to claim:** "Docita uses implicit flow" (implicit flow is deprecated — use PKCE instead). "OAuth is authentication" (OAuth is authorization; OIDC adds authentication).

---

**Q112: "What is OIDC? How does it extend OAuth 2.0?"**

**Answer (full):**

OIDC (OpenID Connect) is an authentication layer built on top of OAuth 2.0. OAuth 2.0 answers "can you access this resource?" while OIDC answers "who are you?"

**The difference:**

```
OAuth 2.0 (Authorization):
  "Can this app access the user's calendar?"
  → Yes/No decision
  → No identity information

OIDC (Authentication + Authorization):
  "Who is this user?" → Identity (name, email, ID)
  "Can this app access the user's calendar?" → Authorization
  → Both identity and authorization
```

**OIDC additions:**

```
1. ID Token (JWT with user info)
   {
     "sub": "user_123",
     "name": "Dr. Smith",
     "email": "smith@clinic.de",
     "iss": "https://auth.docita.work",
     "aud": "docita-app",
     "exp": 1692000000
   }

2. UserInfo Endpoint
   GET /userinfo
   → { "sub": "user_123", "name": "Dr. Smith", "email": "..." }

3. Standardized Scopes
   openid: Required (triggers OIDC flow)
   profile: Name, username, etc.
   email: Email address
   address: Physical address
```

**When to use OIDC:**

| Scenario | Use OIDC? | Why |
|----------|----------|-----|
| **External IdP (Google, Azure AD)** | ✅ Yes | OIDC is the standard for federated identity |
| **Internal auth (custom)** | ❌ No | Plain JWT is sufficient |
| **Multi-tenant with different IdPs** | ✅ Yes | Each clinic uses their own IdP |

**For Docita:** If clinics want to use their own identity providers (Google Workspace, Azure AD), OIDC enables federation. Otherwise, Docita's internal JWT is sufficient.

**What NOT to claim:** "OIDC replaces OAuth 2.0" (OIDC extends OAuth 2.0). "We need OIDC for all authentication" (OIDC is for federated identity, not internal auth).

---

**Q113: "Explain RBAC vs ABAC vs ReBAC. How does Docita implement ABAC?"**

**Answer (full):**

Access control models determine HOW the system decides who can do what. Each model has different strengths, and the right choice depends on your domain's complexity.

**The three models:**

```
RBAC (Role-Based Access Control):
  User → has Role → Role has Permissions
  "Admin can do X, User can do Y"
  
ABAC (Attribute-Based Access Control):
  Decision = f(subject attributes, resource attributes, action, environment)
  "Admin in Clinic A can view patients in Clinic A"
  
ReBAC (Relationship-Based Access Control):
  User → has Relationship → Resource
  "Dr. Smith is the assigned physician of Patient Alice"
```

**Detailed comparison:**

| Aspect | RBAC | ABAC | ReBAC |
|--------|------|------|-------|
| **Decision based on** | Role name | Attributes (clinic, ownership, time) | Relationships (assigned, member-of) |
| **Granularity** | Coarse (role-level) | Fine (attribute-level) | Very fine (relationship-level) |
| **Multi-tenant** | ❌ Cross-clinic access possible | ✅ Clinic attribute blocks cross-tenant | ✅ Relationship blocks cross-tenant |
| **Complexity** | Low | Medium | High |
| **Performance** | Fast (role lookup) | Medium (attribute evaluation) | Slow (relationship traversal) |
| **Best for** | Simple apps | Multi-tenant SaaS | Social/graph data |

**Why RBAC alone fails for Docita:**

```typescript
// RBAC: ADMIN can do everything
if (user.role === 'ADMIN') {
  return ALLOW; // ✅ Works within one clinic
}

// But: ADMIN in Clinic A can also access Clinic B's data!
// RBAC has no concept of "which clinic"
```

**How Docita implements ABAC:**

```typescript
// Subject attributes (from JWT)
interface Subject {
  id: string;           // User ID
  role: UserRole;       // ADMIN, CLINICIAN, RECEPTIONIST, PATIENT
  clinicId: string;     // Which clinic this user belongs to
  isInternal: boolean;  // System admin vs clinic user
}

// Resource attributes (from database)
interface PatientResource {
  id: string;
  clinicId: string;              // Which clinic owns this patient
  assignedClinicianId: string;   // Which clinician is assigned
  createdBy: string;             // Who created this record
}

// Decision function
function evaluate(subject: Subject, resource: PatientResource, action: string) {
  // Rule 1: Clinic boundary (most important)
  if (subject.clinicId !== resource.clinicId) {
    return DENY; // Cross-clinic access blocked
  }
  
  // Rule 2: Role-based within clinic
  if (subject.role === 'ADMIN') return ALLOW;
  if (subject.role === 'RECEPTIONIST' && action !== 'delete') return ALLOW;
  
  // Rule 3: Ownership/assignment check
  if (subject.role === 'CLINICIAN') {
    if (resource.assignedClinicianId === subject.id) return ALLOW;
    return DENY; // Can only view assigned patients
  }
  
  // Rule 4: Patient self-access
  if (subject.role === 'PATIENT' && resource.id === subject.patientId) {
    return ALLOW;
  }
  
  return DENY;
}
```

**Real-world ABAC examples for Docita:**

| Subject | Resource | Action | Decision | Why |
|---------|----------|--------|----------|-----|
| Admin (Clinic A) | Patient (Clinic A) | read | ✅ ALLOW | Same clinic, admin role |
| Admin (Clinic A) | Patient (Clinic B) | read | ❌ DENY | Different clinic |
| Clinician (Clinic A) | Patient (Clinic A, assigned) | read | ✅ ALLOW | Same clinic, assigned |
| Clinician (Clinic A) | Patient (Clinic A, not assigned) | read | ❌ DENY | Same clinic, not assigned |
| Receptionist (Clinic A) | Patient (Clinic A) | delete | ❌ DENY | Role restriction |
| Patient (Clinic A) | Patient (Clinic A, self) | read | ✅ ALLOW | Self-access |
| Patient (Clinic A) | Patient (Clinic B) | read | ❌ DENY | Different clinic |

**When you WOULD use ReBAC:**

If Docita had complex relationships like "Dr. Smith is a consulting physician for Patient Alice's surgery" or "Nurse Johnson is a temporary substitute for Dr. Smith," ReBAC would model these relationships naturally. RBAC/ABAC would require encoding each relationship as an attribute, which gets messy.

**What NOT to claim:** "We use OPA for ABAC" (Docita uses a custom policy engine). "ABAC is always better than RBAC" (RBAC is simpler and sufficient for single-tenant apps). "ReBAC is the best model" (ReBAC is powerful but adds complexity and performance overhead).

---

**Q114: "How do you handle token refresh in a SPA? What are the failure modes?"**

**Answer (full):**

Token refresh in a SPA is a security-critical flow. The access token is short-lived (15 minutes) for security; the refresh token is long-lived (7 days) for UX. The challenge: when the access token expires mid-request, you must refresh transparently without the user noticing.

**The refresh flow:**

```
Time 0:    User logs in
           Server returns: access_token (15min) + refresh_token (7 days, httpOnly cookie)

Time 0-15min: API calls work normally
           Authorization: Bearer <access_token>

Time 15min: Access token expires
           User makes API call → 401 Unauthorized

           Client detects 401
           Client sends: POST /auth/refresh (refresh_token in httpOnly cookie)
           Server validates refresh token
           Server returns: new access_token + new refresh_token
           Client retries original request with new access_token
```

**Implementation:**

```typescript
// Axios interceptor for transparent token refresh
let isRefreshing = false;
let failedQueue: Array<{ resolve: Function; reject: Function }> = [];

const processQueue = (error: any, token: string | null) => {
  failedQueue.forEach(({ resolve, reject }) => {
    if (error) reject(error);
    else resolve(token);
  });
  failedQueue = [];
};

api.interceptors.response.use(
  (response) => response,
  async (error) => {
    const originalRequest = error.config;
    
    if (error.response?.status === 401 && !originalRequest._retry) {
      if (isRefreshing) {
        // Queue this request until refresh completes
        return new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
          failedQueue.push({ resolve, reject });
        }).then(token => {
          originalRequest.headers.Authorization = `Bearer ${token}`;
          return api(originalRequest);
        });
      }
      
      originalRequest._retry = true;
      isRefreshing = true;
      
      try {
        // Refresh token is in httpOnly cookie — sent automatically
        const { data } = await axios.post('/auth/refresh');
        const newToken = data.accessToken;
        
        processQueue(null, newToken);
        
        originalRequest.headers.Authorization = `Bearer ${newToken}`;
        return api(originalRequest);
      } catch (refreshError) {
        processQueue(refreshError, null);
        // Refresh failed — force re-login
        window.location.href = '/login';
        return Promise.reject(refreshError);
      } finally {
        isRefreshing = false;
      }
    }
    
    return Promise.reject(error);
  }
);
```

**Failure modes:**

| Failure | What Happens | Recovery |
|---------|-------------|----------|
| Access token expired | 401 → automatic refresh → retry | Transparent to user |
| Refresh token expired | Refresh fails → redirect to /login | User re-authenticates |
| Refresh token stolen | Token family detection → ALL tokens invalidated | User re-authenticates (all sessions killed) |
| Server down during refresh | Refresh fails → redirect to /login | User retries after server recovers |
| Concurrent requests during refresh | Request queue → all retry with new token | Transparent to user |
| Refresh token rotated but old one reused | Family tracking detects reuse → invalidate family | Security: compromised token revoked |

**Token family tracking (anti-reuse):**

```typescript
// Server-side: track refresh token families
async function refreshTokens(refreshToken: string) {
  const tokenData = await db.refreshToken.findUnique({
    where: { token: refreshToken }
  });
  
  if (!tokenData) throw new UnauthorizedException('Invalid refresh token');
  
  if (tokenData.used) {
    // This token was already used — possible theft!
    // Invalidate ALL tokens in this family
    await db.refreshToken.deleteMany({
      where: { family: tokenData.family }
    });
    throw new UnauthorizedException('Token reuse detected — all sessions invalidated');
  }
  
  // Mark current token as used
  await db.refreshToken.update({
    where: { id: tokenData.id },
    data: { used: true }
  });
  
  // Issue new tokens in the same family
  const newAccessToken = generateAccessToken(tokenData.userId);
  const newRefreshToken = generateRefreshToken(tokenData.userId, tokenData.family);
  
  return { accessToken: newAccessToken, refreshToken: newRefreshToken };
}
```

**What NOT to claim:** "Access tokens are stored in localStorage" (they should be in memory or httpOnly cookies). "Refresh tokens are immune to theft" (they're vulnerable; family tracking mitigates the risk).

---

**Q115: "What is token binding? How does it prevent token theft?"**

**Answer (full):**

Token binding cryptographically ties a token to a specific client, preventing stolen tokens from being used by anyone else.

**The problem:**

```
Standard JWT:
  1. User logs in → receives JWT
  2. Attacker steals JWT (XSS, network interception, etc.)
  3. Attacker uses stolen JWT to access API
  4. API cannot distinguish legitimate vs stolen token
  → Token theft succeeds
```

**The solution — DPoP (Demonstration of Proof-of-Possession):**

```
1. Client generates a key pair (public + private)
2. Client sends token request with proof:
   POST /token
   Headers: DPoP: <signed_hash>
   
3. Server issues token bound to the public key:
   { access_token: "xxx", token_type: "DPoP-bound" }

4. Client signs every request with private key:
   GET /api/patients
   Headers: 
     Authorization: DPoP <token>
     DPoP: <signature of (method + url + timestamp + jti)>

5. Server verifies:
   - Token is valid
   - DPoP proof matches the token's bound key
   - If attacker uses stolen token without the private key → FAILS
```

**Visual:**

```
Without DPoP:
  Attacker steals JWT → Uses JWT → Access granted ❌

With DPoP:
  Attacker steals JWT → Tries to use JWT without private key
  Server: "DPoP proof missing/invalid" → Access denied ✅
```

**For Docita:** DPoP would be a strong defense against token theft, but adds client complexity (key generation, proof signing). At current scale, standard JWT with httpOnly cookies is sufficient.

**What NOT to claim:** "DPoP is widely adopted" (it's still emerging). "Token theft is impossible with DPoP" (it prevents token replay, not all attacks).

---

### 7.2 Infrastructure Security

**Q116: "Explain mTLS. When would you use it in a microservices architecture?"**

**Answer (full):**

mTLS (mutual TLS) extends standard TLS by requiring both client and server to present certificates. This provides strong identity verification between services.

**The difference from standard TLS:**

```
Standard TLS (HTTPS):
  Client verifies server's certificate
  Server does NOT verify client's certificate
  → Server trusts anyone with a valid URL

Mutual TLS (mTLS):
  Client verifies server's certificate
  Server ALSO verifies client's certificate
  → Both sides prove their identity
```

**How mTLS works:**

```
1. Service A connects to Service B
2. Service B presents its certificate
3. Service A verifies Service B's certificate (is this really Service B?)
4. Service A presents its certificate
5. Service B verifies Service A's certificate (is this really Service A?)
6. Both sides trust each other → connection established
```

**When to use mTLS:**

| Scenario | Use mTLS? | Why |
|----------|----------|-----|
| **Client → API** | ❌ No | Clients don't have certificates |
| **API → Worker** | ⚠️ Optional | JWT/API keys are simpler at small scale |
| **API → Database** | ❌ No | Database uses password auth |
| **Service → Service (high security)** | ✅ Yes | Strong identity verification |
| **Service Mesh (Istio/Linkerd)** | ✅ Yes | Mesh handles cert management automatically |

**For Docita:** At 2 services (API + worker), mTLS is overkill. JWT or API keys between services is simpler. At 10+ services, consider a service mesh that handles mTLS automatically.

**What NOT to claim:** "Docita uses mTLS" (it trusts the API gateway at current scale). "mTLS is always necessary" (it adds complexity not justified at small scale).

---

**Q117: "How do you manage secrets in production? Vault vs AWS SM vs environment variables?"**

**Answer (full):**

Secrets management is critical for security. A leaked DATABASE_URL or JWT_SECRET can compromise the entire system.

**The options:**

```
Environment Variables:
  DATABASE_URL=postgres://user:pass@host/db
  JWT_SECRET=my-secret-key
  
  Pros: Simple, no infrastructure
  Cons: Secrets in plaintext on disk, no rotation, no audit

AWS Secrets Manager:
  aws secretsmanager create-secret --name docita/db --secret-string '{"url":"..."}'
  
  Pros: Managed, automatic rotation, IAM-controlled, audit trail
  Cons: AWS lock-in, costs ~$0.40/secret/month

HashiCorp Vault:
  vault kv put secret/docita/db url="postgres://..."
  
  Pros: Dynamic secrets, encryption as a service, audit trail
  Cons: Complex setup, operational overhead
```

**For Docita: AWS Secrets Manager**

```typescript
// Retrieving secrets at runtime
import { SecretsManagerClient, GetSecretValueCommand } from 
  '@aws-sdk/client-secrets-manager';

const client = new SecretsManagerClient({ region: 'eu-central-1' });

async function getSecret(name: string): Promise<string> {
  const command = new GetSecretValueCommand({ SecretId: name });
  const response = await client.send(command);
  return response.SecretString;
}

// Usage
const dbUrl = await getSecret('docita/database-url');
const jwtSecret = await getSecret('docita/jwt-secret');
```

**What NOT to do:**

```typescript
// ❌ NEVER commit secrets to Git
const config = {
  databaseUrl: 'postgres://user:pass@host/db',  // IN SOURCE CODE!
  jwtSecret: 'my-secret-key',                     // COMMITTED TO GIT!
};

// ❌ NEVER hardcode secrets
const prisma = new PrismaClient({
  datasources: {
    db: { url: 'postgres://user:pass@host/db' }  // HARDCODED!
  }
});

// ✅ ALWAYS use environment variables or secrets manager
const prisma = new PrismaClient({
  datasources: {
    db: { url: process.env.DATABASE_URL }  // From secrets manager
  }
});
```

**Pre-commit hook to detect secrets:**

```bash
#!/bin/bash
# .git/hooks/pre-commit
if git diff --cached --name-only | xargs grep -l "password\|secret\|api_key\|token" 2>/dev/null; then
  echo "⚠️  Potential secret detected in staged files!"
  echo "Please remove secrets before committing."
  exit 1
fi
```

**What NOT to claim:** "We use HashiCorp Vault" (describe what you actually use). "Secrets are encrypted at rest" (describe your actual security measures).

---

**Q118: "What is supply chain security? How do you prevent dependency attacks?"**

**Answer (full):**

Supply chain security ensures that the dependencies your code relies on haven't been compromised. This is a real threat — not theoretical.

**Real attacks:**

```
2021: ua-parser-js (9M weekly downloads)
  - Maintainer account compromised
  - Malicious code injected
  - Ran cryptocurrency miners on victim machines

2022: colors.js / faker.js
  - Maintainer intentionally broke packages
  - Infected millions of projects

2023: node-ipc
  - Maintainer added political protest code
  - Deleted files on machines in certain countries
```

**Prevention layers:**

```
Layer 1: Lockfile (package-lock.json)
  - Pins exact versions of all dependencies
  - Prevents supply chain attacks via version updates
  - Commit to Git, never delete

Layer 2: Dependency auditing
  npm audit  # Check for known vulnerabilities
  npm audit fix  # Auto-fix where possible

Layer 3: Automated scanning
  - Dependabot (GitHub): auto-creates PRs for vulnerable dependencies
  - Snyk: continuous vulnerability monitoring
  - Socket.dev: detects malicious behavior in packages

Layer 4: Pin versions
  "dependencies": {
    "express": "4.18.2",  // ✅ Pinned
    "express": "^4.18.2"  // ❌ Allows minor updates
  }

Layer 5: Review dependency updates
  - Never auto-merge dependency PRs
  - Review changelog before updating
  - Check package maintainer reputation
```

**For Docita:**

| Layer | Implementation | Why |
|-------|---------------|-----|
| **Lockfile** | package-lock.json committed | Prevents version drift |
| **Auditing** | npm audit in CI | Catches known vulnerabilities |
| **Scanning** | Dependabot enabled | Auto-detects new vulnerabilities |
| **Pinning** | Exact versions in package.json | Prevents unexpected updates |
| **Review** | Manual PR review for deps | Catches suspicious changes |

**What NOT to claim:** "Supply chain attacks don't affect us" (they affect everyone). "npm audit fixes everything" (some vulnerabilities require manual intervention).

---

**Q119: "How do you handle CORS in a SPA + API architecture?"**

**Answer (full):**

CORS (Cross-Origin Resource Sharing) is a browser security mechanism that restricts which origins can access your API. Misconfiguring CORS is a common security vulnerability.

**The problem:**

```
SPA (https://app.docita.work) makes API call to:
  API (https://api.docita.work)

Browser blocks this because:
  - Different origins (app vs api)
  - CORS headers required for cross-origin access
```

**The solution:**

```typescript
// NestJS CORS configuration
app.enableCors({
  origin: 'https://app.docita.work',  // Only this origin
  methods: ['GET', 'POST', 'PUT', 'DELETE', 'PATCH'],
  allowedHeaders: ['Authorization', 'Content-Type', 'Idempotency-Key'],
  credentials: true,  // Allow cookies
  maxAge: 86400,      // Cache preflight for 24 hours
});
```

**The preflight flow:**

```
1. Browser sends OPTIONS request (preflight):
   OPTIONS /api/patients
   Origin: https://app.docita.work
   Access-Control-Request-Method: POST
   Access-Control-Request-Headers: Authorization, Content-Type

2. API responds with CORS headers:
   Access-Control-Allow-Origin: https://app.docita.work
   Access-Control-Allow-Methods: GET, POST, PUT, DELETE
   Access-Control-Allow-Headers: Authorization, Content-Type
   Access-Control-Max-Age: 86400

3. Browser sends actual request:
   POST /api/patients
   Origin: https://app.docita.work
   Authorization: Bearer <token>
```

**Security mistakes to avoid:**

```typescript
// ❌ BAD: Allow all origins
app.enableCors({ origin: '*' });
// Any website can make requests to your API

// ❌ BAD: Reflect origin
app.enableCors({
  origin: (origin, callback) => {
    callback(null, origin);  // Reflects any origin!
  }
});
// Any website can make requests (reflected origin)

// ✅ GOOD: Specific origin
app.enableCors({
  origin: 'https://app.docita.work',
});
// Only the Docita SPA can access the API
```

**What NOT to claim:** "CORS prevents all attacks" (CORS is a browser mechanism; server-side auth is still required). "We use wildcard CORS" (wildcard is insecure).

---

**Q120: "What is a service mesh? When is it useful vs overkill?"**

**Answer (full):**

A service mesh is an infrastructure layer that handles service-to-service communication. It adds a sidecar proxy to each service that intercepts all network traffic.

**The concept:**

```
Without service mesh:
  Service A ──HTTP──▶ Service B
  (direct communication)

With service mesh:
  Service A ──▶ Sidecar Proxy ──mTLS──▶ Sidecar Proxy ──▶ Service B
                    │                         │
                    └── retries, circuit breaking, metrics ──┘
  
  The sidecar proxy handles all communication concerns
  Application code doesn't need to implement them
```

**What a service mesh provides:**

```
1. mTLS (mutual TLS)
   - Automatic certificate management
   - Encrypted service-to-service communication
   - Identity verification

2. Retries and circuit breaking
   - Automatic retry on transient failures
   - Circuit breaking to prevent cascading failures
   - Timeout management

3. Observability
   - Request metrics (latency, error rate, throughput)
   - Distributed tracing (automatic context propagation)
   - Access logging

4. Traffic management
   - Canary deployments (route % of traffic to new version)
   - A/B testing (route by header/cookie)
   - Traffic shifting (gradual migration)
```

**When to use a service mesh:**

| Scenario | Service Mesh? | Why |
|----------|--------------|-----|
| **<5 services** | ❌ Overkill | Too simple, adds complexity |
| **5-10 services** | ⚠️ Consider | If you need mTLS or observability |
| **>10 services** | ✅ Yes | Complex communication patterns |
| **Strict security requirements** | ✅ Yes | mTLS is mandatory |
| **Team has ops capacity** | ✅ Yes | Mesh requires operational expertise |

**For Docita (2 services):**

| Concern | Without Mesh | With Mesh |
|---------|-------------|-----------|
| **mTLS** | Manual cert management | Automatic |
| **Retries** | Manual implementation | Automatic |
| **Observability** | Manual instrumentation | Automatic |
| **Complexity** | Low | High (sidecar overhead) |
| **Operational cost** | Low | High (mesh management) |

**Verdict:** Service mesh is overkill for Docita at 2 services. The operational complexity outweighs the benefits.

**What NOT to claim:** "Docita uses a service mesh" (it has 2 services; mesh is overkill). "Service mesh eliminates all communication issues" (it handles communication, not business logic).

---

### 7.3 Application Security

**Q121: "Explain the OWASP Top 10. Which ones are most relevant to Docita?"**

**Answer (full):**

The OWASP API Security Top 10 (2023) lists the most critical API security risks. For a multi-tenant healthcare SaaS, several of these are existential threats.

**OWASP API Security Top 10 (2023):**

| # | Risk | Description | Docita Relevance | Mitigation |
|---|------|-------------|-----------------|------------|
| **API1** | Broken Object Level Authorization (BOLA) | Accessing another tenant's objects | 🔴 CRITICAL | ABAC + tenant isolation + RLS |
| **API2** | Broken Authentication | Weak JWT, token theft, session fixation | 🔴 CRITICAL | JWT validation + refresh tokens + httpOnly cookies |
| **API3** | Broken Object Property Level Authorization | Mass assignment, excessive data exposure | 🟡 HIGH | DTO validation + explicit field selection |
| **API4** | Unrestricted Resource Consumption | No rate limiting, no pagination limits | 🟡 HIGH | Per-tenant rate limiting + pagination |
| **API5** | Broken Function Level Authorization | Admin endpoints accessible to regular users | 🔴 CRITICAL | ABAC + role guards on every endpoint |
| **API6** | Unrestricted Access to Sensitive Business Flows | Bypassing booking rules, skipping payment | 🟡 HIGH | Business logic validation + idempotency |
| **API7** | Server Side Request Forgery (SSRF) | API makes requests to internal services | 🟢 LOW | Validate URLs, block internal IPs |
| **API8** | Security Misconfiguration | Default credentials, verbose errors, CORS | 🟡 HIGH | Strict CORS + sanitized errors |
| **API9** | Improper Inventory Management | Old API versions exposed, undocumented endpoints | 🟡 HIGH | API versioning + deprecation |
| **API10** | Unsafe Consumption of APIs | Trusting third-party API responses | 🟡 HIGH | Validate provider responses + circuit breaker |

**The #1 risk for Docita: BOLA (Broken Object Level Authorization)**

BOLA is the most common API vulnerability. In a multi-tenant system, it means Clinic A can access Clinic B's patients by changing the ID in the URL:

```
GET /api/patients/patient_abc123
→ Returns: Clinic A's patient (correct)

GET /api/patients/patient_xyz789  ← Changed the ID
→ Returns: Clinic B's patient (BOLA vulnerability!)
```

**How Docita prevents BOLA:**

```typescript
// Layer 1: ABAC policy (application level)
// Checks: subject.clinicId === resource.clinicId

// Layer 2: Repository query (database level)
async function getPatient(id: string, clinicId: string) {
  return prisma.patient.findFirst({
    where: { 
      id, 
      clinicId  // Always scoped to clinic
    }
  });
}

// Layer 3: RLS (database safety net)
// Even if clinicId is forgotten, RLS filters the data

// Layer 4: E2E tests (verification)
it('denies cross-clinic patient access', async () => {
  const clinicAPatient = await createPatient(clinicA);
  const response = await request(app)
    .get(`/patients/${clinicAPatient.id}`)
    .set('Authorization', clinicBToken); // Different clinic
    
  expect(response.status).toBe(403);
});
```

**The #2 risk: Mass Assignment (API3)**

An attacker submits extra fields that the API shouldn't accept:

```typescript
// Vulnerable: accepts all fields
await prisma.patient.update({
  where: { id },
  data: req.body // Attacker adds { "clinicId": "other_clinic" }
});

// Safe: DTO validates and whitelists fields
const UpdatePatientDto = z.object({
  name: z.string().optional(),
  email: z.string().email().optional(),
  // clinicId is NOT in the DTO — cannot be mass-assigned
});
```
