```sql
  AVG(value) AS avg_value
FROM clinic_metrics
GROUP BY bucket, clinic_id, metric_type;
```

**Dashboard metrics:**

| Dashboard | Metrics | Refresh |
|-----------|---------|---------|
| Revenue | Daily/weekly/monthly revenue per clinic | 5 min |
| Utilization | Practitioner hours booked vs available | 1 min |
| Patient Flow | Check-in to check-out duration | Real-time |
| No-show Rate | Cancelled/missed appointments | Hourly |

**30-second answer:** Events flow through Kafka to an analytics service that aggregates into 1-minute windows and writes to TimescaleDB. Continuous aggregates pre-compute dashboard data. Grafana visualizes with 1-5 minute refresh. Keep analytics separate from the transactional database to avoid performance impact.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Pipeline: API/worker events → Kafka topic → Flink for real-time aggregation → ClickHouse for OLAP → Grafana dashboards. Metrics: appointments/hour, no-show rate, average wait time, revenue per clinic. Real-time: update every 30 seconds. Batch: daily reports. PHI: aggregated data only (no patient names in analytics). Scaling: ClickHouse handles billions of rows, Flink handles 10K events/sec. Trade-off: real-time complexity vs batch simplicity."

**Q212: **Answer (full):**

A global rate limiting system must enforce consistent limits across all API instances and services, using a centralized counter store.

**Architecture:**

```
┌──────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────┐
│ Client   │────▶│ API Gateway  │────▶│ NestJS   │
│ (clinic) │     │ (rate limit) │     │ (API)    │
└──────────┘     └──────┬───────┘     └──────────┘
                        │
                   ┌────┴────┐
                   │  Redis  │
                   │ Cluster │
                   └─────────┘
```

**Sliding window implementation:**

```typescript
// Redis Lua script for atomic sliding window rate limiting
const SLIDING_WINDOW_SCRIPT = `
  local key = KEYS[1]
  local now = tonumber(ARGV[1])
  local window = tonumber(ARGV[2])
  local limit = tonumber(ARGV[3])
  
  -- Remove expired entries
  redis.call('ZREMRANGEBYSCORE', key, 0, now - window)
  
  -- Count current entries
  local count = redis.call('ZCARD', key)
  
  if count < limit then
    -- Allow: add request
    redis.call('ZADD', key, now, now .. '-' .. math.random())
    redis.call('PEXPIRE', key, window)
    return {1, limit - count - 1, now + window}
  else
    -- Deny: get oldest entry for Retry-After
    local oldest = redis.call('ZRANGE', key, 0, 0, 'WITHSCORES')
    local retryAfter = oldest[2] and (tonumber(oldest[2]) + window - now) / 1000 or 1
    return {0, 0, retryAfter}
  end
`;
```

**Per-tenant configuration:**

```typescript
const RATE_LIMITS = {
  free:       { requests: 100,   windowMs: 60_000 },
  pro:        { requests: 1_000, windowMs: 60_000 },
  enterprise: { requests: 10_000, windowMs: 60_000 },
};

// Endpoint-specific overrides
const ENDPOINT_LIMITS = {
  'POST /api/v1/appointments': 10,    // 10 bookings/min per tenant
  'GET /api/v1/schedule': 100,        // 100 reads/min per tenant
  'POST /api/v1/webhooks': 5,         // 5 webhook registrations/min
};
```

**Response headers:**

```
HTTP/1.1 200 OK
X-RateLimit-Limit: 1000
X-RateLimit-Remaining: 847
X-RateLimit-Reset: 1692950460
Retry-After: 12  (only on 429)
```

**30-second answer:** Use Redis sorted sets with Lua scripts for atomic sliding window rate limiting. Enforce per-tenant limits based on plan tier, with endpoint-specific overrides for critical operations. Return standard rate limit headers (X-RateLimit-Remaining, Retry-After). Monitor rate limit hits to identify abusive tenants and adjust limits.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Architecture: Redis-based rate limiter with per-clinic keys. Algorithm: token bucket (allows bursty patterns). Scope: per-clinic for API endpoints, per-user for sensitive operations (login, password reset). Implementation: Redis Lua script for atomic token consumption. Headers: X-RateLimit-Limit, X-RateLimit-Remaining, Retry-After. Failure modes: Redis down → local rate limiting (less accurate but functional). Monitoring: rate limit hits per clinic, rejection rate."

**Q213: **Answer (full):**

A feature flag system enables controlled rollouts, A/B testing, and instant rollbacks without code deployments.

**Architecture:**

```
┌──────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────┐
│ NestJS   │────▶│ Flag Service │────▶│ Redis    │
│ (API)    │     │ (evaluation) │     │ (cache)  │
└──────────┘     └──────────────┘     └──────────┘
     │                                       │
     │            ┌──────────────┐           │
     └────────────│ Admin UI     │───────────┘
                  │ (flag mgmt)  │
                  └──────────────┘
```

**Flag evaluation:**

```typescript
interface FeatureFlag {
  key: string;
  enabled: boolean;
  rolloutPercentage: number;  // 0-100
  targetSegments: string[];   // ['beta_clinics', 'enterprise']
  overrides: Record<string, boolean>;  // { 'clinic_123': true }
}

async function isFeatureEnabled(
  flagKey: string,
  context: { tenantId: string; userId: string }
): Promise<boolean> {
  const flag = await redis.get(`flag:${flagKey}`);
  if (!flag) return false;
  
  const parsed: FeatureFlag = JSON.parse(flag);
  
  // Check overrides first (clinic-specific)
  if (parsed.overrides[context.tenantId] !== undefined) {
    return parsed.overrides[context.tenantId];
  }
  
  if (!parsed.enabled) return false;
  
  // Percentage rollout (deterministic based on tenant+flag hash)
  const hash = deterministicHash(`${context.tenantId}:${flagKey}`);
  return (hash % 100) < parsed.rolloutPercentage;
}
```

**Rollout strategy:**

```
Phase 1: 0% → 1% (internal testing)
Phase 2: 1% → 10% (beta clinics)
Phase 3: 10% → 50% (gradual rollout)
Phase 4: 50% → 100% (full release)

Each phase: Monitor error rate, latency, business metrics
If anomaly detected: Roll back to previous phase instantly
```

**30-second answer:** Feature flags are key-value pairs in Redis with percentage rollout and tenant overrides. Evaluate using deterministic hashing (same tenant always gets same result). Roll out gradually: 1% → 10% → 50% → 100%, monitoring metrics at each phase. Instant rollback by setting `enabled: false`.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Components: (1) flag definitions (JSON: name, type, default, targeting rules), (2) evaluation engine (hash user_id + flag_name for consistent bucketing), (3) admin UI (create/edit/kill switch), (4) SDK for client-side evaluation. Targeting: percentage rollout (10% of users), user segments (beta clinics), individual users. Storage: PostgreSQL for flag definitions, Redis for evaluation cache. Kill switch: instant flag disable. Trade-off: SDK simplicity vs server-side evaluation."

**Q214: **Answer (full):**

Disaster recovery for healthcare SaaS requires both RPO (Recovery Point Objective) < 1 hour and RTO (Recovery Time Objective) < 4 hours.

**Architecture:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Primary Region (Frankfurt)      │
│  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  │
│  │ App (3x) │  │ DB Primary│  │ Redis    │  │
│  └──────────┘  └─────┬────┘  └──────────┘  │
│                      │                       │
│              WAL Streaming (sync)            │
└──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┘
                       │
              ┌────────┴────────┐
              │   S3 (cross-region)│
              │   WAL archive     │
              └────────┬────────┘
                       │
┌──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┐
│              DR Region (Amsterdam)           │
│  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  │
│  │ App (1x) │  │ DB Standby│  │ Redis    │  │
│  │ (cold)   │  │ (sync)   │  │ (cold)   │  │
│  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Recovery procedures:**

```bash
# Scenario: Primary database lost
# RPO: < 1 hour (WAL streaming lag + archive gap)
# RTO: < 4 hours

# 1. Promote DR standby to primary
pg_ctl promote -D /var/lib/postgresql/data/

# 2. Update DNS to point to DR region
# (Using Route53 health checks for automatic failover)

# 3. Scale up DR app instances
kubectl scale deployment docita-api --replicas=3

# 4. Verify data integrity
SELECT count(*) FROM appointments WHERE created_at > '2026-08-22 00:00:00';
```

**Backup schedule:**

| Backup Type | Frequency | Retention | RPO Contribution |
|-------------|-----------|-----------|------------------|
| WAL streaming | Continuous | N/A | 0 (sync) or seconds (async) |
| WAL archive | Continuous | 7 years | 0 (if archiving works) |
| Base backup | Daily | 30 days | 0 (WAL fills the gap) |
| Logical backup | Weekly | 1 year | 0 (WAL fills the gap) |

**30-second answer:** Use WAL streaming to a DR region with sync replication for RPO ≈ 0. Maintain a cold standby in another region (Amsterdam). Automatic failover via Route53 health checks. RTO < 4 hours by pre-provisioning DR infrastructure. WAL archives to cross-region S3 for 7-year HIPAA retention.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Architecture: (1) primary PostgreSQL on AWS RDS, (2) automated backups every hour (WAL archival to S3), (3) read replica in same region, (4) cross-region replica for DR. RPO: 1 hour (WAL archival interval). RTO: 1 hour (restore from backup + replay WAL). Process: (1) detect failure, (2) promote read replica to primary, (3) update DNS, (4) verify application connectivity. Testing: quarterly DR drills. Trade-off: RPO < 1 hour requires synchronous replication (higher latency)."

**Q215: **Answer (full):**

Multi-region deployment for healthcare must comply with data residency laws (GDPR requires EU data to stay in EU, some countries require in-country storage).

**Architecture:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Region: Frankfurt (EU-West)     │
│  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  │
│  │ App (3x) │  │ DB Primary│  │ Redis    │  │
│  │ + Worker │  │ (EU data) │  │ Cluster  │  │
│  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Region: Amsterdam (EU-North)    │
│  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  │
│  │ App (2x) │  │ DB Replica│  │ Redis    │  │
│  │          │  │ (EU data) │  │ Cluster  │  │
│  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Region: Mumbai (AP-South)       │
│  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  │
│  │ App (2x) │  │ DB Primary│  │ Redis    │  │
│  │          │  │ (IN data) │  │ Cluster  │  │
│  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Data routing:**

```typescript
// Route requests based on tenant's data residency requirement
async function routeRequest(tenantId: string, request: Request) {
  const tenant = await this.tenantService.get(tenantId);
  
  switch (tenant.dataResidency) {
    case 'EU':
      // Route to Frankfurt or Amsterdam
      return this.routeToRegion('eu-west', request);
    case 'IN':
      // Route to Mumbai (Indian patient data must stay in India)
      return this.routeToRegion('ap-south', request);
    case 'GLOBAL':
      // Route to nearest region
      return this.routeToNearest(request);
  }
}
```

**30-second answer:** Deploy separate database clusters per residency region (EU, India). Route requests based on tenant's data residency requirement. Use read replicas within a region for performance. Cross-region traffic only for non-PHI data (analytics, billing). This ensures GDPR and local data residency compliance.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Architecture: (1) each region has its own PostgreSQL primary + read replicas, (2) API and worker deployed per region, (3) patient data pinned to clinic's region (clinic metadata includes region), (4) global load balancer routes to nearest region. Data residency: EU patient data stays in EU. Cross-region: anonymized/aggregated data only. Schema changes: expand/contract across all regions. Trade-off: operational complexity vs data residency compliance."

**Q216: **Answer (full):**

A real-time notification system must deliver notifications across multiple channels (in-app, email, SMS, push) with delivery guarantees and HIPAA compliance.

**Architecture:**

```
┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────────────┐
│ Outbox   │────▶│ Kafka    │────▶│ Notification     │
│ (events) │     │ (topic)  │     │ Service          │
└──────────┘     └──────────┘     └────────┬─────────┘
                                           │
                    ┌──────────────────────┼──────────────┐
                    ▼                      ▼              ▼
              ┌──────────┐         ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐
              │ WebSocket│         │ SendGrid │   │ Twilio   │
              │ (in-app) │         │ (email)  │   │ (SMS)    │
              └──────────┘         └──────────┘   └──────────┘
```

**Notification types and channels:**

| Event | In-App | Email | SMS | Push |
|-------|--------|-------|-----|------|
| Appointment created | ✅ | ✅ | ❌ | ✅ |
| Appointment reminder (24h) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ |
| Appointment cancelled | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ❌ |
| Prescription ready | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ |
| Lab results available | ✅ | ✅ | ❌ | ✅ |

**Delivery guarantees:**

```typescript
// At-least-once delivery with idempotent processing
async function processNotification(event: NotificationEvent) {
  // Check if already processed (idempotency)
  const existing = await redis.get(`processed:${event.eventId}`);
  if (existing) return;
  
  // Send via each channel
  const results = await Promise.allSettled([
    this.sendInApp(event),
    this.sendEmail(event),
    this.sendSMS(event),
  ]);
  
  // Mark as processed
  await redis.set(`processed:${event.eventId}`, '1', 'EX', 86400);
  
  // Log failures for retry
  const failures = results.filter(r => r.status === 'rejected');
  if (failures.length > 0) {
    await this.outboxService.publish({
      type: 'NOTIFICATION_RETRY',
      payload: { eventId: event.eventId, failures }
    });
  }
}
```

**30-second answer:** Use Kafka to fan out notifications to channel-specific handlers (WebSocket for in-app, SendGrid for email, Twilio for SMS). Implement at-least-once delivery with Redis idempotency keys. Respect patient notification preferences. Scrub PHI from email/SMS content for HIPAA compliance.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Channels: in-app (WebSocket), email (SendGrid), SMS (Twilio), push (FCM). Architecture: outbox event → worker → channel-specific delivery. In-app: WebSocket scoped to clinic room, real-time delivery. Email/SMS: provider calls with retry and idempotency. Preferences: per-user, per-channel (in-app always on for clinical safety). Retry: exponential backoff, max 3 attempts, DLQ. Monitoring: delivery rate per channel, DLQ depth. Trade-off: real-time complexity vs reliability."

### 12.2 Infrastructure Cases

**Q217: **Answer (full):**

Connection pooling at Docita's scale (25 clinics, 5 API instances) requires PgBouncer in transaction mode to prevent PostgreSQL connection exhaustion.

**Architecture:**

```
┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐
│ API (1)  │  │ API (2)  │  │ API (3)  │  ... (5 instances)
└────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘
     │              │              │
     └──────────────┼──────────────┘
                    │ (100 client connections)
             ┌──────┴──────┐
             │  PgBouncer  │
             │ (20 server  │
             │  connections)│
             └──────┬──────┘
                    │
             ┌──────┴──────┐
             │ PostgreSQL  │
             │ (20 connections)│
             └─────────────┘
```

**PgBouncer configuration:**

```ini
[databases]
docita = host=localhost port=5432 dbname=docita

[pgbouncer]
pool_mode = transaction
default_pool_size = 20
max_client_conn = 500
min_pool_size = 5
reserve_pool_size = 5
reserve_pool_timeout = 3

query_timeout = 30
client_idle_timeout = 600
server_idle_timeout = 300
```

**Monitoring:**

```sql
-- PgBouncer stats
SHOW POOLS;
SHOW STATS;
SHOW SERVERS;

-- PostgreSQL connection count
SELECT count(*) FROM pg_stat_activity;
SELECT state, count(*) FROM pg_stat_activity GROUP BY state;
```

**30-second answer:** Use PgBouncer in transaction mode to multiplex 500+ client connections onto 20 PostgreSQL connections. Set pool_size = (CPU cores × 2) + 1. Monitor with SHOW POOLS and pg_stat_activity. Use transaction mode for NestJS (short transactions). Set query_timeout = 30 to kill slow queries.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Architecture: PgBouncer in transaction mode between API/worker and PostgreSQL. Pool size: (CPU cores × 2) + 1 per PgBouncer instance. Configuration: server_idle_timeout = 300s, client_idle_timeout = 600s. Monitoring: pgbouncer stats (active connections, waiting clients), pg_stat_activity (long transactions). Failure modes: pool exhaustion → queue requests, connection leak → PgBouncer kills idle connections. Trade-off: pool size vs memory vs throughput."

**Q218: **Answer (full):**

Zero-downtime migrations require the expand-contract pattern: add new columns/tables first, migrate data, then remove old ones.

**The expand-contract pattern:**

```
Phase 1: EXPAND (add new)
  ALTER TABLE appointments ADD COLUMN slot_start_new TIMESTAMPTZ;
  -- Both old and new columns exist
  -- Old code still works with slot_start (DATE)
  -- New code can write to both columns

Phase 2: MIGRATE (copy data)
  UPDATE appointments SET slot_start_new = slot_start::timestamptz;
  -- Backfill existing rows
  -- Use batched updates to avoid locking

Phase 3: SWITCH (change reads)
  -- Deploy new code that reads from slot_start_new
  -- Old code is still running (dual-write)

Phase 4: CONTRACT (remove old)
  ALTER TABLE appointments DROP COLUMN slot_start;
  -- Only after all instances are on new code
```

**Safe migration patterns:**

```sql
-- ✅ SAFE: Add column (instant in PostgreSQL 11+)
ALTER TABLE appointments ADD COLUMN slot_start_new TIMESTAMPTZ;

-- ✅ SAFE: Add index concurrently (no lock)
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY idx_appointments_new ON appointments (slot_start_new);

-- ❌ DANGEROUS: Rename column (breaks running code)
ALTER TABLE appointments RENAME COLUMN slot_start TO slot_start_v2;

-- ❌ DANGEROUS: Drop column (breaks running code)
ALTER TABLE appointments DROP COLUMN slot_start;
```

**30-second answer:** Use the expand-contract pattern: add new columns first, migrate data in batches, switch reads, then drop old columns. Never rename or drop columns in the same deployment as code changes. Use CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY. Deploy schema changes separately from code changes.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Pattern: expand/contract. (1) Expand: add new columns/tables (backward compatible), (2) deploy code that writes to both old and new, (3) backfill data in batches, (4) verify consistency, (5) deploy code that reads only from new, (6) contract: remove old columns. Tooling: safe-migrate.sh wraps Prisma with backup, dry-run, deploy, restore. Testing: migration tests in CI, staging migration before production. Trade-off: migration duration vs zero downtime."

**Q219: **Answer (full):**

Secrets management for healthcare SaaS must handle database credentials, API keys, and encryption keys across multiple services with audit logging.

**Architecture:**

```
┌──────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────┐
│ Service  │────▶│ Vault / AWS  │────▶│ K8s      │
│ (pod)    │     │ Secrets Mgr  │     │ Secrets  │
└──────────┘     └──────────────┘     └──────────┘
     │
     │ Startup: Fetch secrets from Vault
     │ Runtime: Renew lease automatically
     │ Shutdown: Revoke lease
```

**Secret types:**

| Secret | Storage | Rotation | Access |
|--------|---------|----------|--------|
| Database password | Vault | 90 days | API service only |
| JWT signing key | Vault | 180 days | Auth service only |
| SendGrid API key | Vault | Never (API key) | Notification service |
| Stripe secret key | Vault | Never (API key) | Billing service |
| Encryption key (PHI) | AWS KMS | Auto-rotation | All services |

**Implementation:**

```typescript
// NestJS module to fetch secrets at startup
@Module({})
export class SecretsModule {
  static async forRoot(): Promise<DynamicModule> {
    const vault = new VaultClient({ endpoint: process.env.VAULT_ADDR });
    
    const dbPassword = await vault.read('secret/docita/database');
    const jwtKey = await vault.read('secret/docita/jwt');
    
    return {
      module: SecretsModule,
      providers: [
        { provide: 'DB_PASSWORD', useValue: dbPassword.password },
        { provide: 'JWT_KEY', useValue: jwtKey.signing_key },
      ],
    };
  }
}
```

**30-second answer:** Use HashiCorp Vault or AWS Secrets Manager for all secrets. Never store secrets in environment variables, Git, or Docker images. Rotate database passwords every 90 days. Use Kubernetes Secrets mounted as volumes. Audit all secret access for HIPAA compliance.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Architecture: AWS Secrets Manager for production secrets, .env.example for non-secret documentation. Secrets: DATABASE_URL, JWT_SECRET, provider API keys, Sentry DSN. Rotation: automated for provider keys (quarterly), manual for DATABASE_URL (on incident). Access: IAM roles (API role, worker role, CI role). Monitoring: secret access audit logs, rotation compliance. Trade-off: AWS SM cost vs Vault complexity vs env vars simplicity."

**Q220: **Answer (full):**

A monorepo CI/CD pipeline must detect which applications changed and only build/test/deploy those.

**Architecture:**

```
┌──────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────┐
│ Git Push │────▶│ GitHub       │────▶│ Pipeline │
│ (main)   │     │ Actions      │     │ (CI/CD)  │
└──────────┘     └──────────────┘     └─────┬────┘
                                            │
                    ┌───────────────────────┘
                    ▼
            ┌──────────────┐
            │ Changed apps │
            │ detection    │
            └──────┬───────┘
                   │
        ┌──────────┼──────────┐
        ▼          ▼          ▼
   ┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐
   │ Build   │ │ Test    │ │ Deploy  │
   │ (only   │ │ (only   │ │ (only   │
   │ changed)│ │ changed)│ │ changed)│
   └─────────┘ └─────────┘ └─────────┘
```

**Changed apps detection:**

```yaml
# .github/workflows/ci.yml
- name: Detect changed apps
  id: changes
  uses: dorny/paths-filter@v2
  with:
    filters: |
      api:
        - 'apps/api/**'
      worker:
        - 'apps/worker/**'
      web:
        - 'apps/web/**'
      notification:
        - 'apps/notification/**'
      shared:
        - 'libs/**'
```

**Pipeline stages:**

```
1. Lint (all apps, 30s)
2. Typecheck (all apps, 60s)
3. Test (changed apps only, 2-5min)
4. Build (changed apps only, 1-3min)
5. Deploy to staging (changed apps only, 2-5min)
6. Integration tests (5min)
7. Deploy to production (changed apps only, 5-10min)
```

**30-second answer:** Use path-based filtering to detect changed apps in the monorepo. Only build, test, and deploy changed applications. Use shared libraries for common code. Cache dependencies and build artifacts. Total pipeline: ~15 minutes for single-app changes, ~25 minutes for cross-app changes.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Pipeline: (1) PR triggers: lint, type-check, unit tests, integration tests, (2) merge to main: build Docker images, run E2E tests, (3) deploy to staging: smoke tests, (4) deploy to production: canary (10% traffic), monitor, full rollout. Turborepo: task graph with caching (only rebuild affected apps). GitHub Actions: parallel jobs for each app. Trade-off: pipeline speed vs thoroughness. Optimization: cache node_modules, cache Turborepo build outputs."

### 12.3 Product Cases

**Q221: **Answer (full):**

The appointment scheduling system is Docita's core. It must handle concurrent bookings, timezone management, and practitioner availability.

**Data model:**

```sql
CREATE TABLE practitioners (
  id UUID PRIMARY KEY,
  clinic_id UUID NOT NULL,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  timezone TEXT DEFAULT 'Europe/Berlin'
);

CREATE TABLE availability (
  id UUID PRIMARY KEY,
  practitioner_id UUID REFERENCES practitioners(id),
  day_of_week INT NOT NULL,  -- 0=Sunday, 6=Saturday
  start_time TIME NOT NULL,
  end_time TIME NOT NULL,
  slot_duration_minutes INT DEFAULT 30
);

CREATE TABLE appointments (
  id UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
  clinic_id UUID NOT NULL,
  practitioner_id UUID NOT NULL,
  patient_id UUID NOT NULL,
  slot_start TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL,
  slot_end TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL,
  status TEXT DEFAULT 'pending',
  created_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT now(),
  UNIQUE (practitioner_id, slot_start)
);
```

**Booking flow:**

```
1. Patient selects practitioner and date
2. API queries availability + existing appointments
3. Returns available time slots (30-min intervals)
4. Patient selects a slot
5. API creates appointment with SELECT FOR UPDATE (prevents double-booking)
6. Outbox event published (APPOINTMENT_CREATED)
7. Notification sent (email + in-app)
```

**Concurrency control:**

```sql
-- Prevent double-booking with SELECT FOR UPDATE
BEGIN;
SELECT * FROM appointments
WHERE practitioner_id = 'dr_1'
  AND tstzrange(slot_start, slot_end, '[]') &&
      tstzrange('2026-08-25 10:00'::timestamptz, '2026-08-25 10:30'::timestamptz, '[]')
FOR UPDATE;  -- Lock prevents concurrent inserts

-- If no overlap, insert
INSERT INTO appointments (practitioner_id, patient_id, slot_start, slot_end)
VALUES ('dr_1', 'p1', '2026-08-25 10:00', '2026-08-25 10:30');
COMMIT;
```

**30-second answer:** Use `tstzrange` for overlap detection with `SELECT FOR UPDATE` to prevent double-booking. Store availability as recurring rules (day_of_week + time range). Generate available slots dynamically. Use UUID v7 for primary keys (timestamp-ordered). Publish outbox events for downstream services.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Entities: Clinic, Practitioner, Patient, Appointment, Slot, Room. Invariants: patient not double-booked, practitioner not overlapping, room not over-capacity, all access clinic-scoped. Write path: lock order (patient → practitioner → room), check overlaps, insert, outbox event. Read path: composite index on (clinic_id, practitioner_id, slot_start), calendar view subtracts booked from available. Scaling: partition by clinic, cache practitioner schedules, optimistic concurrency for high-contention slots."

**Q222: **Answer (full):**

The patient record system stores PHI (Protected Health Information) and must comply with HIPAA/GDPR with full audit logging.

**Data model:**

```sql
CREATE TABLE patients (
  id UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
  clinic_id UUID NOT NULL,
  first_name TEXT NOT NULL,
  last_name TEXT NOT NULL,
  date_of_birth DATE NOT NULL,
  email TEXT,
  phone TEXT,
  insurance_id TEXT,
  created_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT now(),
  deleted_at TIMESTAMPTZ  -- Soft delete for GDPR
);

CREATE TABLE patient_records (
  id UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
  patient_id UUID REFERENCES patients(id),
  clinic_id UUID NOT NULL,
  record_type TEXT NOT NULL,  -- 'consultation', 'lab_result', 'prescription'
  content JSONB NOT NULL,     -- Flexible schema for different record types
  created_by UUID NOT NULL,
  created_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT now()
);

-- Audit log (append-only, never deleted)
CREATE TABLE audit_log (
  id BIGSERIAL PRIMARY KEY,
  clinic_id UUID NOT NULL,
  user_id UUID NOT NULL,
  action TEXT NOT NULL,  -- 'READ', 'CREATE', 'UPDATE', 'DELETE'
  resource_type TEXT NOT NULL,
  resource_id UUID NOT NULL,
  old_values JSONB,
  new_values JSONB,
  ip_address INET,
  created_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT now()
);
```

**PHI encryption:**

```typescript
// Encrypt sensitive fields at rest
const encryptedFields = ['email', 'phone', 'insurance_id'];

// Use AWS KMS for encryption keys
const kms = new AWS.KMS({ region: 'eu-central-1' });

async function encryptPHI(data: PatientData): Promise<EncryptedPatientData> {
  const encrypted = { ...data };
  for (const field of encryptedFields) {
    if (data[field]) {
      const result = await kms.encrypt({
        KeyId: 'alias/docita-phi',
        Plaintext: Buffer.from(data[field])
      }).promise();
      encrypted[field] = result.CiphertextBlob.toString('base64');
    }
  }
  return encrypted;
}
```

**30-second answer:** Store PHI with column-level encryption using AWS KMS. Use soft deletes for GDPR right-to-erasure. Append-only audit log for every access. Use Row-Level Security for tenant isolation. Encrypt backups. Implement data retention policies (7 years for medical records).**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Entities: Patient, MedicalRecord, Prescription, Invoice, AuditLog. Access control: ABAC (role + clinic + ownership). Search: full-text search on patient name, filtered by clinic. Audit: every PHI access logged (actor, action, resource, timestamp). Export: structured JSON bundle (appointments, prescriptions, invoices, audit logs). Deletion: soft-delete patient, hard-delete media, retain audit logs. Scaling: partition by clinic, read replica for search, full-text search via PostgreSQL tsvector."

**Q223: **Answer (full):**

The billing system processes payments for clinic services, manages subscriptions, and generates invoices with HIPAA-compliant audit trails.

**Data model:**

```sql
CREATE TABLE invoices (
  id UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
  clinic_id UUID NOT NULL,
  patient_id UUID NOT NULL,
  amount_cents INT NOT NULL,
  currency TEXT DEFAULT 'EUR',
  status TEXT DEFAULT 'draft',  -- draft, sent, paid, overdue, void
  stripe_invoice_id TEXT,
  created_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT now(),
  paid_at TIMESTAMPTZ
);

CREATE TABLE payments (
  id UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
  invoice_id UUID REFERENCES invoices(id),
  amount_cents INT NOT NULL,
  method TEXT NOT NULL,  -- 'card', 'bank_transfer', 'insurance'
  stripe_payment_id TEXT,
  status TEXT DEFAULT 'pending',
  created_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT now()
);
```

**Payment flow:**

```
1. Appointment completed → Invoice created (draft)
2. Invoice sent to patient (email with payment link)
3. Patient pays via Stripe (card or bank transfer)
4. Stripe webhook confirms payment
5. Invoice status updated to 'paid'
6. Outbox event: INVOICE_PAID
7. Receipt sent to patient
```

**Idempotent payment processing:**

```typescript
@Post('webhooks/stripe')
async handleStripeWebhook(@Body() payload: Stripe.Event) {
  // Verify webhook signature
  const event = stripe.webhooks.constructEvent(
    payload, signature, process.env.STRIPE_WEBHOOK_SECRET
  );
  
  if (event.type === 'payment_intent.succeeded') {
    const paymentIntent = event.data.object;
    const idempotencyKey = `stripe:${paymentIntent.id}`;
    
    // Check if already processed
    const existing = await this.redis.get(`processed:${idempotencyKey}`);
    if (existing) return { received: true };
    
    // Process payment
    await this.paymentService.confirmPayment(paymentIntent.id);
    
    // Mark as processed
    await this.redis.set(`processed:${idempotencyKey}`, '1', 'EX', 86400);
  }
  
  return { received: true };
}
```

**30-second answer:** Use Stripe for payment processing with webhook-based confirmation. Implement idempotent payment handling (check `processed:{stripe_payment_id}` before processing). Store invoices with status lifecycle (draft → sent → paid). Generate HIPAA-compliant receipts. Use outbox pattern for downstream service notifications.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Entities: Invoice, Payment, LineItem, Tax, Discount. Flow: (1) generate invoice from completed appointments, (2) send invoice to clinic, (3) process payment (Stripe/Adyen), (4) reconcile payment with invoice. Idempotency: idempotency key per payment. Reconciliation: daily batch comparing internal ledger with provider records. Scaling: invoice generation is batch (worker), payment processing is synchronous (API), reconciliation is daily (worker)."

**Q224: **Answer (full):**

The prescription system manages electronic prescriptions with digital signatures, drug interaction checks, and regulatory compliance.

**Data model:**

```sql
CREATE TABLE prescriptions (
  id UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
  clinic_id UUID NOT NULL,
  patient_id UUID NOT NULL,
  practitioner_id UUID NOT NULL,
  medications JSONB NOT NULL,  -- [{ drug, dosage, frequency, duration }]
  notes TEXT,
  digital_signature TEXT,      -- Practitioner's digital signature
  status TEXT DEFAULT 'active', -- active, completed, discontinued
  issued_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT now(),
  expires_at TIMESTAMPTZ
);

-- Drug interaction log
CREATE TABLE drug_interactions (
  id UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
  prescription_id UUID REFERENCES prescriptions(id),
  drug_a TEXT NOT NULL,
  drug_b TEXT NOT NULL,
  severity TEXT NOT NULL,  -- 'mild', 'moderate', 'severe', 'contraindicated'
  description TEXT NOT NULL
);
```

**Prescription flow:**

```
1. Practitioner selects patient
2. System loads patient's current medications
3. Practitioner adds new prescription
4. System checks drug interactions (real-time API call)
5. If interactions found → Warning displayed (practitioner can override)
6. Practitioner signs digitally
7. Prescription stored with digital signature
8. Sent to pharmacy (HL7 FHIR format)
9. Patient receives notification
```

**Digital signature:**

```typescript
// Sign prescription with practitioner's private key
async function signPrescription(
  prescription: Prescription,
  practitionerPrivateKey: string
): Promise<string> {
  const data = JSON.stringify({
    id: prescription.id,
    patientId: prescription.patientId,
    medications: prescription.medications,
    issuedAt: prescription.issuedAt,
  });
  
  const sign = crypto.createSign('SHA256');
  sign.update(data);
  return sign.sign(practitionerPrivateKey, 'base64');
}
```

**30-second answer:** Store prescriptions with medications as JSONB for flexibility. Check drug interactions in real-time before saving. Require digital signatures for legal validity. Send to pharmacies via HL7 FHIR. Implement prescription expiration and refill tracking. Audit every access for regulatory compliance.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Entities: Prescription, Medication, Dosage, Refill, Interaction. Flow: (1) clinician creates prescription, (2) system checks drug interactions (external API or local database), (3) prescription stored with patient and medication references, (4) pharmacy fulfills, (5) refill requested and approved. Safety: drug interaction checks before prescription creation, dosage validation, refill limits. Audit: every prescription access logged. Scaling: read-heavy (prescription lookup), write-light (creation is infrequent)."

**Q225: **Answer (full):**

The messaging system enables secure communication between practitioners and patients within the Docita platform, with HIPAA-compliant message retention.

**Data model:**

```sql
CREATE TABLE conversations (
  id UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
  clinic_id UUID NOT NULL,
  participant_ids UUID[] NOT NULL,  -- [practitioner_id, patient_id]
  created_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT now()
);

CREATE TABLE messages (
  id UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
  conversation_id UUID REFERENCES conversations(id),
  sender_id UUID NOT NULL,
  content TEXT NOT NULL,
  content_type TEXT DEFAULT 'text',  -- text, image, document
  read_at TIMESTAMPTZ,
  created_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT now()
);
```

**Real-time delivery:**

```
1. Practitioner sends message
2. API stores in database
3. Publishes to Kafka topic: messaging.messages.created
4. WebSocket service picks up event
5. Pushes to recipient's connected clients via Socket.IO room
6. If recipient offline → Queue for email notification
```

**WebSocket implementation:**

```typescript
@WebSocketGateway({ cors: true })
export class MessagingGateway {
  @WebSocketServer()
  server: Server;
  
  @SubscribeMessage('message:send')
  async handleMessage(client: Socket, payload: SendMessageDto) {
    // Store in database
    const message = await this.messageService.create(payload);
    
    // Publish event
    await this.kafkaService.publish('messaging.messages.created', message);
    
    // Push to recipient's room
    this.server.to(`user:${payload.recipientId}`).emit('message:new', message);
  }
  
  handleConnection(client: Socket) {
    const userId = client.handshake.auth.userId;
    client.join(`user:${userId}`);
  }
}
```

**30-second answer:** Use Socket.IO rooms for real-time message delivery. Store messages in PostgreSQL with conversation threads. Publish events via Kafka for downstream processing (email notifications for offline users). Implement message read receipts. Encrypt message content at rest. Implement retention policies (7 years for medical conversations).**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Channels: patient-clinician (secure messaging), clinician-clinician (internal), notifications (system). Architecture: messages stored in PostgreSQL (with clinic scope), real-time via WebSocket (clinic room), offline via email notification. PHI: messages may contain PHI, so audit logging and access control are critical. Scaling: message volume is moderate (not chat-scale), WebSocket scoped to clinic room, email notifications via outbox."
