## Section 13: DBA-Level PostgreSQL (15 questions)

**Q236: **Answer (full):**

A 500GB table requires immediate action — it's impacting backup times, VACUUM performance, and query latency.

**Diagnosis:**

```sql
-- Check table size and bloat
SELECT
  pg_size_pretty(pg_total_relation_size('appointments')) AS total_size,
  pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size('appointments')) AS table_size,
  pg_size_pretty(pg_indexes_size('appointments')) AS index_size;

-- Check dead tuple ratio
SELECT n_live_tup, n_dead_tup,
  round(n_dead_tup::numeric / nullif(n_live_tup, 0) * 100, 1) AS dead_pct
FROM pg_stat_user_tables WHERE relname = 'appointments';
```

**Remediation plan:**

```
Step 1: Partition the table (if not already)
  - Range partition by month (appointments are time-series data)
  - Old partitions can be archived/dropped

Step 2: Clean up bloat
  - Run pg_repack (no lock) to reclaim dead tuple space
  - Or VACUUM FULL during maintenance window

Step 3: Archive old data
  - Move appointments older than 2 years to archive table
  - Drop the archive from main table

Step 4: Add missing indexes
  - Check pg_stat_user_indexes for unused indexes (drop them)
  - Add covering indexes for frequent queries
```

**Partitioning strategy:**

```sql
-- Convert to partitioned table
CREATE TABLE appointments_new (
  LIKE appointments INCLUDING ALL
) PARTITION BY RANGE (slot_start);

-- Create monthly partitions
CREATE TABLE appointments_2026_01 PARTITION OF appointments_new
  FOR VALUES FROM ('2026-01-01') TO ('2026-02-01');
CREATE TABLE appointments_2026_02 PARTITION OF appointments_new
  FOR VALUES FROM ('2026-02-01') TO ('2026-03-01');

-- Migrate data
INSERT INTO appointments_new SELECT * FROM appointments;
```

**30-second answer:** Partition by month, archive old data (>2 years), run pg_repack to reclaim bloat, drop unused indexes, add covering indexes. Partitioning allows dropping old data instantly (DROP PARTITION) instead of slow DELETEs.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Steps: (1) identify slow queries via pg_stat_statements, (2) check index usage via pg_stat_user_indexes, (3) run EXPLAIN ANALYZE on slow queries, (4) options: add missing index, partition by date range (appointments), archive old data to separate table, add read replica for read-heavy queries, tune shared_buffers/work_mem. The key: measure first, don't guess. For Docita's appointments table: partition by month, archive appointments older than 2 years to a separate table."

**Q237: **Answer (full):**

Logical replication replicates specific tables (not the entire database), allowing you to filter, transform, and replicate selectively.

**Setup:**

```sql
-- On PRIMARY: Create publication
CREATE PUBLICATION docita_pub FOR TABLE
  appointments, practitioners, patients;

-- On REPLICA: Create subscription
CREATE SUBSCRIPTION docita_sub
  CONNECTION 'host=primary port=5432 dbname=docita user=replicator'
  PUBLICATION docita_pub;

-- Verify replication status
SELECT * FROM pg_stat_subscription;
SELECT * FROM pg_replication_slots;
```

**When to use logical vs physical replication:**

| Aspect | Physical | Logical |
|--------|----------|---------|
| Granularity | Entire database | Specific tables |
| Replica writable | No (read-only) | Yes (can write locally) |
| Version | Must match | Can differ |
| Use case | HA failover | Selective replication, analytics |

**30-second answer:** Use `CREATE PUBLICATION` on primary, `CREATE SUBSCRIPTION` on replica. Logical replication replicates specific tables, allows writable replicas, and supports different PostgreSQL versions. Use it for selective data replication (e.g., replicate only appointment data to analytics replica).**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Steps: (1) create publication on primary: `CREATE PUBLICATION docita_pub FOR ALL TABLES`, (2) create subscription on replica: `CREATE SUBSCRIPTION docita_sub CONNECTION '...' PUBLICATION docita_pub`, (3) verify replication lag via `pg_stat_subscription`. Logical replication replicates DML (not DDL), so schema changes must be applied manually to both primary and replica. Use case: read replica for reporting queries that shouldn't impact primary performance."

**Q238: **Answer (full):**

Point-in-time recovery (PITR) restores the database to any specific moment using WAL archiving and a base backup.

**Recovery process:**

```bash
# 1. Stop PostgreSQL
pg_ctl stop -D /var/lib/postgresql/data

# 2. Remove current data directory
rm -rf /var/lib/postgresql/data/*

# 3. Restore base backup
pg_restore -D /var/lib/postgresql/data /backup/base.tar

# 4. Configure recovery
cat > /var/lib/postgresql/data/postgresql.auto.conf << 'EOF'
restore_command = 'cp /archive/%f %p'
recovery_target_time = '2026-08-20 14:30:00+00'
recovery_target_action = 'promote'
EOF

# 5. Create recovery signal
touch /var/lib/postgresql/data/recovery.signal

# 6. Start PostgreSQL (it replays WAL to target time)
pg_ctl start -D /var/lib/postgresql/data

# 7. Verify
SELECT max(created_at) FROM appointments;
-- Should be at or before 2026-08-20 14:30:00
```

**30-second answer:** Restore base backup, configure `recovery_target_time` in postgresql.auto.conf, create recovery.signal, start PostgreSQL. It replays WAL to the target time. Verify with `SELECT max(created_at)`. RPO depends on your `archive_timeout` setting.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Steps: (1) restore the most recent base backup to a new PostgreSQL instance, (2) configure recovery.conf with `recovery_target_time = '2024-01-15 14:30:00'`, (3) start PostgreSQL — it replays WAL segments from the backup time to the target time, (4) verify the recovered data, (5) promote the instance to primary if needed. RPO depends on WAL archival frequency. For Docita: WAL archived every minute to S3, so RPO ≤ 1 minute."

**Q239: **Answer (full):**

Deadlocks occur when two transactions wait for each other's locks. PostgreSQL detects and resolves them automatically, but you need to fix the root cause.

**Diagnosis:**

```sql
-- Check for deadlocks in logs
SHOW log_lock_waits;  -- Enable deadlock logging
SET deadlock_timeout = '1s';

-- Find blocking queries
SELECT
  blocked.pid AS blocked_pid,
  blocked.query AS blocked_query,
  blocking.pid AS blocking_pid,
  blocking.query AS blocking_query,
  age(now(), blocked.query_start) AS waiting_duration
FROM pg_stat_activity blocked
JOIN pg_locks bl ON blocked.pid = bl.pid AND NOT bl.granted
JOIN pg_locks gl ON bl.relation = gl.relation AND gl.granted
JOIN pg_stat_activity blocking ON gl.pid = blocking.pid
WHERE bl.mode != gl.mode;
```

**Common deadlock patterns:**

```
Pattern 1: Foreign key lock
  Transaction A: UPDATE appointments (locks row)
  Transaction B: UPDATE patients (locks row, needs FK lock on appointments)
  → Deadlock

Fix: Consistent lock ordering (always lock parent before child)

Pattern 2: Concurrent updates
  Transaction A: UPDATE appointments WHERE id = 1 (locks row 1)
  Transaction A: UPDATE appointments WHERE id = 2 (locks row 2)
  Transaction B: UPDATE appointments WHERE id = 2 (locks row 2)
  Transaction B: UPDATE appointments WHERE id = 1 (locks row 1)
  → Deadlock

Fix: Use SELECT FOR UPDATE with ORDER BY to enforce consistent ordering
```

**30-second answer:** Check pg_stat_activity for blocked queries. Deadlocks are caused by inconsistent lock ordering. Fix by always locking rows in the same order (e.g., by ID). Use SELECT FOR UPDATE with ORDER BY. Set deadlock_timeout = 1s to log deadlocks quickly.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Diagnosis: (1) check PostgreSQL logs for 'deadlock detected' messages, (2) the log includes the conflicting queries and lock types, (3) use `pg_locks` and `pg_stat_activity` to see current locks. Fix: (1) ensure consistent lock acquisition order across all code paths (patient → practitioner → room), (2) reduce transaction duration (shorter transactions hold locks less time), (3) use SELECT FOR UPDATE with NOWAIT to fail fast instead of waiting. Prevention: always lock resources in the same order."

**Q240: **Answer (full):**

Partitioning splits a large table into smaller, manageable pieces. For appointments, range partitioning by month is ideal.

**Implementation:**

```sql
-- 1. Create partitioned table
CREATE TABLE appointments (
  id UUID DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
  clinic_id UUID NOT NULL,
  practitioner_id UUID NOT NULL,
  patient_id UUID NOT NULL,
  slot_start TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL,
  slot_end TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL,
  status TEXT DEFAULT 'pending',
  created_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT now()
) PARTITION BY RANGE (slot_start);

-- 2. Create monthly partitions
CREATE TABLE appointments_2026_01 PARTITION OF appointments
  FOR VALUES FROM ('2026-01-01') TO ('2026-02-01');
CREATE TABLE appointments_2026_02 PARTITION OF appointments
  FOR VALUES FROM ('2026-02-01') TO ('2026-03-01');
-- ... create future partitions

-- 3. Default partition (catches rows without matching partition)
CREATE TABLE appointments_default PARTITION OF appointments DEFAULT;
```

**Benefits:**

```
Query: WHERE slot_start >= '2026-08-01' AND slot_start < '2026-09-01'
Without partitioning: Scans ALL rows (100K)
With partitioning: Scans only August partition (5K rows)
Speed improvement: 20x

Maintenance: DROP PARTITION appointments_2025_01;
Instead of: DELETE FROM appointments WHERE slot_start < '2026-01-01' (slow)
```

**30-second answer:** Use `PARTITION BY RANGE (slot_start)` with monthly partitions. Create partitions in advance. Use a default partition as a safety net. Partition pruning automatically skips irrelevant partitions. Dropping old data is instant (DROP PARTITION vs slow DELETE).**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Approach: (1) create a partitioned table: `CREATE TABLE appointments (...) PARTITION BY RANGE (slot_start)`, (2) create partitions: `CREATE TABLE appointments_2024_01 PARTITION OF appointments FOR VALUES FROM ('2024-01-01') TO ('2024-02-01')`, (3) create a partition for each month, (4) use pg_partman for automatic partition management. Benefits: queries filtered by date only scan relevant partitions, old partitions can be dropped/archived cheaply. Trade-off: partition pruning must match the query's WHERE clause, cross-partition queries are slower."

**Q241: **Answer (full):**

When VACUUM falls behind, dead tuples accumulate, causing table bloat and performance degradation.

**Diagnosis:**

```sql
-- Check if VACUUM is running
SELECT pid, relid::regclass, phase, heap_blks_vacuumed, heap_blks_total
FROM pg_stat_progress_vacuum;

-- Check dead tuple accumulation
SELECT relname, n_dead_tup, n_live_tup,
  round(n_dead_tup::numeric / nullif(n_live_tup, 0) * 100, 1) AS dead_pct
FROM pg_stat_user_tables
WHERE n_dead_tup > 10000
ORDER BY n_dead_tup DESC;
```

**Fixes:**

```sql
-- 1. Increase autovacuum aggressiveness
ALTER TABLE appointments SET (
  autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor = 0.02,  -- Vacuum at 2% dead (not 20%)
  autovacuum_vacuum_cost_limit = 400,     -- Higher = faster cleanup
  autovacuum_vacuum_cost_delay = 0        -- No throttling (use cautiously)
);

-- 2. Run manual VACUUM (non-blocking)
VACUUM VERBOSE appointments;

-- 3. If severely behind, run VACUUM ANALYZE during off-hours
VACUUM ANALYZE appointments;
```

**30-second answer:** Check `pg_stat_progress_vacuum` for active vacuum. Increase `autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor` and `cost_limit` for hot tables. Run manual VACUUM VERBOSE during off-hours. If severely behind, increase `autovacuum_max_workers` to run more vacuums in parallel.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Symptoms: n_dead_tup growing, table bloat increasing, queries slowing down. Diagnosis: check `pg_stat_user_tables` for dead tuple counts, check autovacuum logs for duration. Fixes: (1) lower autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor for the specific table (e.g., 0.01), (2) increase autovacuum_max_workers, (3) increase autovacuum_naptime, (4) run manual VACUUM VERBOSE to see what's happening, (5) if severe: pg_repack for online table rebuild without long locks."

**Q242: **Answer (full):**

Healthcare workloads are read-heavy (dashboard queries, schedule lookups) with occasional write bursts (booking confirmations).

**Key tuning parameters:**

```sql
-- Memory (assuming 16GB RAM server)
shared_buffers = '4GB'           -- 25% of RAM
effective_cache_size = '12GB'    -- 75% of RAM
work_mem = '64MB'                -- Per-operation memory for sorts/hashes
maintenance_work_mem = '1GB'     -- For VACUUM, CREATE INDEX

-- Query planning
random_page_cost = 1.1           -- SSD (default is 4.0 for HDD)
effective_io_concurrency = 200   -- SSD
default_statistics_target = 100  -- Better query plans

-- Write-ahead log
wal_buffers = '64MB'
checkpoint_completion_target = 0.9
max_wal_size = '2GB'

-- Connections
max_connections = 100            -- Use PgBouncer for more
```

**Index strategy for reads:**

```sql
-- Covering indexes for frequent queries
CREATE INDEX idx_appt_prac_slot_covering
ON appointments (practitioner_id, slot_start)
INCLUDE (patient_id, status, slot_end);

-- Partial indexes for pending appointments
CREATE INDEX idx_appt_pending
ON appointments (practitioner_id, slot_start)
WHERE status = 'pending';
```

**30-second answer:** Tune for reads: `shared_buffers = 25% RAM`, `effective_cache_size = 75% RAM`, `random_page_cost = 1.1` for SSD. Use covering indexes and partial indexes. Set `default_statistics_target = 100` for better query plans. Use PgBouncer for connection pooling.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Tuning: (1) shared_buffers = 25% of RAM (more for read-heavy), (2) effective_cache_size = 75% of RAM, (3) work_mem = 16-64MB for complex patient queries, (4) random_page_cost = 1.1 (SSD), (5) effective_io_concurrency = 200 (SSD), (6) enable index-only scans where possible. Monitoring: pg_stat_user_tables for sequential vs index scan ratio, pg_stat_statements for slow queries. For Docita: read-heavy means more focus on index optimization and connection pooling than write throughput."

**Q243: **Answer (full):**

Unused indexes waste disk space and slow down writes (every INSERT/UPDATE must update the index).

**Detection:**

```sql
-- Find indexes with zero scans
SELECT
  schemaname, relname, indexrelname,
  pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size(indexrelid)) AS index_size,
  idx_scan AS times_used
FROM pg_stat_user_indexes
WHERE idx_scan = 0
  AND indexrelid NOT IN (
    SELECT conindid FROM pg_constraint WHERE contype IN ('p', 'u')
  )  -- Exclude primary key and unique constraint indexes
ORDER BY pg_relation_size(indexrelid) DESC;

-- Also check for rarely-used indexes
SELECT
  indexrelname, idx_scan,
  pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size(indexrelid)) AS size
FROM pg_stat_user_indexes
WHERE idx_scan < 10  -- Used less than 10 times since last stats reset
ORDER BY pg_relation_size(indexrelid) DESC;
```

**Safe drop process:**

```sql
-- 1. Verify the index is truly unused (check for 30 days)
-- 2. Drop concurrently (no lock)
DROP INDEX CONCURRENTLY idx_unused_index;

-- 3. Monitor for 1 week
-- 4. If queries slow down, recreate it
```

**30-second answer:** Query `pg_stat_user_indexes` for `idx_scan = 0`. Exclude primary keys and unique constraints. Drop with `DROP INDEX CONCURRENTLY` (no lock). Monitor for 1 week before confirming. Unused indexes waste disk and slow down writes.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Detection: (1) query pg_stat_user_indexes for idx_scan = 0 (never used), (2) check idx_size to see how much space it consumes, (3) verify the index isn't needed for constraint enforcement (UNIQUE, PRIMARY KEY). Process: (1) mark as unused in monitoring, (2) monitor for 30 days to confirm, (3) drop during low-traffic window, (4) monitor query performance after drop. Benefits: faster writes, less storage, less VACUUM overhead. Caution: some indexes are used by rare but critical queries — check pg_stat_statements before dropping."

**Q244: **Answer (full):**

Column-level encryption protects PHI at rest, ensuring that even database dumps or stolen disk images don't expose sensitive data.

**Implementation with pgcrypto:**

```sql
-- Enable pgcrypto extension
CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS pgcrypto;

-- Encrypt column using AES-256
CREATE TABLE patients (
  id UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
  first_name TEXT NOT NULL,
  last_name TEXT NOT NULL,
  email BYTEA,  -- Encrypted
  phone BYTEA,  -- Encrypted
  insurance_id BYTEA  -- Encrypted
);

-- Insert with encryption
INSERT INTO patients (first_name, last_name, email, phone)
VALUES (
  'John', 'Doe',
  pgp_sym_encrypt('john@example.com', 'encryption_key'),
  pgp_sym_encrypt('+491234567890', 'encryption_key')
);

-- Query with decryption
SELECT first_name, last_name,
  pgp_sym_decrypt(email, 'encryption_key') AS email,
  pgp_sym_decrypt(phone, 'encryption_key') AS phone
FROM patients WHERE id = '...';
```

**Better approach: Application-level encryption:**

```typescript
// Use AWS KMS for key management
import { KMS } from 'aws-sdk';
const kms = new KMS();

async function encryptField(value: string): Promise<string> {
  const result = await kms.encrypt({
    KeyId: 'alias/docita-phi',
    Plaintext: Buffer.from(value),
  }).promise();
  return result.CiphertextBlob.toString('base64');
}
```

**30-second answer:** Use pgcrypto for database-level encryption or AWS KMS for application-level encryption. Store encrypted values as BYTEA or base64 TEXT. Decrypt only when needed (lazy decryption). Use separate encryption keys per tenant for maximum isolation. Key rotation: re-encrypt with new key periodically.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Options: (1) pgcrypto extension: `pgp_sym_encrypt(column, key)` — encrypts at the application level, (2) application-level encryption: encrypt before insert, decrypt after select, (3) Transparent Data Encryption (TDE): encrypts at the storage level (AWS RDS supports this). For Docita: application-level encryption for specific columns (e.g., patient SSN, national ID). Key management: AWS KMS for key storage, rotate keys annually. Trade-off: encrypted columns can't be indexed or queried directly — use a hash column for lookups."

**Q245: **Answer (full):**

Schema migrations on large tables (100M+ rows) require careful planning to avoid locking the table for extended periods.

**Safe migration patterns:**

```sql
-- ✅ SAFE: Add column (instant in PostgreSQL 11+)
ALTER TABLE appointments ADD COLUMN notes TEXT;

-- ✅ SAFE: Add NOT NULL with default (instant in PostgreSQL 11+)
ALTER TABLE appointments ADD COLUMN status_v2 TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'pending';

-- ✅ SAFE: Create index concurrently (no lock)
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY idx_appointments_notes ON appointments (notes);

-- ❌ DANGEROUS: ALTER COLUMN TYPE (locks table, rewrites entire table)
ALTER TABLE appointments ALTER COLUMN slot_start TYPE TIMESTAMPTZ;
-- This locks the table for minutes/hours on large tables
```

**For dangerous migrations (type changes):**

```sql
-- Step 1: Add new column
ALTER TABLE appointments ADD COLUMN slot_start_new TIMESTAMPTZ;

-- Step 2: Backfill in batches (no lock)
UPDATE appointments SET slot_start_new = slot_start::timestamptz
WHERE id IN (SELECT id FROM appointments WHERE slot_start_new IS NULL LIMIT 10000);

-- Step 3: Deploy code that reads from both columns

-- Step 4: Drop old column
ALTER TABLE appointments DROP COLUMN slot_start;
ALTER TABLE appointments RENAME COLUMN slot_start_new TO slot_start;
```

**30-second answer:** Use the expand-contract pattern: add new columns first (instant), backfill in batches, switch reads, drop old columns. Never ALTER COLUMN TYPE on large tables. Use CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY. Test migrations on a production-sized clone first.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Expand/contract: (1) add new column as NULLABLE (no lock on existing rows), (2) deploy code that writes to both old and new column, (3) backfill in batches: `UPDATE table SET new_col = old_col WHERE id BETWEEN ? AND ?`, (4) add NOT NULL constraint after backfill completes, (5) deploy code that reads from new column, (6) drop old column in a later migration. For large tables: batch size 1000 rows, sleep 100ms between batches to avoid overwhelming the database. Use pt-online-schema-change for MySQL, or pg_repack for PostgreSQL."

**Q246: **Answer (full):**

PostgreSQL provides rich monitoring views that reveal performance bottlenecks, lock contention, and resource usage.

**Essential queries:**

```sql
-- 1. Active queries and their duration
SELECT pid, now() - pg_stat_activity.query_start AS duration, query, state
FROM pg_stat_activity
WHERE state != 'idle'
ORDER BY duration DESC;

-- 2. Slow queries (requires pg_stat_statements)
SELECT query, calls, mean_exec_time, total_exec_time
FROM pg_stat_statements
ORDER BY mean_exec_time DESC
LIMIT 10;

-- 3. Table bloat
SELECT relname, n_live_tup, n_dead_tup,
  round(n_dead_tup::numeric / nullif(n_live_tup, 0) * 100, 1) AS dead_pct
FROM pg_stat_user_tables
ORDER BY n_dead_tup DESC;

-- 4. Index usage
SELECT indexrelname, idx_scan, idx_tup_read, idx_tup_fetch
FROM pg_stat_user_indexes
ORDER BY idx_scan ASC;

-- 5. Connection count by state
SELECT state, count(*) FROM pg_stat_activity GROUP BY state;
```

**Dashboard metrics:**

| Metric | Source | Alert Threshold |
|--------|--------|-----------------|
| Connections | pg_stat_activity | > 80% of max |
| Slow queries | pg_stat_statements | > 5s average |
| Dead tuples | pg_stat_user_tables | > 20% dead |
| Cache hit ratio | pg_stat_database | < 99% |
| Replication lag | pg_stat_replication | > 10s |

**30-second answer:** Enable `pg_stat_statements` for query analysis. Monitor connections, slow queries, dead tuples, cache hit ratio, and replication lag. Use Grafana + Prometheus for dashboards. Set alerts for connections > 80%, dead tuples > 20%, cache hit < 99%.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Key metrics: (1) connections: pg_stat_activity (active, idle, idle in transaction), (2) queries: pg_stat_statements (mean time, calls, rows), (3) cache hit ratio: pg_stat_bgwriter (>99% target), (4) replication lag: pg_stat_replication, (5) table bloat: pg_stat_user_tables (n_dead_tup), (6) lock waits: pg_locks. Tools: pganalyze, Datadog PostgreSQL integration, Grafana + postgres_exporter. Alerts: connection pool >80%, cache hit <99%, replication lag >30s, dead tuples >10% of table."

**Q247: **Answer (full):**

Write contention on hot rows (like a popular practitioner's schedule) causes lock waits and reduced throughput.

**The problem:**

```
Dr. Smith (popular practitioner):
- 20 concurrent booking requests per time slot
- Each request: SELECT FOR UPDATE on the same row
- Only 1 succeeds, 19 wait (lock contention)
- Throughput: 20 bookings/30s = 0.67 bookings/second
```

**Solutions:**

```
1. Row-level locking (current approach):
   SELECT FOR UPDATE → 1 row locked → others wait
   Throughput: Low (serial execution)

2. Optimistic locking (version check):
   Read version, compare on update, retry if stale
   Throughput: Medium (retry on conflict)

3. Partition by time slot:
   Each 30-min slot is a separate row
   20 concurrent requests → 20 different rows (no contention)
   Throughput: High (parallel execution)

4. Redis-based pre-check:
   Check availability in Redis first (fast)
   Only hit database if Redis says slot is available
   Reduces database contention by 90%
```

**Optimistic locking implementation:**

```typescript
async function bookAppointment(dto: BookAppointmentDto) {
  const maxRetries = 3;
  for (let attempt = 0; attempt < maxRetries; attempt++) {
    try {
      const result = await prisma.$transaction(async (tx) => {
        // Read with version
        const slot = await tx.slot.findFirst({
          where: { practitionerId: dto.practitionerId, slotStart: dto.slotStart },
        });
        
        if (!slot || slot.status !== 'available') {
          throw new ConflictException('Slot not available');
        }
        
        // Update with version check
        const updated = await tx.slot.updateMany({
          where: { id: slot.id, version: slot.version },
          data: { status: 'booked', version: slot.version + 1 },
        });
        
        if (updated.count === 0) {
          throw new ConflictException('Concurrent modification — retrying');
        }
        
        return slot;
      });
      
      return result; // Success
    } catch (error) {
      if (error instanceof ConflictException && attempt < maxRetries - 1) {
        await new Promise(r => setTimeout(r, 50 * (attempt + 1))); // Backoff
        continue;
      }
      throw error;
    }
  }
}
```

**30-second answer:** For hot rows, use optimistic locking (version check + retry) instead of pessimistic locking (SELECT FOR UPDATE). Alternatively, partition by time slot so concurrent requests hit different rows. Use Redis pre-checks to reduce database contention by 90%. Set max retries = 3 with exponential backoff.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Options: (1) optimistic locking: version column, retry on conflict, (2) partition the lock: lock by time window (hourly locks instead of session-wide), (3) queue writes: serialize writes to the hot row via a queue, (4) denormalize: split the hot row into multiple rows (e.g., morning/afternoon slots). For Docita: if a practitioner's schedule is heavily contested, use optimistic concurrency with version column — the booking check is: `UPDATE slots SET version = version + 1 WHERE id = ? AND version = ?`. If 0 rows affected, retry."

**Q248: **Answer (full):**

Row-Level Security (RLS) ensures that each tenant can only access their own data, enforced at the database level (not just application code).

**Implementation:**

```sql
-- 1. Enable RLS on the table
ALTER TABLE appointments ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY;

-- 2. Create policy
CREATE POLICY tenant_isolation ON appointments
  USING (clinic_id = current_setting('app.current_tenant_id')::uuid);

-- 3. In your application, set the tenant context
SET LOCAL app.current_tenant_id = 'clinic_123';

-- Now all queries automatically filter by clinic_id:
SELECT * FROM appointments;  -- Only returns clinic_123's appointments
```

**Benefits:**

```
1. Defense in depth: Even if application code has a bug,
   the database prevents cross-tenant data leaks

2. Audit compliance: RLS is enforced at the database level,
   making it verifiable by auditors

3. Simpler application code: No need to add WHERE clinic_id = X
   to every query (the policy handles it)
```

**NestJS integration:**

```typescript
// TenantContextInterceptor sets the tenant for each request
@Injectable()
export class TenantContextInterceptor implements NestInterceptor {
  intercept(context: ExecutionContext, next: CallHandler) {
    const request = context.switchToHttp().getRequest();
    const tenantId = request.user.clinicId;
    
    return from(
      this.prisma.$executeRaw`SET LOCAL app.current_tenant_id = ${tenantId}`
    ).pipe(
      switchMap(() => next.handle())
    );
  }
}
```

**30-second answer:** Enable RLS with `ALTER TABLE ... ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY`. Create a policy using `current_setting('app.current_tenant_id')`. Set the tenant context in NestJS interceptor via `SET LOCAL`. This provides database-level tenant isolation as defense-in-depth, even if application code has bugs.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Steps: (1) enable RLS on the table: `ALTER TABLE appointments ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY`, (2) create policy: `CREATE POLICY clinic_isolation ON appointments USING (clinic_id = current_setting('app.current_clinic')::uuid)`, (3) in the API: set the session variable before queries: `SET app.current_clinic = '<clinicId>'`. With Prisma: route RLS-enforced queries through a Prisma transaction that sets the variable. Trade-off: RLS adds connection hold time (transaction must stay open for the variable to persist). At current scale it's fine; at 100x consider connection pooling tuning."

**Q249: **Answer (full):**

Connection pool exhaustion happens when all database connections are in use, causing new requests to queue or fail.

**Symptoms:**

```
- API responses slow down (requests waiting for connections)
- PgBouncer SHOW POOLS shows all connections active
- PostgreSQL: SELECT count(*) FROM pg_stat_activity → 100 (max)
- Error: "FATAL: too many connections" or "connection timeout"
```

**Immediate response:**

```sql
-- 1. Find long-running queries
SELECT pid, now() - query_start AS duration, query, state
FROM pg_stat_activity
WHERE state = 'active'
ORDER BY duration DESC;

-- 2. Kill long-running queries (> 30s)
SELECT pg_terminate_backend(pid)
FROM pg_stat_activity
WHERE state = 'active'
  AND now() - query_start > interval '30 seconds';

-- 3. Kill idle transactions (> 5min)
SELECT pg_terminate_backend(pid)
FROM pg_stat_activity
WHERE state = 'idle in transaction'
  AND state_change < now() - interval '5 minutes';
```

**Prevention:**

```ini
# PgBouncer limits
query_timeout = 30           # Kill queries after 30s
client_idle_timeout = 600    # Disconnect idle clients after 10min
server_idle_timeout = 300    # Return idle connections to pool

# PostgreSQL limits
idle_in_transaction_session_timeout = '30s'  # Kill idle transactions
statement_timeout = '30s'                     # Kill slow queries
```

**30-second answer:** Kill long-running queries with `pg_terminate_backend`. Set `query_timeout = 30` in PgBouncer and `statement_timeout = 30s` in PostgreSQL. Monitor with `pg_stat_activity`. Prevent with proper pool sizing (connections = CPU cores × 2 + 1) and PgBouncer timeouts.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Symptoms: new connections fail, queries queue, latency spikes. Diagnosis: check PgBouncer stats (active connections, waiting clients), check pg_stat_activity for long-running queries or idle-in-transaction. Fixes: (1) kill long-running queries: `SELECT pg_terminate_backend(pid) WHERE state = 'idle in transaction' AND query_start < now() - interval '5 minutes'`, (2) increase PgBouncer pool_size (if PostgreSQL can handle it), (3) reduce connection hold time (shorter transactions), (4) add connection pool monitoring and alerting. Prevention: PgBouncer with query_timeout and client_idle_timeout."

**Q250: **Answer (full):**

WAL archiving enables point-in-time recovery and is critical for HIPAA compliance.

**Setup:**

```ini
# postgresql.conf
wal_level = replica
archive_mode = on
archive_command = 'test ! -f /archive/%f && cp %p /archive/%f'
archive_timeout = 300  # Force archive every 5 minutes
```

**Monitoring:**

```sql
-- Check archiver status
SELECT * FROM pg_stat_archiver;
-- archives_done: Should be increasing
-- last_archived_time: Should be recent
-- failed_count: Should be 0 (if > 0, archiving is broken!)

-- Check WAL retention
SELECT slot_name, wal_status, safe_wal_size
FROM pg_replication_slots;

-- Check archive directory size
-- du -sh /archive/
```

**Alerting:**

```yaml
# Alert if archiving is broken
- alert: WALArchivingBroken
  expr: pg_stat_archiver_failed > 0
  for: 5m
  annotations:
    summary: "WAL archiving is failing — backup integrity at risk"

# Alert if archive is too old
- alert: WALArchiveLag
  expr: time() - pg_stat_archiver_last_archived_time > 600
  for: 5m
  annotations:
    summary: "WAL archive is >10 minutes behind"
```

**30-second answer:** Enable `archive_mode = on` and set `archive_command` to copy WAL segments. Set `archive_timeout = 300` for 5-minute RPO. Monitor `pg_stat_archiver` for failed_count > 0 and stale last_archived_time. Alert immediately if archiving breaks — it means you can't recover to a point in time.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Setup: (1) configure postgresql.conf: `archive_mode = on`, `archive_command = 'aws s3 s3://backups/wal/%f'`, (2) configure WAL retention: `archive_timeout = 300` (archive at least every 5 minutes), (3) verify with `pg_stat_archiver` (archived_count, failed_count). Monitoring: alert on failed_count > 0, alert on archive lag > 10 minutes. Recovery: restore base backup + replay WAL segments. For Docita: WAL archived to S3 every minute, RPO ≤ 1 minute, tested quarterly via DR drills."

---

## Section 14: Node.js/NestJS Internals (15 questions)

**Q251: **Answer (full):**

The event loop is Node.js's concurrency model — it allows single-threaded JavaScript to handle thousands of concurrent operations without threads.

**Event loop phases:**

```
┌───────────────────────────────────────┐
│           Event Loop Phases           │
├───────────────────────────────────────┤
│ 1. Timers       → setTimeout, setInterval callbacks  │
│ 2. Pending      → I/O callbacks (file, network)      │
│ 3. Idle/Prepare → Internal use                         │
│ 4. Poll         → New I/O events, execute callbacks   │
│ 5. Check        → setImmediate callbacks               │
│ 6. Close        → close event callbacks                │
└───────────────────────────────────────┘

After each phase, the event loop processes microtasks:
  - process.nextTick() callbacks
  - Promise.then() callbacks
```

**What blocks the event loop:**

```typescript
// ❌ BLOCKING: Synchronous file read
const data = fs.readFileSync('/large/file.json');  // Blocks for 500ms
// All other requests are queued during this time

// ✅ NON-BLOCKING: Asynchronous file read
const data = await fs.promises.readFile('/large/file.json');  // Doesn't block

// ❌ BLOCKING: CPU-intensive computation
function fibonacci(n) {
  if (n <= 1) return n;
  return fibonacci(n - 1) + fibonacci(n - 2);  // Blocks for seconds
}

// ✅ NON-BLOCKING: Offload to worker thread
const { Worker } = require('worker_threads');
const worker = new Worker('./fibonacci.js', { workerData: 40 });
```

**Impact on Docita:**

NestJS runs on Node.js. A synchronous database query (without await) blocks the event loop, causing all other requests to queue. This is why Prisma queries must always be awaited. For CPU-intensive tasks (report generation, data export), use BullMQ workers to offload from the event loop.

**30-second answer:** The event loop processes I/O callbacks in phases (timers → poll → check → close). Microtasks (Promises) run between phases. Synchronous operations block the entire event loop. Always use async I/O and offload CPU-intensive tasks to worker threads or BullMQ.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"The event loop is a single-threaded loop that processes callbacks. Phases: timers (setTimeout/setInterval) → pending callbacks → idle/prepare → poll (I/O) → check (setImmediate) → close callbacks. A synchronous operation (e.g., JSON.parse on a 10MB payload, or a regex on a long string) blocks the entire event loop — no other requests are processed until it completes. For Docita: keep synchronous operations under 1ms, offload heavy computation to worker threads or the worker service."

**Q252: **Answer (full):**

Backpressure occurs when a readable stream produces data faster than a writable stream can consume it. Without handling, this causes memory to grow unbounded.

**The problem:**

```
Readable stream (file upload):  100 MB/s
Writable stream (database):    10 MB/s
Without backpressure: Buffer grows to 100MB+ → OOM crash

With backpressure:
  Readable pauses when writable buffer is full
  Readable resumes when writable drains
  Memory stays bounded
```

**Implementation:**

```typescript
// ✅ WITH backpressure (pipe)
const readStream = fs.createReadStream('large-file.csv');
const writeStream = fs.createWriteStream('output.csv');
readStream.pipe(writeStream);  // Automatically handles backpressure

// ✅ WITH backpressure (manual)
const readable = getUploadStream();
const writable = getDbWriteStream();

readable.on('data', (chunk) => {
  const canContinue = writable.write(chunk);
  if (!canContinue) {
    readable.pause();  // Stop reading
    writable.once('drain', () => {
      readable.resume();  // Resume when writable is ready
    });
  }
});

// ✅ FOR FILE UPLOADS (NestJS + Multer)
@Post('upload')
async uploadFile(@UploadedFile() file: Express.Multer.File) {
  // Multer already handles backpressure via stream piping
  // It buffers to disk if memory threshold exceeded
}
```

**30-second answer:** Use `.pipe()` for automatic backpressure handling. For manual control, check `writable.write()` return value — if false, pause the readable stream until `drain` event. Never store entire uploads in memory — stream to disk or database.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Backpressure: the consumer is slower than the producer. In Node.js streams: if the readable stream produces data faster than the writable stream consumes it, the internal buffer fills up. The readable stream pauses (emits 'drain' event when buffer is drained). Handling: (1) respect the return value of write() — if false, stop writing until 'drain', (2) use pipe() which handles backpressure automatically, (3) use pipeline() for proper error handling and cleanup. For Docita: streams are used for file uploads — backpressure handling prevents memory exhaustion."

**Q253: "How does NestJS dependency injection work? What are the lifecycle hooks?"**

**Answer skeleton: "**
"NestJS DI: (1) modules declare providers (services), (2) the DI container instantiates providers in dependency order, (3) providers are singletons by default (shared within the module), (4) request-scoped providers create a new instance per request. Lifecycle hooks: onModuleInit, onApplicationBootstrap, onModuleDestroy, beforeApplicationShutdown, onApplicationShutdown. For Docita: TenantContextInterceptor uses request-scoped injection to get the current clinic context."

**Q254: **Answer (full):**

Long-running tasks (report generation, data export, email sending) must not block the NestJS event loop, which would slow down all other requests.

**Strategy 1: BullMQ background jobs:**

```typescript
// Producer (API endpoint)
@Post('reports/generate')
async generateReport(@Body() dto: GenerateReportDto) {
  await this.reportQueue.add('generate', dto, {
    attempts: 3,
    backoff: { type: 'exponential', delay: 5000 },
  });
  return { jobId: 'pending', message: 'Report is being generated' };
}

// Consumer (worker process)
@Processor('reports')
export class ReportProcessor {
  @Process('generate')
  async handleGenerate(job: Job<GenerateReportDto>) {
    // CPU-intensive work runs in worker process
    const data = await this.reportService.generate(job.data);
    await this.storageService.upload(data);
    return { fileUrl: data.url };
  }
}
```

**Strategy 2: Worker threads:**

```typescript
import { Worker } from 'worker_threads';

async function runInWorker(task: string, data: any): Promise<any> {
  return new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
    const worker = new Worker('./worker.js', {
      workerData: { task, data }
    });
    worker.on('message', resolve);
    worker.on('error', reject);
  });
}

// Usage
const result = await runInWorker('generateReport', { clinicId: '123' });
```

**30-second answer:** Use BullMQ for background jobs (report generation, email sending, data export). Use worker threads for CPU-intensive tasks. Never block the event loop with synchronous operations. Return a job ID immediately and let clients poll for completion.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Options: (1) offload to the worker service (outbox pattern — best for Docita), (2) use NestJS Bull module for queue-based processing, (3) use worker_threads for CPU-bound tasks (rare in NestJS), (4) use child_process for truly independent tasks. For Docita: long-running tasks (email sending, document generation) go to the outbox → worker pipeline. The API never blocks on provider calls. The NestJS request handler completes quickly, and the worker processes the task asynchronously."

**Q255: **Answer (full):**

Rate limiting in NestJS is best implemented as a custom interceptor or guard that checks Redis counters.

**Implementation:**

```typescript
// Rate limit decorator
export const RateLimit = (limit: number, windowMs: number) =>
  SetMetadata('rateLimit', { limit, windowMs });

// Rate limit guard
@Injectable()
export class RateLimitGuard implements CanActivate {
  constructor(private redis: Redis) {}

  async canActivate(context: ExecutionContext): Promise<boolean> {
    const reflector = new Reflector();
    const rateLimit = reflector.get<{ limit: number; windowMs: number }>(
      'rateLimit', context.getHandler()
    );
    if (!rateLimit) return true;

    const request = context.switchToHttp().getRequest();
    const tenantId = request.user?.clinicId || request.ip;
    const key = `ratelimit:${tenantId}:${request.route.path}`;

    const current = await this.redis.incr(key);
    if (current === 1) {
      await this.redis.expire(key, Math.ceil(rateLimit.windowMs / 1000));
    }

    if (current > rateLimit.limit) {
      throw new HttpException('Rate limit exceeded', 429);
    }
    return true;
  }
}

// Usage
@Post('appointments')
@RateLimit(10, 60000)  // 10 requests per minute
async create(@Body() dto: CreateAppointmentDto) {
  return this.appointmentService.create(dto);
}
```

**30-second answer:** Implement rate limiting as a NestJS guard using Redis sorted sets or INCR. Apply per-tenant limits via `@RateLimit(limit, windowMs)` decorator. Return 429 with `Retry-After` header when exceeded. Monitor rate limit hits to identify abusive tenants.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Implementation: (1) use @nestjs/throttler package, (2) configure in the module: `ThrottlerModule.forRoot([{ ttl: 60000, limit: 100 }])`, (3) apply globally or per-route with @UseGuards(ThrottlerGuard). Per-tenant rate limiting: (1) create a custom ThrottlerGuard that extracts clinicId from the request, (2) use Redis as the store with key = `ratelimit:{clinicId}:{window}`. For Docita: rate limit by clinic_id, with higher limits for premium clinics."

**Q256: **Answer (full):**

WebSocket connections in NestJS use Socket.IO with room-based routing for multi-tenant, real-time features.

**Implementation:**

```typescript
@WebSocketGateway({ cors: true })
export class AppointmentGateway implements OnGatewayConnection, OnGatewayDisconnect {
  @WebSocketServer()
  server: Server;

  handleConnection(client: Socket) {
    const userId = client.handshake.auth.userId;
    const clinicId = client.handshake.auth.clinicId;
    
    // Join rooms
    client.join(`user:${userId}`);
    client.join(`clinic:${clinicId}`);
    
    // Track online status
    this.redis.sadd(`online:${clinicId}`, userId);
  }

  handleDisconnect(client: Socket) {
    const userId = client.handshake.auth.userId;
    const clinicId = client.handshake.auth.clinicId;
    this.redis.srem(`online:${clinicId}`, userId);
  }

  // Send appointment update to clinic
  notifyAppointmentUpdate(clinicId: string, appointment: Appointment) {
    this.server.to(`clinic:${clinicId}`).emit('appointment:updated', appointment);
  }
}
```

**Scaling with Redis adapter:**

```typescript
import { RedisAdapter } from '@socket.io/redis-adapter';
import { createAdapter } from '@socket.io/redis-adapter';
import { createClient } from 'redis';

const pubClient = createClient({ url: 'redis://localhost:6379' });
const subClient = pubClient.duplicate();

io.adapter(createAdapter(pubClient, subClient));
// Now events are broadcast across all server instances
```

**30-second answer:** Use Socket.IO with NestJS `@WebSocketGateway`. Join rooms per user and clinic. Scale across instances with Redis adapter. Track online status in Redis sets. Handle authentication in `handleConnection` (verify JWT from handshake auth). Emit events to specific rooms for targeted updates.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Implementation: (1) use @nestjs/websockets gateway, (2) @WebSocketGateway decorator on the gateway class, (3) handleConnection for authentication (validate JWT, join clinic room), (4) handleDisconnect for cleanup, (5) @SubscribeMessage for message handling. Clinic-room scoping: `client.join('clinic:${clinicId}')`. Broadcasting: `this.server.to('clinic:${clinicId}').emit('event', data)`. For Docita: WebSocket gateway authenticates on connection, joins the client to their clinic room, and broadcasts appointment changes to all connected clinicians."

**Q257: **Answer (full):**

Request-scoped services create a new instance for each HTTP request, ensuring no data leaks between requests.

**Implementation:**

```typescript
@Injectable()
export class TenantContextService {
  private tenantId: string;

  setTenant(tenantId: string) {
    this.tenantId = tenantId;
  }

  getTenant(): string {
    return this.tenantId;
  }
}

// Register as REQUEST scope
@Module({
  providers: [
    { provide: TenantContextService, scope: Scope.REQUEST }
  ]
})
export class AppModule {}

// Set tenant in interceptor
@Injectable()
export class TenantInterceptor implements NestInterceptor {
  constructor(private tenantService: TenantContextService) {}

  intercept(context: ExecutionContext, next: CallHandler) {
    const request = context.switchToHttp().getRequest();
    this.tenantService.setTenant(request.user.clinicId);
    return next.handle();
  }
}
```

**When to use request scope:**

```
✅ USE request scope for:
  - Tenant context (must be different per request)
  - Request-scoped logging (correlation IDs)
  - Per-request caching (different cache per tenant)

❌ DON'T USE request scope for:
  - Database connections (use connection pool instead)
  - HTTP clients (create once, reuse)
  - Configuration (use ConfigModule)
```

**Performance impact:** Request-scoped services create new instances per request, which adds overhead. Use `Scope.REQUEST` only when you truly need per-request isolation. For most services, the default `Scope.DEFAULT` (singleton) is fine.

**30-second answer:** Use `scope: Scope.REQUEST` for services that must be isolated per request (tenant context, correlation IDs). Set values in an interceptor. Avoid request scope for performance-critical services — it creates new instances per request. Use singleton scope for database connections and configuration.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Request-scoped: a new instance of the service is created for every HTTP request. Implementation: (1) set `scope: Scope.REQUEST` in the provider definition, (2) inject REQUEST to access the current request object. Use case: TenantContextInterceptor stores clinic/user context in the request, and downstream services need access to it. Trade-off: request-scoped providers are more expensive to create (new instance per request). Only use when request-specific data is needed."

**Q258: **Answer (full):**

Graceful shutdown ensures that in-flight requests complete before the process exits, preventing data loss during deployments.

**Implementation:**

```typescript
// main.ts
async function bootstrap() {
  const app = await NestFactory.create(AppModule);
  
  // Enable graceful shutdown
  app.enableShutdownHooks();
  
  // Custom shutdown handler
  const server = app.getHttpServer();
  
  async function gracefulShutdown(signal: string) {
    console.log(`Received ${signal}. Starting graceful shutdown...`);
    
    // Stop accepting new connections
    server.close(() => {
      console.log('HTTP server closed');
    });
    
    // Wait for in-flight requests (max 30s)
    setTimeout(() => {
      console.log('Forcing shutdown after timeout');
      process.exit(1);
    }, 30000);
    
    // Close database connections
    await app.close();
    
    // Close Redis connections
    await redis.quit();
    
    // Close Kafka connections
    await kafka.disconnect();
    
    process.exit(0);
  }
  
  process.on('SIGTERM', () => gracefulShutdown('SIGTERM'));
  process.on('SIGINT', () => gracefulShutdown('SIGINT'));
}
```

**30-second answer:** Call `app.enableShutdownHooks()` in main.ts. Handle SIGTERM/SIGINT by: (1) Stop accepting new connections, (2) Wait for in-flight requests (30s timeout), (3) Close database/Redis/Kafka connections, (4) Exit. This prevents data loss during Kubernetes pod termination.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Implementation: (1) listen for SIGTERM/SIGINT signals, (2) call app.close() which triggers lifecycle hooks (onModuleDestroy, beforeApplicationShutdown), (3) in lifecycle hooks: close database connections, drain queues, finish in-flight requests, (4) set a timeout (e.g., 30 seconds) — if shutdown doesn't complete, force exit. For Docita: graceful shutdown closes Prisma connections, drains the outbox queue, and finishes in-flight API requests. This prevents data loss during deployments."

**Q259: **Answer (full):**

Health checks verify that the application and its dependencies (database, Redis, external services) are operational.

**Implementation:**

```typescript
import { HealthCheckModule, HealthCheckService, TypeOrmHealthIndicator } from '@nestjs/terminus';

@Module({
  imports: [
    HealthCheckModule,
    TerminusModule,
  ],
})
export class AppModule {}

@Controller('health')
export class HealthController {
  constructor(
    private health: HealthCheckService,
    private db: TypeOrmHealthIndicator,
    private redis: RedisHealthIndicator,
  ) {}

  @Get()
  @HealthCheck()
  async check() {
    return this.health.check([
      () => this.db.pingCheck('database', { timeout: 1000 }),
      () => this.redis.pingCheck('redis', { timeout: 1000 }),
      () => this.http.pingCheck('sendgrid', 'https://api.sendgrid.com/v3/ping'),
    ]);
  }
}
```

**Response:**

```json
{
  "status": "ok",
  "info": {
    "database": { "status": "up", "responseTime": 5 },
    "redis": { "status": "up", "responseTime": 2 },
    "sendgrid": { "status": "up", "responseTime": 150 }
  }
}
```

**Kubernetes integration:**

```yaml
livenessProbe:
  httpGet:
    path: /health
    port: 3000
  initialDelaySeconds: 10
  periodSeconds: 30

readinessProbe:
  httpGet:
    path: /health
    port: 3000
  initialDelaySeconds: 5
  periodSeconds: 10
```

**30-second answer:** Use `@nestjs/terminus` for health checks. Check database (ping), Redis (ping), and external services (HTTP ping). Expose `/health` endpoint. Configure Kubernetes liveness and readiness probes to restart unhealthy pods automatically.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Implementation: (1) use @nestjs/terminus package, (2) create a health module with indicators: TypeOrmHealthIndicator (database), HttpHealthIndicator (external services), MemoryHealthIndicator (heap), (3) expose GET /health endpoint. Indicators: database connectivity, PostgreSQL version, disk space, memory usage, queue depth. For Docita: health check verifies PostgreSQL connectivity and outbox queue depth. Kubernetes uses this for liveness/readiness probes."

**Q260: **Answer (full):**

File uploads in NestJS use Multer middleware with configurable storage, size limits, and validation.

**Implementation:**

```typescript
// Multer configuration
@Module({
  imports: [
    MulterModule.register({
      storage: diskStorage({
        destination: './uploads',
        filename: (req, file, cb) => {
          const uniqueName = `${uuid()}-${file.originalname}`;
          cb(null, uniqueName);
        },
      }),
      limits: { fileSize: 10 * 1024 * 1024 },  // 10MB max
      fileFilter: (req, file, cb) => {
        const allowed = ['image/jpeg', 'image/png', 'application/pdf'];
        if (allowed.includes(file.mimetype)) {
          cb(null, true);
        } else {
          cb(new BadRequestException('Invalid file type'), false);
        }
      },
    }),
  ],
})
export class AppModule {}

// Controller
@Post('upload')
@UseInterceptors(FileInterceptor('file'))
async uploadFile(
  @UploadedFile() file: Express.Multer.File,
  @Body() dto: UploadDto,
) {
  // Store metadata in database
  return this.uploadService.create({
    filename: file.filename,
    mimetype: file.mimetype,
    size: file.size,
  });
}
```

**S3 uploads (production):**

```typescript
import { S3Client } from '@aws-sdk/client-s3';
import { S3Storage } from 'multer-s3';

storage: new S3Storage({
  s3: new S3Client({ region: 'eu-central-1' }),
  bucket: 'docita-uploads',
  key: (req, file) => `${uuid()}-${file.originalname}`,
}),
```

**30-second answer:** Use Multer with `FileInterceptor` for file uploads. Configure storage (disk for dev, S3 for production), size limits (10MB), and file type validation. Store file metadata in database. For healthcare, scan uploaded files for malware before storing.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Implementation: (1) use @UseInterceptors(FileInterceptor('file')) on the controller method, (2) validate file type and size in the interceptor or pipe, (3) for large files: stream to storage (S3/R2) using a pre-signed URL pattern (API generates URL, client uploads directly). For Docita: medical document uploads use pre-signed URLs — the API never touches the file bytes. Validation: magic bytes (not just MIME type), max size, file extension whitelist. PHI: never log file contents or names."

**Q261: **Answer (full):**

Caching in NestJS uses the `@nestjs/cache-manager` module with Redis as the backend store.

**Implementation:**

```typescript
@Module({
  imports: [
    CacheModule.register({
      store: redisStore,
      host: 'localhost',
      port: 6379,
      ttl: 30,  // Default 30 seconds
    }),
  ],
})
export class AppModule {}

// Service with caching
@Injectable()
export class ScheduleService {
  constructor(
    @Inject(CACHE_MANAGER) private cache: Cache,
    private prisma: PrismaService,
  ) {}

  async getSchedule(practitionerId: string, date: string) {
    const key = `schedule:${practitionerId}:${date}`;
    
    // Check cache first
    const cached = await this.cache.get(key);
    if (cached) return cached;
    
    // Fetch from database
    const schedule = await this.prisma.appointment.findMany({
      where: { practitionerId, slotStart: { gte: startOfDay(date) } },
    });
    
    // Store in cache
    await this.cache.set(key, schedule, 30);  // 30 second TTL
    return schedule;
  }

  async invalidateSchedule(practitionerId: string) {
    // Delete all cache keys for this practitioner
    const keys = await this.cache.store.keys(`schedule:${practitionerId}:*`);
    await Promise.all(keys.map(k => this.cache.del(k)));
  }
}
```

**30-second answer:** Use `@nestjs/cache-manager` with Redis backend. Cache with `cache.get(key)` / `cache.set(key, value, ttl)`. Invalidate on write with `cache.del(key)`. Use tenant-scoped cache keys to prevent cross-tenant leaks. Set TTL based on data freshness requirements (30s for schedules, 5min for config).**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Implementation: (1) use @nestjs/cache-manager, (2) configure with Redis store, (3) use @UseInterceptors(CacheInterceptor) on routes, (4) set TTL per route. Manual caching: inject CacheService, use `cacheManager.get(key)` / `cacheManager.set(key, value, ttl)`. For Docita: cache practitioner schedules (30s TTL), invalidate on booking/cancellation. Key strategy: `cache:{clinicId}:schedule:{practitionerId}`. Cache-aside pattern: check cache first, miss → query DB → populate cache."

**Q262: **Answer (full):**

NestJS error handling uses exception filters to catch and format errors consistently across the application.

**Global exception filter:**

```typescript
@Catch()
export class AllExceptionsFilter implements ExceptionFilter {
  private readonly logger = new Logger(AllExceptionsFilter.name);

  catch(exception: unknown, host: ArgumentsHost) {
    const ctx = host.switchToHttp();
    const response = ctx.getResponse();
    const request = ctx.getRequest();

    const status = exception instanceof HttpException
      ? exception.getStatus()
      : 500;

    const message = exception instanceof HttpException
      ? exception.getResponse()
      : { message: 'Internal server error' };

    this.logger.error(`${request.method} ${request.url} ${status}`, 
      exception instanceof Error ? exception.stack : '');

    response.status(status).json({
      statusCode: status,
      timestamp: new Date().toISOString(),
      path: request.url,
      message: typeof message === 'string' ? message : message['message'],
    });
  }
}

// Register globally
app.useGlobalFilters(new AllExceptionsFilter());
```

**Custom exception filters per module:**

```typescript
@Catch(PrismaClientKnownRequestError)
export class PrismaExceptionFilter implements ExceptionFilter {
  catch(exception: PrismaClientKnownRequestError, host: ArgumentsHost) {
    const response = host.switchToHttp().getResponse();
    
    switch (exception.code) {
      case 'P2025':
        response.status(404).json({ message: 'Record not found' });
        break;
      case 'P2002':
        response.status(409).json({ message: 'Record already exists' });
        break;
      default:
        response.status(500).json({ message: 'Database error' });
    }
  }
}
```

**30-second answer:** Use global exception filters (`@Catch()`) for consistent error formatting. Create custom filters for specific exceptions (Prisma errors, validation errors). Log all 5xx errors with stack traces. Never leak internal details in production error responses. Use correlation IDs for request tracing.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"NestJS error handling: (1) exception filters catch unhandled exceptions, (2) built-in exceptions (HttpException, NotFoundException, ForbiddenException), (3) custom exception filters for consistent error response format. Implementation: (1) create an AllExceptionsFilter that catches all exceptions, (2) format response: `{ error: { code, message, details, requestId } }`, (3) log the error (without PHI), (4) never expose internal errors to clients. For Docita: the exception filter ensures all errors have the same shape and all PHI is scrubbed from logs."

**Q263: **Answer (full):**

Structured logging produces machine-parseable JSON logs that are easy to search, filter, and aggregate.

**Implementation:**

```typescript
// Use Pino for structured logging
import { LoggerModule } from 'nestjs-pino';

@Module({
  imports: [
    LoggerModule.forRoot({
      pinoHttp: {
        transport: process.env.NODE_ENV === 'development'
          ? { target: 'pino-pretty', options: { colorize: true } }
          : undefined,  // JSON in production
        formatters: {
          level: (label) => ({ level: label }),
        },
        mixin: () => ({
          serviceName: 'docita-api',
          version: process.env.APP_VERSION,
        }),
      },
    }),
  ],
})
export class AppModule {}

// Usage in service
@Injectable()
export class AppointmentService {
  private readonly logger = new Logger(AppointmentService.name);

  async create(dto: CreateAppointmentDto) {
    this.logger.info({ practitionerId: dto.practitionerId, patientId: dto.patientId },
      'Creating appointment');
    
    try {
      const appointment = await this.prisma.appointment.create({ data: dto });
      this.logger.info({ appointmentId: appointment.id }, 'Appointment created');
      return appointment;
    } catch (error) {
      this.logger.error({ error: error.message, dto }, 'Failed to create appointment');
      throw error;
    }
  }
}
```

**30-second answer:** Use Pino for structured JSON logging in NestJS. Include correlation IDs, tenant IDs, and user IDs in every log entry. Log at appropriate levels: info for business events, error for failures, warn for degraded states. Ship logs to Loki or ELK for searching. Scrub PHI from log entries for HIPAA compliance.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Implementation: (1) use NestJS Logger class or a library like pino/winston, (2) structured format: JSON with consistent fields (requestId, userId, clinicId, action, duration, status), (3) log levels: error, warn, info, debug, (4) PHI scrubbing: never log patient names, appointment details, or medical data. For Docita: the logger includes requestId for correlation, clinicId for tenant context, and userId for audit. Sentry integration: the Sentry scrubber removes PHI from error reports before sending."

**Q264: **Answer (full):**

Authentication in NestJS uses guards and decorators to protect routes and extract user identity.

**JWT authentication guard:**

```typescript
@Injectable()
export class JwtAuthGuard extends AuthGuard('jwt') {
  canActivate(context: ExecutionContext) {
    return super.canActivate(context);
  }

  handleRequest(err: any, user: any, info: any) {
    if (err || !user) {
      throw new UnauthorizedException('Invalid or expired token');
    }
    return user;
  }
}

// JWT strategy
@Injectable()
export class JwtStrategy extends PassportStrategy(Strategy) {
  constructor() {
    super({
      jwtFromRequest: ExtractJwt.fromAuthHeaderAsBearerToken(),
      ignoreExpiration: false,
      secretOrKey: process.env.JWT_SECRET,
    });
  }

  async validate(payload: any) {
    return {
      userId: payload.sub,
      email: payload.email,
      clinicId: payload.clinicId,
      role: payload.role,
    };
  }
}

// Usage
@UseGuards(JwtAuthGuard)
@Get('appointments')
async findAll(@Request() req) {
  return this.appointmentService.findAll(req.user.clinicId);
}
```

**30-second answer:** Use `@UseGuards(JwtAuthGuard)` on protected routes. Extract user from JWT payload in `JwtStrategy.validate()`. Include clinicId and role in the JWT payload. Use `@CurrentUser()` decorator to access user in handlers. Never trust client-provided tenant IDs — always extract from JWT.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Implementation: (1) create an AuthGuard that validates JWT, (2) extract token from Authorization header, (3) verify signature and expiry, (4) attach decoded payload to the request object, (5) apply globally: `app.useGlobalGuards(new AuthGuard())` or per-route with @UseGuards(AuthGuard). For Docita: the AuthGuard validates the JWT, extracts { userId, clinicId, role }, and attaches it to the request. The TenantContextInterceptor then uses this to set the clinic context for the request."

**Q265: **Answer (full):**

Background jobs in NestJS use BullMQ with Redis for reliable, delayed, and retryable job processing.

**Implementation:**

```typescript
// Producer (in API service)
@Injectable()
export class NotificationService {
  constructor(@InjectQueue('notifications') private queue: Queue) {}

  async sendAppointmentReminder(appointment: Appointment) {
    await this.queue.add('appointment-reminder', {
      appointmentId: appointment.id,
      patientEmail: appointment.patient.email,
      practitionerName: appointment.practitioner.name,
      slotStart: appointment.slotStart,
    }, {
      delay: 24 * 60 * 60 * 1000,  // 24 hours before appointment
      attempts: 3,
      backoff: { type: 'exponential', delay: 5000 },
      removeOnComplete: true,
    });
  }
}

// Consumer (in worker process)
@Processor('notifications')
export class NotificationProcessor {
  constructor(private emailService: EmailService) {}

  @Process('appointment-reminder')
  async handleReminder(job: Job) {
    await this.emailService.send({
      to: job.data.patientEmail,
      template: 'appointment-reminder',
      data: {
        practitionerName: job.data.practitionerName,
        slotStart: job.data.slotStart,
      },
    });
  }

  @OnQueueFailed()
  async onFailed(job: Job, error: Error) {
    this.logger.error(`Job ${job.id} failed: ${error.message}`);
  }
}
```

**30-second answer:** Use BullMQ with Redis for background jobs. Producer adds jobs to queues with delay, attempts, and backoff config. Consumer processes jobs in a separate worker process. Use `@Process()` decorator for job handlers. Monitor failed jobs with `@OnQueueFailed()`. Remove completed jobs with `removeOnComplete: true`.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Options: (1) outbox pattern (recommended for Docita): API writes outbox event, worker processes it, (2) Bull module: NestJS integration with Bull queue (Redis-backed), (3) @nestjs/bull for Redis-based queues, (4) custom worker process that polls a database table. For Docita: the outbox pattern is preferred because it's transactional (event is written in the same transaction as the business data) and doesn't require Redis. The worker polls the outbox table, processes events, and marks them as completed."
