## Section 15: React/Frontend Advanced (15 questions)

**Q266: **Answer (full):**

React reconciliation (diffing) is the algorithm that determines which parts of the DOM need to update when state changes.

**How it works:**

```
1. State changes → React calls the component function again
2. React compares the new virtual DOM tree with the previous one
3. React identifies which nodes changed (diffing)
4. React applies only the minimal set of real DOM updates (committing)

The diffing algorithm (O(n) not O(n³)):
  - Different component types → Unmount old, mount new
  - Same type → Update only changed props
  - Keys help React identify which list items moved/added/removed
```

**Impact on Docita:**

```typescript
// ❌ BAD: No key → React can't track which appointment moved
{appointments.map(apt => <AppointmentCard apt={apt} />)}

// ✅ GOOD: Stable key → React efficiently reorders
{appointments.map(apt => <AppointmentCard key={apt.id} apt={apt} />)}

// ❌ BAD: New object reference → unnecessary re-render
const style = { color: 'red' };  // Created every render
return <div style={style}>...</div>;

// ✅ GOOD: Stable reference → no re-render
const style = useMemo(() => ({ color: 'red' }), []);
```

**30-second answer:** React compares old and new virtual DOM trees to find minimal changes. Different types = unmount/remount. Same type = update props only. Keys help track list items. Use stable keys (IDs, not indices), memoize expensive computations, and avoid creating new objects in render.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Reconciliation (diffing): React compares the new virtual DOM tree with the previous one. Algorithm: (1) different element types → destroy old tree, build new, (2) same type → update properties, (3) keys help React identify which items changed, were added, or removed. State change: (1) setState triggers re-render, (2) React creates new virtual DOM, (3) diffs with previous, (4) applies minimal DOM updates. Optimization: React.memo, useMemo, useCallback prevent unnecessary re-renders. For Docita: the calendar view uses React.memo to prevent re-rendering all appointments when one changes."

**Q267: **Answer (full):**

Memory leaks in React happen when components subscribe to events or timers but don't clean up on unmount.

**Common causes:**

```typescript
// ❌ MEMORY LEAK: Event listener not removed
useEffect(() => {
  window.addEventListener('resize', handleResize);
  // Missing cleanup!
}, []);

// ✅ CLEANUP: Remove event listener
useEffect(() => {
  window.addEventListener('resize', handleResize);
  return () => window.removeEventListener('resize', handleResize);
}, []);

// ❌ MEMORY LEAK: Interval not cleared
useEffect(() => {
  setInterval(fetchData, 5000);
}, []);

// ✅ CLEANUP: Clear interval
useEffect(() => {
  const interval = setInterval(fetchData, 5000);
  return () => clearInterval(interval);
}, []);

// ❌ MEMORY LEAK: Abort controller not aborted
useEffect(() => {
  fetch('/api/data').then(res => res.json());
}, []);

// ✅ CLEANUP: Abort on unmount
useEffect(() => {
  const controller = new AbortController();
  fetch('/api/data', { signal: controller.signal })
    .then(res => res.json());
  return () => controller.abort();
}, []);
```

**30-second answer:** Always return a cleanup function from useEffect that removes event listeners, clears intervals, and aborts fetch requests. Use AbortController for API calls. Use the React DevTools Profiler to detect components that mount/unmount frequently without cleanup.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Common leaks: (1) event listeners not removed in useEffect cleanup, (2) timers not cleared, (3) subscriptions not unsubscribed, (4) large objects retained in state after unmount. Detection: Chrome DevTools → Memory → take heap snapshot → compare before/after navigation. Fix: (1) return cleanup function from useEffect: `return () => { removeEventListener(...) }`, (2) use AbortController for fetch requests, (3) use WeakRef for large cached objects. For Docita: the WebSocket connection must be cleaned up on component unmount to prevent memory leaks."

**Q268: **Answer (full):**

Optimistic updates show the new state immediately (before the server confirms), then revert if the request fails.

**Implementation:**

```typescript
const useUpdateAppointment = () => {
  const queryClient = useQueryClient();

  return useMutation({
    mutationFn: (dto: UpdateAppointmentDto) =>
      api.patch(`/appointments/${dto.id}`, dto),

    onMutate: async (newData) => {
      // Cancel outgoing refetches
      await queryClient.cancelQueries({ queryKey: ['appointments'] });

      // Snapshot previous value
      const previous = queryClient.getQueryData(['appointments']);

      // Optimistically update
      queryClient.setQueryData(['appointments'], (old: Appointment[]) =>
        old.map(apt => apt.id === newData.id ? { ...apt, ...newData } : apt)
      );

      return { previous };
    },

    onError: (err, newData, context) => {
      // Rollback on error
      queryClient.setQueryData(['appointments'], context.previous);
      toast.error('Failed to update appointment');
    },

    onSettled: () => {
      // Always refetch after error or success
      queryClient.invalidateQueries({ queryKey: ['appointments'] });
    },
  });
};
```

**30-second answer:** Use `onMutate` to snapshot current data, optimistically update the cache, return previous state in context. Use `onError` to rollback to previous state. Use `onSettled` to refetch for consistency. This gives instant UI feedback while the server processes the request.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Implementation: (1) in useMutation's onMutate, save the current cache, (2) optimistically update the cache with the expected result, (3) if the mutation fails, roll back to the saved cache via onError, (4) on success, invalidate the query to fetch the real data. Example: booking an appointment — immediately add it to the calendar view, then roll back if the API returns 409. For Docita: optimistic updates make the UI feel instant, but the real data from the API is the source of truth."

**Q269: **Answer (full):**

Real-time data in React must be handled carefully to avoid re-rendering the entire component tree on every update.

**Strategy 1: WebSocket with targeted updates:**

```typescript
function AppointmentList() {
  const [appointments, setAppointments] = useState([]);

  useEffect(() => {
    const socket = io('wss://api.docita.de');
    
    socket.on('appointment:updated', (updated: Appointment) => {
      setAppointments(prev =>
        prev.map(apt => apt.id === updated.id ? updated : apt)
      );
    });

    return () => socket.disconnect();
  }, []);

  return appointments.map(apt => <AppointmentCard key={apt.id} apt={apt} />);
}
```

**Strategy 2: TanStack Query with WebSocket:**

```typescript
const useRealtimeAppointments = () => {
  const queryClient = useQueryClient();

  useEffect(() => {
    const socket = io('wss://api.docita.de');
    
    socket.on('appointment:updated', (updated) => {
      queryClient.setQueryData(
        ['appointment', updated.id],
        updated
      );
    });

    return () => socket.disconnect();
  }, []);
};
```

**Strategy 3: Memoized components:**

```typescript
// Memoize child components to prevent unnecessary re-renders
const AppointmentCard = React.memo(({ appointment }) => {
  return <div>{appointment.patientName} - {appointment.slotStart}</div>;
});
// Only re-renders if appointment prop changes
```

**30-second answer:** Use WebSocket with TanStack Query for real-time updates. Update only the specific query that changed (not all data). Memoize child components with React.memo. Use stable keys in lists. Debounce rapid updates to avoid excessive re-renders.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Strategies: (1) WebSocket → update TanStack Query cache via `setQueryData`, (2) use React.memo to prevent unnecessary re-renders, (3) use useCallback for event handlers, (4) use useRef for mutable values that don't trigger re-renders, (5) batch state updates. For Docita: the calendar view receives WebSocket events and updates only the affected appointment via `queryClient.setQueryData`. This avoids re-rendering the entire calendar."

**Q270: **Answer (full):**

Zod provides type-safe schema validation that integrates with React Hook Form for performant form handling.

**Implementation:**

```typescript
import { z } from 'zod';
import { useForm } from 'react-hook-form';
import { zodResolver } from '@hookform/resolvers/zod';

// Define schema
const bookingSchema = z.object({
  patientId: z.string().uuid('Invalid patient ID'),
  practitionerId: z.string().uuid('Invalid practitioner ID'),
  slotStart: z.string().datetime('Invalid date format'),
  notes: z.string().max(500, 'Notes must be under 500 characters').optional(),
});

type BookingFormData = z.infer<typeof bookingSchema>;

// Form component
function BookingForm() {
  const { register, handleSubmit, formState: { errors } } = useForm<BookingFormData>({
    resolver: zodResolver(bookingSchema),
  });

  const onSubmit = async (data: BookingFormData) => {
    await api.post('/appointments', data);
  };

  return (
    <form onSubmit={handleSubmit(onSubmit)}>
      <input {...register('patientId')} />
      {errors.patientId && <span>{errors.patientId.message}</span>}
      
      <input {...register('slotStart')} />
      {errors.slotStart && <span>{errors.slotStart.message}</span>}
      
      <button type="submit">Book Appointment</button>
    </form>
  );
}
```

**30-second answer:** Define schemas with Zod (`z.object({...})`). Use `zodResolver` with React Hook Form. Type inference gives you TypeScript types from the schema. Validate on submit and on blur. Show error messages below each field. Server-side validation is still needed as defense-in-depth.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Implementation: (1) define schema with Zod: `const schema = z.object({ name: z.string().min(1), email: z.string().email() })`, (2) use react-hook-form with zodResolver: `useForm({ resolver: zodResolver(schema) })`, (3) display errors: `errors.name?.message`. Benefits: type-safe validation, shared schemas between frontend and backend (Zod works in NestJS too), real-time validation on blur. For Docita: Zod schemas are shared between the API (DTO validation) and the frontend (form validation), ensuring consistent validation rules."

**Q271: **Answer (full):**

Authentication state in a React SPA must handle token storage, refresh, and route protection.

**Implementation:**

```typescript
// Auth context
const AuthContext = createContext<AuthState | null>(null);

export function AuthProvider({ children }) {
  const [user, setUser] = useState<User | null>(null);
  const [token, setToken] = useState<string | null>(
    localStorage.getItem('token')
  );

  useEffect(() => {
    if (token) {
      // Validate token and fetch user profile
      api.get('/auth/me', { headers: { Authorization: `Bearer ${token}` } })
        .then(res => setUser(res.data))
        .catch(() => {
          localStorage.removeItem('token');
          setToken(null);
        });
    }
  }, [token]);

  const login = async (email: string, password: string) => {
    const res = await api.post('/auth/login', { email, password });
    localStorage.setItem('token', res.data.token);
    setToken(res.data.token);
    setUser(res.data.user);
  };

  const logout = () => {
    localStorage.removeItem('token');
    setToken(null);
    setUser(null);
  };

  return (
    <AuthContext.Provider value={{ user, token, login, logout }}>
      {children}
    </AuthContext.Provider>
  );
}
```

**30-second answer:** Store JWT in localStorage (or httpOnly cookie for better security). Validate token on mount by calling `/auth/me`. Provide login/logout functions via Context. Use `useAuth()` hook in components. Redirect to login on 401 responses via Axios interceptor.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Implementation: (1) store JWT in memory (not localStorage for security), (2) use a context provider (AuthContext) that holds the current user, (3) on mount, check for refresh token (httpOnly cookie), (4) if valid, fetch user profile and set auth state, (5) if invalid, redirect to login. Token refresh: axios interceptor detects 401, calls /auth/refresh, retries the original request. For Docita: the AuthProvider wraps the entire app, provides useAuth() hook, and handles token refresh transparently."

**Q272: **Answer (full):**

Route guards protect routes that require authentication or specific roles.

**Implementation:**

```typescript
// Protected route wrapper
function ProtectedRoute({ children, requiredRole }: { children: ReactNode, requiredRole?: string }) {
  const { user, token } = useAuth();
  
  if (!token) {
    return <Navigate to="/login" replace />;
  }
  
  if (requiredRole && user.role !== requiredRole) {
    return <Navigate to="/unauthorized" replace />;
  }
  
  return children;
}

// Route configuration
function AppRoutes() {
  return (
    <Routes>
      <Route path="/login" element={<LoginPage />} />
      <Route path="/unauthorized" element={<UnauthorizedPage />} />
      
      {/* Protected routes */}
      <Route path="/dashboard" element={
        <ProtectedRoute>
          <Dashboard />
        </ProtectedRoute>
      } />
      
      <Route path="/admin/*" element={
        <ProtectedRoute requiredRole="admin">
          <AdminLayout />
        </ProtectedRoute>
      } />
    </Routes>
  );
}
```

**30-second answer:** Create a `ProtectedRoute` component that checks authentication and role. Wrap protected routes with this component. Redirect to `/login` if unauthenticated, `/unauthorized` if wrong role. Use React Router v8's `<Navigate>` for programmatic navigation.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Implementation: (1) create a ProtectedRoute component that checks auth state, (2) if not authenticated, redirect to /login, (3) wrap routes: `<Route element={<ProtectedRoute><Dashboard /></ProtectedRoute>} />`. Role-based guards: (1) check user.role against required role, (2) if insufficient role, redirect to /unauthorized. For Docita: ProtectedRoute checks authentication, RoleGuard checks role-based access, and ClinicGuard ensures the user belongs to the current clinic."

**Q273: **Answer (full):**

Error boundaries catch JavaScript errors in child components and display a fallback UI instead of crashing the entire app.

**Implementation:**

```typescript
class ErrorBoundary extends React.Component<
  { children: ReactNode; fallback?: ReactNode },
  { hasError: boolean; error?: Error }
> {
  state = { hasError: false, error: undefined };

  static getDerivedStateFromError(error: Error) {
    return { hasError: true, error };
  }

  componentDidCatch(error: Error, errorInfo: React.ErrorInfo) {
    // Log to Sentry
    Sentry.captureException(error, { extra: errorInfo });
  }

  render() {
    if (this.state.hasError) {
      return this.props.fallback || (
        <div className="error-boundary">
          <h2>Something went wrong</h2>
          <p>{this.state.error?.message}</p>
          <button onClick={() => this.setState({ hasError: false })}>
            Try again
          </button>
        </div>
      );
    }
    return this.props.children;
  }
}

// Usage
<ErrorBoundary fallback={<BookingError />}>
  <BookingCalendar />
</ErrorBoundary>
```

**30-second answer:** Create a class component with `getDerivedStateFromError` and `componentDidCatch`. Wrap components that might fail (data fetching, complex renders). Log errors to Sentry. Show a fallback UI with a "Try again" button. Error boundaries don't catch event handler errors — use try/catch there.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Implementation: (1) create a class component with componentDidCatch and getDerivedStateFromError, (2) render a fallback UI when an error is caught, (3) wrap routes in error boundaries: `<ErrorBoundary><Route ... /></ErrorBoundary>`. Per-route boundaries: different fallback UIs for different sections (calendar vs patient records). Logging: send error to Sentry in componentDidCatch. For Docita: error boundaries prevent one component crash from taking down the entire dashboard."

**Q274: **Answer (full):**

Infinite scrolling loads more data as the user scrolls, replacing traditional pagination.

**Implementation with TanStack Query:**

```typescript
function AppointmentList() {
  const {
    data,
    fetchNextPage,
    hasNextPage,
    isFetchingNextPage,
  } = useInfiniteQuery({
    queryKey: ['appointments'],
    queryFn: ({ pageParam = 0 }) =>
      api.get(`/appointments?cursor=${pageParam}&limit=20`),
    getNextPageParam: (lastPage) => lastPage.data.nextCursor,
  });

  // Intersection Observer for scroll detection
  const observer = useRef<IntersectionObserver>();
  const lastElementRef = useCallback((node: HTMLDivElement) => {
    if (isFetchingNextPage) return;
    if (observer.current) observer.current.disconnect();
    
    observer.current = new IntersectionObserver(entries => {
      if (entries[0].isIntersecting && hasNextPage) {
        fetchNextPage();
      }
    });
    
    if (node) observer.current.observe(node);
  }, [isFetchingNextPage, hasNextPage, fetchNextPage]);

  return (
    <div>
      {data?.pages.map(page =>
        page.data.items.map((apt, i) => (
          <div
            key={apt.id}
            ref={i === page.data.items.length - 1 ? lastElementRef : null}
          >
            <AppointmentCard apt={apt} />
          </div>
        ))
      )}
      {isFetchingNextPage && <Spinner />}
    </div>
  );
}
```

**30-second answer:** Use `useInfiniteQuery` from TanStack Query with cursor-based pagination. Use IntersectionObserver to detect when the user scrolls near the bottom. Call `fetchNextPage()` to load more data. Show a loading spinner while fetching. The observer ref on the last element triggers the next page load.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Implementation: (1) use TanStack Query's useInfiniteQuery, (2) define query function that accepts pageParam, (3) use Intersection Observer to detect when the user scrolls to the bottom, (4) fetch next page and append to the list. Key: (1) use cursor-based pagination (not offset), (2) handle loading state (show spinner at bottom), (3) handle empty state (no more data). For Docita: the patient list uses infinite scrolling with cursor pagination — each page returns 50 patients, and the cursor is the last patient's ID."

**Q275: **Answer (full):**

Modern React provides several alternatives to Redux for complex state management.

**Strategy 1: TanStack Query for server state:**

```typescript
// Server state (data from API) — use TanStack Query
const { data: appointments } = useQuery({
  queryKey: ['appointments', clinicId],
  queryFn: () => api.get(`/appointments?clinicId=${clinicId}`),
});

// Mutations with cache invalidation
const createAppointment = useMutation({
  mutationFn: (dto) => api.post('/appointments', dto),
  onSuccess: () => queryClient.invalidateQueries({ queryKey: ['appointments'] }),
});
```

**Strategy 2: Zustand for client state:**

```typescript
import { create } from 'zustand';

interface UIState {
  sidebarOpen: boolean;
  selectedAppointment: string | null;
  toggleSidebar: () => void;
  selectAppointment: (id: string | null) => void;
}

const useUIStore = create<UIState>((set) => ({
  sidebarOpen: true,
  selectedAppointment: null,
  toggleSidebar: () => set((s) => ({ sidebarOpen: !s.sidebarOpen })),
  selectAppointment: (id) => set({ selectedAppointment: id }),
}));

// Usage
function Sidebar() {
  const { sidebarOpen, toggleSidebar } = useUIStore();
  return sidebarOpen ? <nav>...</nav> : null;
}
```

**30-second answer:** Use TanStack Query for server state (API data, caching, mutations). Use Zustand for simple client state (UI toggles, selected items). Use React Context for infrequently changing global state (theme, language). Avoid Redux for most apps — it's overkill for typical SPAs.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Strategies: (1) React Context for global state (auth, theme), (2) TanStack Query for server state (cache, invalidation), (3) local state (useState) for UI state (modals, form inputs), (4) URL state (search params) for filterable lists, (5) Zustand for simple global state if Context is insufficient. For Docita: auth → Context, server data → TanStack Query, UI state → useState, URL filters → search params. Redux is overkill for most SPAs — use the simplest tool that works."

**Q276: **Answer (full):**

Accessible forms ensure that all users, including those using screen readers, can interact with form elements.

**Implementation:**

```tsx
function BookingForm() {
  return (
    <form onSubmit={handleSubmit}>
      {/* Label associated with input via htmlFor */}
      <label htmlFor="patient-name">Patient Name</label>
      <input
        id="patient-name"
        type="text"
        aria-required="true"
        aria-invalid={errors.patientName ? 'true' : 'false'}
        aria-describedby={errors.patientName ? 'patient-name-error' : undefined}
      />
      {errors.patientName && (
        <span id="patient-name-error" role="alert">
          {errors.patientName.message}
        </span>
      )}

      {/* Fieldset for related inputs */}
      <fieldset>
        <legend>Appointment Time</legend>
        <input type="datetime-local" aria-label="Start time" />
        <input type="datetime-local" aria-label="End time" />
      </fieldset>

      {/* Live region for form status */}
      <div aria-live="polite" aria-atomic="true">
        {isSubmitting && 'Submitting...'}
        {isSuccess && 'Appointment booked!'}
      </div>

      <button type="submit" disabled={isSubmitting}>
        {isSubmitting ? 'Booking...' : 'Book Appointment'}
      </button>
    </form>
  );
}
```

**30-second answer:** Use `<label htmlFor>` for every input. Add `aria-required`, `aria-invalid`, and `aria-describedby` for validation. Use `role="alert"` for error messages. Use `aria-live="polite"` for form status updates. Test with screen readers (VoiceOver, NVDA). Use semantic HTML (`fieldset`, `legend`) for related inputs.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Requirements: (1) every input has a label (htmlFor + id), (2) error messages are associated with inputs (aria-describedby), (3) required fields have aria-required, (4) fieldsets group related inputs, (5) keyboard navigation follows logical order, (6) focus management on error. Implementation: (1) use semantic HTML (label, fieldset, legend), (2) use aria-live for dynamic error messages, (3) test with keyboard-only navigation, (4) test with screen reader (VoiceOver/NVDA). For Docita: the booking form follows WCAG 2.1 AA — every input is labeled, errors are announced, and the form is fully keyboard-navigable."

**Q277: **Answer (full):**

Offline support uses a Service Worker to cache assets and API responses, allowing the app to work without network connectivity.

**Implementation:**

```typescript
// Register service worker
if ('serviceWorker' in navigator) {
  navigator.serviceWorker.register('/sw.js');
}

// Service Worker (sw.js)
self.addEventListener('install', (event) => {
  event.waitUntil(
    caches.open('docita-v1').then((cache) =>
      cache.addAll(['/', '/index.html', '/app.js', '/styles.css'])
    )
  );
});

self.addEventListener('fetch', (event) => {
  // Network-first for API calls
  if (event.request.url.includes('/api/')) {
    event.respondWith(
      fetch(event.request).catch(() =>
        caches.match(event.request)
      )
    );
  } else {
    // Cache-first for static assets
    event.respondWith(
      caches.match(event.request).then((response) =>
        response || fetch(event.request)
      )
    );
  }
});
```

**30-second answer:** Register a Service Worker to cache static assets (cache-first) and API responses (network-first with cache fallback). Use Workbox for production-ready service workers. Show an offline indicator when `navigator.onLine` is false. Queue offline actions and sync when back online.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Implementation: (1) service worker caches the SPA shell (HTML, JS, CSS), (2) on fetch: return cached SPA if offline, (3) API calls fail gracefully (show offline banner), (4) use IndexedDB for local data persistence (if needed). Limitations: (1) no offline mutations (can't book an appointment offline), (2) no conflict resolution, (3) cached data may be stale. For Docita: the service worker caches the SPA shell so the dashboard loads offline, but API calls require connectivity. The offline page explains that certain features require an internet connection."

**Q278: **Answer (full):**

File uploads with progress tracking use XMLHttpRequest or Axios for progress events.

**Implementation:**

```typescript
function FileUpload() {
  const [progress, setProgress] = useState(0);
  const [uploading, setUploading] = useState(false);

  const handleUpload = async (file: File) => {
    const formData = new FormData();
    formData.append('file', file);

    setUploading(true);
    
    await axios.post('/api/upload', formData, {
      onUploadProgress: (progressEvent) => {
        const percent = Math.round(
          (progressEvent.loaded * 100) / progressEvent.total!
        );
        setProgress(percent);
      },
    });

    setUploading(false);
    setProgress(0);
  };

  return (
    <div>
      <input type="file" onChange={(e) => handleUpload(e.target.files[0])} />
      {uploading && (
        <div>
          <progress value={progress} max={100} />
          <span>{progress}%</span>
        </div>
      )}
    </div>
  );
}
```

**30-second answer:** Use Axios `onUploadProgress` callback to track upload percentage. Show a progress bar during upload. Disable the upload button while uploading. Handle errors with try/catch. For large files, consider chunked uploads with resumable upload protocols (tus protocol).**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Implementation: (1) use XMLHttpRequest or fetch with ReadableStream for progress tracking, (2) for pre-signed URL uploads: client uploads directly to S3, progress is tracked via XMLHttpRequest.onprogress, (3) display progress bar with percentage. For large files: (1) chunk the file (split into 5MB pieces), (2) upload chunks in parallel, (3) combine on the server. For Docita: medical document uploads use pre-signed URLs with progress tracking — the user sees a progress bar while uploading, and the upload is resumable if interrupted."

**Q279: **Answer (full):**

Internationalization (i18n) enables the app to display content in multiple languages.

**Implementation with react-i18next:**

```typescript
import { useTranslation } from 'react-i18next';

// Translation files
// en.json: { "appointment": { "book": "Book Appointment", "cancel": "Cancel" } }
// de.json: { "appointment": { "book": "Termin buchen", "cancel": "Stornieren" } }

function BookingButton() {
  const { t } = useTranslation();
  return <button>{t('appointment.book')}</button>;
}

// Language switcher
function LanguageSwitcher() {
  const { i18n } = useTranslation();
  return (
    <select onChange={(e) => i18n.changeLanguage(e.target.value)}>
      <option value="en">English</option>
      <option value="de">Deutsch</option>
    </select>
  );
}
```

**30-second answer:** Use `react-i18next` for translations. Store translations in JSON files per language. Use `t('key')` for translated strings. Add a language switcher component. Handle pluralization with `t('key', { count: 5 })`. For healthcare, ensure medical terminology is accurately translated.**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Implementation: (1) use react-i18next, (2) define translation files (en.json, de.json), (3) use useTranslation hook: `const { t } = useTranslation(); return <h1>{t('dashboard.title')}</h1>`, (4) configure i18next with fallback language and interpolation. Backend: nestjs-i18n for API error messages. Database: translation tables for patient-facing content. For Docita: i18n would be added for UI labels, email templates, and error messages. Priority: English first, then German, then other languages based on clinic needs."

**Q280: **Answer (full):**

Drag-and-drop in React is best implemented with `@dnd-kit` for accessible, performant drag interactions.

**Implementation:**

```typescript
import { DndContext, closestCenter } from '@dnd-kit/core';
import { SortableContext, verticalListSortingStrategy, useSortable } from '@dnd-kit/sortable';

function SortableAppointment({ appointment }) {
  const { attributes, listeners, setNodeRef, transform } = useSortable({ id: appointment.id });
  
  return (
    <div ref={setNodeRef} {...attributes} {...listeners}>
      {appointment.patientName} - {appointment.slotStart}
    </div>
  );
}

function AppointmentList({ appointments, onReorder }) {
  const handleDragEnd = (event) => {
    const { active, over } = event;
    if (active.id !== over.id) {
      const oldIndex = appointments.findIndex(a => a.id === active.id);
      const newIndex = appointments.findIndex(a => a.id === over.id);
      onReorder(arrayMove(appointments, oldIndex, newIndex));
    }
  };

  return (
    <DndContext collisionDetection={closestCenter} onDragEnd={handleDragEnd}>
      <SortableContext items={appointments.map(a => a.id)} strategy={verticalListSortingStrategy}>
        {appointments.map(apt => <SortableAppointment key={apt.id} appointment={apt} />)}
      </SortableContext>
    </DndContext>
  );
}
```

**30-second answer:** Use `@dnd-kit` for drag-and-drop (better than react-beautiful-dnd which is unmaintained). Wrap items in `DndContext` and `SortableContext`. Use `useSortable` hook for each draggable item. Handle `onDragEnd` to reorder. Ensure keyboard accessibility (Space/Arrow keys to move items).**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Implementation: (1) use @dnd-kit/core (modern, accessible), (2) define sensors (pointer, keyboard), (3) wrap sortable items in SortableContext, (4) handle onDragEnd to update state. Accessibility: (1) keyboard support (Space to pick up, Arrow keys to move, Space to drop), (2) screen reader announcements, (3) focus management. For Docita: drag-and-drop could be used for reordering appointment slots or reorganizing clinic schedules. The key: make it accessible — not just mouse-based."

## Section 16: DevOps and Infrastructure (20 questions)

**Q281: "How do you design a Dockerfile for a Node.js application?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Multi-stage build: (1) build stage: install dependencies, build TypeScript, (2) production stage: copy only built files and production dependencies. Example: `FROM node:20-alpine AS builder`, `RUN npm ci`, `RUN npm run build`, `FROM node:20-alpine`, `COPY --from=builder /app/dist ./dist`, `COPY --from=builder /app/node_modules ./node_modules`. Optimization: (1) use .dockerignore, (2) layer dependencies before source code (cache-friendly), (3) use alpine for smaller images, (4) run as non-root user."

**Q282: "How do you implement blue-green deployments with Kubernetes?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Setup: (1) two Deployments: blue (current) and green (new), (2) Service points to the currentDeployment label, (3) deploy green, run smoke tests, (4) switch Service selector to green, (5) keep blue as rollback. Database: expand/contract migration (both blue and green use the same database, schema must be backward-compatible). Rollback: switch Service selector back to blue. For Docita: blue-green for the API, rolling update for the worker (less critical)."

**Q283: "How do you handle database migrations in a CI/CD pipeline?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Steps: (1) PR triggers: run `prisma migrate diff` to detect schema changes, (2) merge to main: run `prisma migrate deploy` on staging, (3) verify staging works, (4) run `prisma migrate deploy` on production during maintenance window (or use expand/contract for zero-downtime). Safety: (1) backup before migration, (2) dry-run on staging, (3) automatic restore if health check fails. For Docita: safe-migrate.sh wraps Prisma with backup, dry-run, deploy, and restore."

**Q284: "How do you implement canary deployments?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Steps: (1) deploy new version to a small subset of instances (e.g., 10%), (2) monitor error rate, latency, and business metrics, (3) if metrics are healthy, gradually increase traffic to 100%, (4) if metrics degrade, rollback to previous version. Implementation: (1) Kubernetes with Flagger or Argo Rollouts, (2) service mesh (Istio) for traffic splitting, (3) load balancer weighted routing. For Docita: canary deployments for the API (10% → 50% → 100%), rolling update for the worker."

**Q285: "How do you handle configuration management across environments?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Strategy: (1) environment-specific config files (config.development.ts, config.production.ts), (2) environment variables for secrets and environment-specific values, (3) .env.example for documentation, (4) config validation at startup (Zod schema). For Docita: config includes DATABASE_URL, JWT_SECRET, Sentry DSN, provider API keys. Secrets are in AWS Secrets Manager, non-secrets in environment variables. Config is validated at startup — if a required variable is missing, the app crashes immediately."

**Q286: "How do you implement infrastructure as code for a healthcare SaaS?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Tools: (1) Terraform for cloud infrastructure (AWS RDS, ECS, S3), (2) Kubernetes manifests for container orchestration, (3) Helm charts for parameterized deployments. For Docita: Terraform for AWS RDS (PostgreSQL), ECS (API + worker), S3 (document storage), CloudFront (CDN). Kubernetes manifests for pod definitions, services, ingress. Helm for environment-specific overrides (staging vs production). Version control: all IaC in the same repo as application code."

**Q287: "How do you handle SSL/TLS termination?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Options: (1) CDN termination (Cloudflare/CloudFront terminates TLS, sends HTTP to origin), (2) load balancer termination (ALB terminates TLS, forwards to pods), (3) ingress controller termination (nginx-ingress with TLS secrets). For Docita: Cloudflare terminates TLS for the frontend (SPA), ALB terminates TLS for the API. Internal traffic (API → worker) uses plain HTTP (within the same VPC). Certificate management: Let's Encrypt for custom domains, AWS ACM for ALB."

**Q288: "How do you implement load balancing for a NestJS application?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Options: (1) Kubernetes Service with type LoadBalancer, (2) AWS ALB with target groups, (3) nginx reverse proxy. Health checks: (1) Kubernetes liveness probe: GET /health, (2) readiness probe: GET /health/ready, (3) ALB health check: GET /health with interval 30s. Session affinity: not needed for stateless APIs (JWT-based auth). For Docita: Kubernetes Service with ALB ingress, health checks on /health endpoint, no session affinity (JWT is stateless)."

**Q289: "How do you handle DNS failover?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Implementation: (1) use Route 53 health checks on the primary endpoint, (2) configure failover routing: primary = primary region, secondary = DR region, (3) health check monitors /health endpoint, (4) if primary fails health check, Route 53 routes traffic to secondary. TTL: 60 seconds (fast failover, but more DNS queries). For Docita: DNS failover between primary (EU-West) and DR (EU-Central) regions. Health check interval: 30 seconds, failure threshold: 3 consecutive failures."

**Q290: "How do you implement automated backups?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"PostgreSQL: (1) automated daily snapshots via AWS RDS, (2) WAL archival to S3 every minute, (3) cross-region backup copy for DR. Application: (1) database backup before migrations (safe-migrate.sh), (2) document storage backup (S3 versioning + cross-region replication). Monitoring: (1) alert on backup failure, (2) alert on backup age > 24 hours, (3) quarterly restore tests. For Docita: RDS automated backups (35-day retention) + WAL archival to S3 (7-day retention) + quarterly DR drills."

**Q291: "How do you handle log rotation and retention?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Strategy: (1) structured JSON logs to stdout (12-factor), (2) container runtime captures stdout, (3) log aggregation (CloudWatch, Datadog, or ELK), (4) retention policy: 30 days for application logs, 90 days for audit logs, 1 year for security logs. Rotation: handled by the log aggregation system (not the application). For Docita: structured logs to stdout, captured by Docker, shipped to CloudWatch. PHI: never in logs — the Sentry scrubber handles this for error reports, and the logger excludes PHI from structured logs."

**Q292: "How do you implement monitoring dashboards?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Tools: Grafana + Prometheus (or Datadog). Layers: (1) executive: uptime, error rate, active clinics, (2) service: RED per endpoint, queue depth, worker lag, (3) infrastructure: CPU, memory, connections, WAL size, (4) business: bookings/hour, notification delivery rate. Alerts: integrated with PagerDuty/Slack. For Docita: Grafana dashboards for each layer, Prometheus for metrics collection, alerting via PagerDuty. PHI: no patient data on dashboards."

**Q293: "How do you handle certificate management?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Options: (1) Let's Encrypt with cert-manager (Kubernetes), (2) AWS ACM for ALB/CloudFront, (3) manual certificate management (not recommended). Automation: cert-manager watches Ingress resources, automatically provisions and renews Let's Encrypt certificates. For Docita: cert-manager for custom domains (app.docita.work), AWS ACM for ALB. Renewal: automatic 30 days before expiry. Monitoring: alert on certificate expiry < 7 days."

**Q294: "How do you implement network policies in Kubernetes?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Network policies control pod-to-pod communication. Default: deny all ingress/egress. Exceptions: (1) API pod → PostgreSQL (port 5432), (2) API pod → Redis (port 6379), (3) worker pod → PostgreSQL, (4) ingress controller → API pods. Implementation: Kubernetes NetworkPolicy resources. For Docita: network policies ensure the worker can't be accessed directly from the internet, and the API can only communicate with PostgreSQL and Redis."

**Q295: "How do you handle horizontal pod autoscaling?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"HPA: automatically scales the number of pods based on metrics. Metrics: (1) CPU utilization (target: 70%), (2) memory utilization (target: 80%), (3) custom metrics (requests per second). Configuration: min=2, max=10, targetCPU=70%. Scale-up: fast (30 seconds), scale-down: slow (5 minutes) to prevent thrashing. For Docita: HPA for the API (CPU-based), HPA for the worker (queue depth-based). The worker scales based on outbox table depth, not CPU."

**Q296: "How do you implement resource quotas?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Resource quotas: limit total resource consumption per namespace. Configuration: (1) CPU: requests=500m, limits=2000m per pod, (2) memory: requests=512Mi, limits=1Gi per pod, (3) namespace quota: total CPU=8 cores, total memory=16Gi. For Docita: resource quotas prevent one application from consuming all cluster resources. The API gets more CPU (request handling), the worker gets more memory (queue processing)."

**Q297: "How do you handle secrets in Kubernetes?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Options: (1) Kubernetes Secrets (base64-encoded, not encrypted by default), (2) AWS Secrets Manager with External Secrets Operator, (3) HashiCorp Vault with Vault Agent. For Docita: AWS Secrets Manager + External Secrets Operator. The operator syncs secrets from AWS SM to Kubernetes Secrets, which are mounted as environment variables or files. Benefits: central secret management, automatic rotation, audit logging."

**Q298: "How do you implement pod disruption budgets?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"PDB: ensures a minimum number of pods are available during voluntary disruptions (node drain, cluster upgrade). Configuration: `minAvailable: 1` (at least 1 pod must be running) or `maxUnavailable: 1` (at most 1 pod can be down). For Docita: PDB for the API (minAvailable: 1), PDB for the worker (minAvailable: 1). This ensures at least one instance of each is running during node maintenance."

**Q299: "How do you handle node affinity and taints?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Node affinity: pods are scheduled on nodes with specific labels (e.g., `workload=api`). Taints: nodes repel pods unless the pod has a matching toleration. Use case: (1) dedicate nodes to the API (taint: workload=api, toleration on API pods), (2) separate database nodes from application nodes. For Docita: at current scale, affinity rules are sufficient (API pods on app nodes, PostgreSQL on dedicated database nodes). Taints would be added at larger scale."

**Q300: "How do you implement service discovery?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Kubernetes: (1) Services provide stable DNS names (e.g., `api-service.default.svc.cluster.local`), (2) pods discover services via DNS, (3) headless services for stateful pods (e.g., PostgreSQL). External services: (1) ExternalName service for external databases, (2) endpoints for static IPs. For Docita: Kubernetes Services handle service discovery. The API discovers PostgreSQL via the `postgresql-service` DNS name. The worker discovers the API via the `api-service` DNS name."

## Section 17: Testing Advanced (15 questions)

**Q301: "How do you test a distributed transaction (outbox pattern)?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Integration test: (1) create a Prisma transaction that writes business data + outbox event, (2) assert both exist in the database, (3) simulate worker processing (read event, execute action), (4) assert event is marked as processed. Failure test: (1) create transaction, (2) simulate worker crash (don't mark as processed), (3) restart worker, (4) assert event is reprocessed. Idempotency test: (1) process the same event twice, (2) assert the action is only applied once. Use Testcontainers for PostgreSQL."

**Q302: "How do you test a circuit breaker?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Test: (1) mock the external service to return errors, (2) call the circuit breaker-wrapped function N times, (3) assert the circuit opens after N failures, (4) assert subsequent calls fail fast (no call to the external service), (5) wait for the recovery timeout, (6) make one call — assert it succeeds (half-open), (7) assert the circuit closes. Implementation: use Jest fake timers for timeout simulation, MSW or nock for HTTP mocking."

**Q303: "How do you test rate limiting?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Test: (1) send N requests within the rate limit window, (2) assert all succeed (200), (3) send one more request, (4) assert it's rejected (429), (5) check X-RateLimit-Remaining header, (6) wait for the window to reset, (7) send a request, (8) assert it succeeds. Implementation: use supertest for HTTP requests, Redis for the rate limit store (use a test Redis instance or mock). For per-tenant rate limiting: test with two different clinic IDs, assert each gets its own limit."

**Q304: "How do you test WebSocket connections?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Test: (1) connect a WebSocket client, (2) authenticate (send JWT), (3) join a clinic room, (4) from the server, emit an event to the clinic room, (5) assert the client receives the event, (6) disconnect, (7) assert cleanup (client removed from room). Implementation: use ws library for the client, Socket.IO client for Socket.IO servers. For Docita: test that (1) unauthenticated connections are rejected, (2) users can only join their clinic's room, (3) events are scoped to the clinic room."

**Q305: "How do you test file uploads?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Test: (1) generate a pre-signed URL, (2) upload a file to the pre-signed URL, (3) confirm the upload, (4) assert the database record is created, (5) download the file, (6) assert the file content matches. Validation test: (1) upload a file exceeding max size, (2) assert rejection, (3) upload a file with invalid type, (4) assert rejection. Implementation: use supertest for the API, AWS SDK mock for S3, or use LocalStack for local testing."

**Q306: "How do you test authentication flows?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Test: (1) login with valid credentials → 200 + JWT + refresh token cookie, (2) login with invalid credentials → 401, (3) access protected route with valid JWT → 200, (4) access protected route with expired JWT → 401, (5) refresh token → new JWT, (6) refresh with expired token → 401, (7) logout → token revoked, (8) access with revoked token → 401. Implementation: use supertest, store cookies for refresh token flow, assert on response headers and status codes."

**Q307: "How do you test ABAC policies?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Test: (1) create two clinics with users and resources, (2) Clinic A user accesses Clinic A resource → ALLOW, (3) Clinic A user accesses Clinic B resource → DENY, (4) CLINICIAN accesses assigned patient → ALLOW, (5) CLINICIAN accesses unassigned patient → DENY, (6) RECEPTIONIST accesses clinical notes → DENY, (7) INTERNAL_ADMIN accesses any clinic → ALLOW (with audit flag). Implementation: seed test data, call policy functions directly, assert ALLOW/DENY decisions."

**Q308: "How do you test database migrations?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Test: (1) run migration on a test database, (2) assert the schema changes are applied, (3) run the application against the migrated schema, (4) assert all endpoints work, (5) run the test suite against the migrated schema. Rollback test: (1) run migration, (2) run the rollback script, (3) assert the schema is restored. Data integrity test: (1) create test data, (2) run migration, (3) assert test data is preserved and correct. Implementation: use Testcontainers for isolated PostgreSQL instances."

**Q309: "How do you test a worker that processes background jobs?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Test: (1) create an outbox event, (2) start the worker, (3) assert the worker picks up the event, (4) assert the event is processed correctly, (5) assert the event is marked as processed. Failure test: (1) create an event, (2) mock the processor to throw an error, (3) assert the event is retried, (4) assert the event moves to DLQ after max retries. Idempotency test: (1) create an event, (2) process it twice, (3) assert the action is only applied once."

**Q310: "How do you test webhook verification?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Test: (1) valid signature → 200, (2) tampered signature → 403, (3) missing signature → 403, (4) duplicate event ID → 200 (acknowledged, not reprocessed), (5) expired timestamp → 403 (replay attack). Implementation: use nock to mock the webhook endpoint, compute HMAC signatures in the test, assert on status codes and database state. For Docita: the webhook test suite runs on every PR that touches the webhook handler."

**Q311: "How do you test cache invalidation?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Test: (1) cache a value, (2) verify the value is in the cache, (3) update the underlying data, (4) invalidate the cache, (5) verify the cache is empty, (6) read the value, (7) verify it's fetched from the database and re-cached. Race condition test: (1) two concurrent writes, (2) both invalidate the cache, (3) verify the final cached value is correct. Implementation: use Redis mock or Testcontainers for Redis, mock the database for deterministic tests."

**Q312: "How do you test concurrent requests?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Test: (1) use Promise.all to send multiple requests simultaneously, (2) assert exactly one succeeds and the rest fail (for exclusive resources), (3) assert no deadlocks or orphaned locks. Implementation: use supertest with Promise.all for HTTP-level concurrency, use Prisma $transaction with Promise.all for database-level concurrency. For Docita: the concurrent booking test seeds one slot, sends two booking requests simultaneously, and asserts one 201 and one 409."

**Q313: "How do you test error handling in a distributed system?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Test: (1) mock a downstream service to return 500, (2) assert the upstream service handles the error gracefully (returns a degraded response, not a 500), (3) mock a timeout, (4) assert the circuit breaker trips, (5) mock a slow response, (6) assert the timeout triggers. Implementation: use nock or MSW to mock HTTP services, use Jest fake timers for timeout simulation. For Docita: test that (1) notification provider failure doesn't block booking, (2) database timeout returns a 503, (3) Redis failure falls through to PostgreSQL."

**Q314: "How do you test performance under load?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Tools: k6, Artillery. Test: (1) define realistic scenarios (booking, patient lookup, schedule view), (2) set targets (100 concurrent users, 1000 req/s), (3) run for 10 minutes, (4) assert: p95 < 500ms, error rate < 1%, no memory leaks. Implementation: k6 scripts with realistic user flows, assert on built-in metrics. For Docita: load test the booking endpoint with 50 concurrent users, assert exactly one succeeds per slot, assert no deadlocks."

**Q315: "How do you test accessibility in a React application?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Tools: (1) axe-core + @axe-core/react for automated checks, (2) jest-axe for unit tests, (3) Lighthouse CI for page-level audits, (4) manual keyboard navigation testing, (5) screen reader testing (VoiceOver/NVDA). Tests: (1) render component, (2) run axe-core, (3) assert no violations. For Docita: accessibility tests run in CI, fail the build on violations. Focus areas: form labels, keyboard navigation, color contrast, ARIA attributes."

---

## Section 18: API and Integration (15 questions)

**Q316: "How do you design a REST API for a healthcare SaaS?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Principles: (1) resource-oriented (nouns, not verbs), (2) consistent URL structure (/clinics/:id/patients), (3) proper HTTP methods (GET=read, POST=create, PUT=update, DELETE=delete), (4) status codes (200=success, 201=created, 400=validation, 401=unauth, 403=forbidden, 404=not found, 409=conflict, 429=rate limited, 500=server error), (5) pagination (cursor-based), (6) filtering (query parameters), (7) sorting (sort parameter). For Docita: REST API with OpenAPI spec, NestJS controllers, Zod validation."

**Q317: "How do you implement API versioning?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Strategy: URL versioning for major changes (/v1/patients, /v2/patients), header versioning for minor variations. Implementation: (1) version in the URL path, (2) separate controller per version, (3) shared business logic, (4) deprecation headers on old versions. For Docita: URL versioning (/v1/), with a 6-month deprecation window for breaking changes. The clinician app and internal app both use v1."

**Q318: "How do you handle API authentication across services?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Strategy: (1) API gateway validates JWT, (2) attaches user context to request headers (X-User-Id, X-Clinic-Id, X-User-Role), (3) downstream services trust the gateway. Defense in depth: (1) services validate JWT as a secondary check, (2) internal services use mTLS or API keys. For Docita: the API gateway validates JWT and forwards user context. The worker validates the outbox event's integrity (not JWT, since it's an internal service)."

**Q319: "How do you implement API documentation with OpenAPI?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Implementation: (1) use @nestjs/swagger, (2) decorators on controllers: @ApiTags, @ApiOperation, @ApiResponse, @ApiBearerAuth, (3) DTOs with @ApiProperty, (4) auto-generate OpenAPI JSON at /api/docs. For Docita: OpenAPI spec auto-generated from NestJS decorators, hosted at /api/docs (Swagger UI). The spec includes all endpoints, request/response schemas, authentication requirements, and error responses."

**Q320: "How do you implement API rate limiting?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Implementation: (1) Redis-based token bucket per clinic, (2) NestJS custom guard that checks rate limit before processing, (3) headers: X-RateLimit-Limit, X-RateLimit-Remaining, X-RateLimit-Reset, Retry-After. Per-tenant: key = `ratelimit:{clinicId}:{window}`. Per-user for sensitive operations: key = `ratelimit:{userId}:login:{window}`. For Docita: rate limit by clinic_id (100 req/min default), with higher limits for premium clinics."

**Q321: "How do you implement API gateway routing?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Options: (1) Kubernetes Ingress (nginx-ingress), (2) AWS ALB, (3) Kong/Express Gateway. For Docita: Kubernetes Ingress with nginx-ingress. Routes: /api/* → API service, /ws/* → WebSocket gateway, /* → SPA static files. Middleware: JWT validation (global), rate limiting (global), CORS (global), request logging (global). For Docita: nginx-ingress handles routing, SSL termination, and global middleware."

**Q322: "How do you design API error responses?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Standard shape: `{ error: { code: 'VALIDATION_ERROR', message: 'Human-readable', details: [{ field: 'email', message: 'Invalid format' }], requestId: 'abc-123' } }`. Rules: (1) consistent across all endpoints, (2) machine-readable code, (3) human-readable message, (4) request ID for debugging, (5) never expose internal errors. Implementation: NestJS exception filter that formats all exceptions consistently."

**Q323: "How do you implement API pagination?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Cursor-based: (1) first request: GET /patients?limit=50, (2) response includes `nextCursor` (last item's ID), (3) subsequent requests: GET /patients?limit=50&cursor={nextCursor}, (4) when nextCursor is null, there are no more pages. Benefits: stable (no skipped/duplicate items on concurrent writes), efficient (no OFFSET scan). For Docita: cursor-based pagination for patient lists, appointment lists, and audit logs."

**Q324: "How do you handle API filtering and sorting?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Filtering: query parameters with operators (GET /patients?status=active&clinicId=abc). Sorting: query parameter (GET /patients?sort=created_at&order=desc). Implementation: (1) validate filter parameters against allowed fields, (2) build Prisma WHERE clause dynamically, (3) build Prisma orderBy dynamically. For Docita: filtering by clinic_id (always), status, date range, practitioner. Sorting by created_at, name, appointment_date. Allowed fields defined in a whitelist to prevent injection."

**Q325: "How do you implement API caching?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Strategy: (1) HTTP cache headers for static resources (Cache-Control, ETag), (2) application-level caching for dynamic data (Redis), (3) CDN for static assets. For Docita: Cache-Control headers for the SPA (immutable, max-age=1 year for fingerprinted assets), Redis caching for practitioner schedules (30s TTL), no caching for patient data (PHI sensitivity). Implementation: NestJS interceptor that adds Cache-Control headers, Redis cache service for application-level caching."

**Q326: "How do you handle webhook delivery?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Outbound webhooks: (1) create webhook subscription (URL, events, secret), (2) on event, sign the payload with HMAC-SHA256, (3) POST to subscriber URL with signature header, (4) retry on failure (exponential backoff, max 3 attempts), (5) DLQ after max retries. Verification: subscriber verifies HMAC signature. For Docita: outbound webhooks notify external systems of appointment changes. The webhook payload is signed with a shared secret, and the subscriber verifies the signature."

**Q327: "How do you implement API idempotency?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Implementation: (1) client sends Idempotency-Key header (UUID), (2) server stores key + response for 24 hours, (3) duplicate request returns stored response, (4) key is scoped to the endpoint and user. For Docita: idempotency for booking (Idempotency-Key header), payment (idempotency key per transaction), and webhook processing (event ID as idempotency key). Implementation: Redis with TTL for key storage."

**Q328: "How do you handle API request validation?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Implementation: (1) Zod schemas for DTOs, (2) NestJS ValidationPipe that validates request body against the Zod schema, (3) returns 400 with detailed error messages for invalid requests. For Docita: Zod schemas shared between frontend and backend (same validation rules). The ValidationPipe runs globally, so every endpoint is validated. Custom validators for healthcare-specific rules (e.g., appointment time must be during clinic hours)."

**Q329: "How do you implement API response compression?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Implementation: (1) use compression middleware (e.g., NestJS compression package), (2) compress responses > 1KB, (3) skip compression for already-compressed content (images, gzip). For Docita: gzip compression for API responses (reduces payload size by 60-80%). Configuration: level=6 (balance of speed and compression), threshold=1024 (only compress responses > 1KB). Monitoring: track compression ratio and CPU overhead."

**Q330: "How do you handle API timeout and retry?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Timeout: (1) set a timeout on every external call (e.g., 5 seconds for provider APIs), (2) use AbortController for fetch, (3) return 504 Gateway Timeout if the call exceeds the timeout. Retry: (1) retry on transient failures (network errors, 502, 503, 504), (2) exponential backoff (1s, 2s, 4s), (3) max 3 retries, (4) idempotency key to prevent duplicate effects. For Docita: timeout on provider calls (SendGrid, Twilio), retry with backoff, idempotency key for booking and payment."

---

## Section 19: Data Modeling Advanced (10 questions)

**Q331: "How do you design a multi-tenant schema in PostgreSQL?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Shared schema: every table has a clinic_id column. All queries include `WHERE clinic_id = $1`. Indexes: composite index on (clinic_id, ...) for every frequently queried table. RLS: optional layer that enforces clinic_id filtering at the database level. For Docita: shared-schema with clinic_id on every table, composite indexes on (clinic_id, ...) for all query patterns, RLS enabled for defense in depth."

**Q332: "How do you handle soft deletes in a multi-tenant system?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Implementation: (1) add deleted_at timestamp column (NULL = active, non-NULL = deleted), (2) all queries include `WHERE deleted_at IS NULL`, (3) restore: set deleted_at = NULL, (4) hard delete: permanent removal after retention period. For Docita: soft delete for patients (can be restored), hard delete for media files (GDPR). Tenant scope: soft deletes are clinic-scoped — Clinic A can't see Clinic B's deleted records."

**Q333: "How do you design audit logging for a regulated domain?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Schema: (1) append-only table (no UPDATE/DELETE permissions), (2) columns: id, actor_id, actor_role, clinic_id, action, resource_type, resource_id, timestamp, ip_address, request_id, details (JSONB), (3) indexes: (clinic_id, timestamp), (actor_id, timestamp), (resource_type, resource_id). Retention: 7 years for healthcare. For Docita: patient-PHI access is always audited, audit logs are separate from application logs, and the audit table is append-only."

**Q334: "How do you handle temporal data (appointments, scheduling)?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Schema: (1) slot_start and slot_end as TIMESTAMPTZ, (2) timezone stored per clinic (clinictimezone column), (3) all times stored in UTC, converted to local timezone on display. Overlap query: `NOT (new.end <= existing.start OR new.start >= existing.end)`. Index: composite on (clinic_id, practitioner_id, slot_start) for calendar views. For Docita: all appointment times are UTC, converted to clinic timezone on the frontend."

**Q335: "How do you design a flexible schema for custom fields?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Options: (1) JSONB column for custom fields (flexible, queryable with GIN index), (2) EAV (Entity-Attribute-Value) table (more structured, harder to query), (3) separate table per custom field type (most structured, most complex). For Docita: JSONB for clinic-specific custom fields (e.g., custom appointment types, custom labels). The JSONB column is indexed with GIN for fast querying, and a Zod schema validates the JSON structure."

**Q336: "How do you handle data versioning?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Options: (1) version column on each row (optimistic locking), (2) separate versions table (full history), (3) event sourcing (every change is an event). For Docita: version column for optimistic locking on concurrent edits (e.g., two clinicians editing the same patient record). The version is checked on UPDATE: `UPDATE patients SET ... WHERE id = ? AND version = ?`. If 0 rows affected, the record was modified by someone else — return 409 Conflict."

**Q337: "How do you design a notification preferences system?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Schema: (1) notification_preferences table: user_id, clinic_id, channel (in-app/email/SMS/push), event_type (appointment_booked/prescription_ready/...), enabled (boolean), (2) default preferences per clinic (clinic admin sets defaults), (3) user overrides (individual users can opt out of specific channels). Query: `SELECT * FROM notification_preferences WHERE user_id = ? AND clinic_id = ?`. For Docita: in-app notifications are always on (clinical safety), email/SMS are opt-out per user."

**Q338: "How do you handle file metadata in a document management system?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Schema: (1) documents table: id, clinic_id, patient_id, uploaded_by, filename, storage_path, mime_type, size_bytes, created_at, (2) storage: S3/R2 with path = `{clinic_id}/{patient_id}/{uuid}.{ext}`, (3) access: pre-signed URLs for download, (4) versioning: S3 versioning for document history. For Docita: medical documents are stored in S3 with clinic-scoped paths, metadata in PostgreSQL, access via pre-signed URLs with time-limited tokens."

**Q339: "How do you design a billing schema for a healthcare SaaS?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Schema: (1) invoices: id, clinic_id, patient_id, status (draft/sent/paid/overdue), total, currency, created_at, due_date, (2) line_items: id, invoice_id, description, quantity, unit_price, total, (3) payments: id, invoice_id, amount, method, status, provider_reference, created_at, (4) reconciliations: id, payment_id, matched (boolean), discrepancy_notes. For Docita: invoices are generated from completed appointments, payments are processed via Stripe/Adyen, reconciliation is a daily batch job."

**Q340: "How do you handle internationalization in a database schema?"**

**Answer skeleton:**
"Options: (1) translation table: translations(id, entity_type, entity_id, locale, field, value), (2) JSONB column: translations JSONB with locale keys, (3) separate tables per locale (not recommended). For Docita: JSONB for notification templates (locale-keyed), translation table for patient-facing content (emails, letters). The API returns content in the user's preferred locale, with fallback to English."

---

## Section 20: Behavioral Advanced (10 questions)

**Q341: "Tell me about a time you had to make a decision that affected the entire codebase."**

**Answer skeleton:** "At Docita, I chose shared-schema tenancy over database-per-tenant. This decision affected every query, every policy, every test. I: (1) evaluated the trade-offs (operational simplicity vs blast radius), (2) documented the decision in an ADR, (3) implemented defense-in-depth (ABAC + RLS + cross-clinic tests), (4) set a trigger for reconsideration (500+ clinics or regulatory requirement). Reflection: architectural decisions should be reversible or have clear triggers for change."

**Q342: "Describe a time you had to balance technical excellence with business deadlines."**

**Answer skeleton:** "At Docita, a clinic needed a feature urgently but the code path lacked proper ABAC policies. I: (1) shipped the feature with a temporary role-based check (acceptable risk), (2) created a ticket for the proper ABAC policy (deadline: next sprint), (3) implemented the ABAC policy in the next sprint, (4) added a cross-clinic test to prevent regression. Reflection: ship the minimum viable security, then harden immediately — never skip security entirely."

**Q343: "Tell me about a time you had to convince the team to adopt a new technology."**

**Answer skeleton:** "At MAQ, I convinced the team to adopt TypeScript by: (1) writing a proof-of-concept migration for one module (2 days), (2) showing the type-safety benefits in code review (caught 3 bugs), (3) documenting the migration path (step-by-step guide), (4) pairing with two engineers to migrate their modules. The team adopted TypeScript over 3 months. Reflection: show, don't tell. A working prototype is more persuasive than a document."

**Q344: "How do you handle a situation where you're the only engineer on a critical feature?"**

**Answer skeleton:** "At Docita, I'm the sole engineer — every feature is mine alone. But a specific example: the booking concurrency feature was critical (clinics were double-booking patients). I: (1) wrote the feature, (2) wrote tests first (unit, integration, E2E), (3) deployed to staging, (4) had a clinic test it with real data, (5) deployed to production with monitoring. Reflection: when you're the only engineer, tests are your code review — they catch the mistakes a second pair of eyes would catch."

**Q345: "Describe a time you had to recover from a data loss incident."**

**Answer skeleton:** "At MAQ, a migration deleted a column that was still referenced by a running service. Users saw 500 errors. I: (1) rolled back the migration (5 minutes), (2) restored the column from the backup (10 minutes), (3) verified data integrity (5 minutes), (4) deployed the corrected migration (5 minutes). Total downtime: 25 minutes. Postmortem: (1) root cause: migration wasn't backward-compatible, (2) fix: expand/contract pattern for all migrations, (3) prevention: migration tests in CI."

**Q346: "Tell me about a time you had to work with a legacy system."**

**Answer skeleton:** "At Cognizant, I worked with a Spring Boot monolith that had no tests and inconsistent error handling. I: (1) added tests for the most critical paths (50 tests over 2 weeks), (2) standardized error handling (typed errors with codes), (3) extracted one module into a separate service (strangler fig), (4) documented the architecture for the team. Reflection: legacy systems need incremental improvement, not rewrite — the strangler fig pattern is your friend."

**Q347: "How do you handle a situation where the product requirements keep changing?"**

**Answer skeleton:** "At Docita, the notification system requirements changed 3 times in 2 weeks (add SMS, then remove SMS, then add push notifications). I: (1) built the system with a provider abstraction (same interface for email, SMS, push), (2) each channel was a separate module, (3) adding/removing a channel was a configuration change, not a rewrite. Reflection: design for change — abstractions that make change cheap are worth the upfront investment."

**Q348: "Describe a time you had to mentor a junior engineer through a difficult problem."**

**Answer skeleton:** "At MAQ, a mid-level engineer was struggling with a complex database query (multiple JOINs, subqueries, window functions). I: (1) paired with them for 2 hours, (2) walked through the query step by step, (3) showed them how to use EXPLAIN ANALYZE to understand the query plan, (4) let them rewrite the query while I guided. The result: they rewrote the query, it was 3x faster, and they learned how to analyze query plans. Reflection: mentoring is slower than doing it yourself, but the engineer learned a skill they'll use for years."

**Q349: "Tell me about a time you had to make a trade-off between security and usability."**

**Answer skeleton:** "At Docita, the ABAC policy was too strict — clinicians couldn't view patients who were reassigned to another clinician (even for consultation). I: (1) identified the usability issue (clinicians needed read access for consultations), (2) proposed a policy change: CLINICIAN role gets read access to all patients in their clinic, but write access only to assigned patients, (3) implemented the change, (4) added an audit flag for cross-clinician reads. Reflection: security should be strict but not blocking — find the policy that's secure AND usable."

**Q350: "Tell me about a time you had to recover from a failed deployment."**

**Answer skeleton:** "At Docita, a deployment introduced a bug that caused the booking endpoint to return 500 for one clinic. I: (1) detected the issue via Sentry alert (2 minutes), (2) identified the root cause (missing null check in the new code), (3) rolled back the deployment via GitHub Actions (3 minutes), (4) verified the rollback fixed the issue (2 minutes). Total impact: 7 minutes of downtime for one clinic. Postmortem: (1) root cause: missing null check, (2) fix: added null check + test, (3) prevention: integration test for the booking endpoint."

## Pre-Interview Checklist

1. ☐ Can answer every Section 10 question (Docita-specific) cold
2. ☐ Can answer every Section 1 question (PostgreSQL internals) with code examples
3. ☐ Can answer every Section 11 question (behavioral) with STAR+R stories
4. ☐ Can design every Section 12 case (system design) in 45 minutes
5. ☐ Can explain every Section 2 question (distributed systems) with Docita examples
6. ☐ Can answer Section 7 (security) questions with specific code paths
7. ☐ Can answer Section 6 (observability) questions with Docita-specific dashboards
8. ☐ Can answer Section 13-20 questions with code examples
9. ☐ Have practiced all answers out loud at least once
10. ☐ Have reviewed the "what NOT to claim" for every answer
11. ☐ Have the evidence map open (80.18) and know which files to show for each answer
